# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

Kuafi Fund will contribute to the transition to low-carbon and resilient economies in selected countries in Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) countries by fostering the systemic adoption of best practices by financial institutions, including banks, microfinance institutions, credit unions, cooperatives, other non-bank financial Institutions and FinTech companies denominated as all Financial Service Providers or FSPs). Kuafi Fund will transform FSPs in their transition to become greener and more inclusive. The Fund will support the development of innovative solutions for small and medium-sized Enterprises (SMEs) and to smallerholder farmers (or smallholders). Kuafi will also support companies with innovative business models that can provide financing, technology, and services to help implement climate mitigation and adaptation solutions that could eventually be financed by FSP.

Kuafi Fund will invest at least 70% of its capital in 14 countries in Latin America and the Caribbean, as the region is most vulnerable to climate change. Some 14 million Latin American citizens live in areas considered to be at high risk from climate change. Up to a maximum of 30% will be invested in India and Africa to bridge the finance gap between India and Latin America on how to use technology to be more efficient and amplify impact.

Kuafi Fund will therefore be invested in 15 countries. However, as some of these countries have not issued the required legislation for the Fund, Kuafi Fund will consist of two Funded Activities: (i) Kuafi Fund-Acted or Kuafi Fund-CDF) which will only invest in the following seven countries: Mexico, Brazil, Colombia, El Salvador, Panama, Honduras and Guatemala; and (ii) the Kuafi Fund-nongCDF), which will invest in 15 countries. The two Funded Activities will be managed by a single team of professionals with expertise designed for the universe of seven countries, and even though Kuafi Fund will cover a wider area, the investment proposals to the Kuafi Fund (for Kuafi Fund-Acted) will differeniated in Part I from the Kuafi Fund as a whole.

Kuafi Fund-CDF is expected to directly support over 237,900 households and 4,428 SMEs that will collectively add 2,705,465 tonnes of CO2 equivalent in 10 years across Latin America. According to the World Bank, the climate finance gap for LAC countries is US$ 23 billion by 2030. This gap implies an increase in climate finance from USD 22 trillion to USD 44.5 trillion over this period. If this target is met, climate finance from financial institutions will account for 30% of total lending from financial institutions by 2030. The Funded Activity will support the development and deployment of innovative solutions to finance large renewable energy projects, large industrial power plants and large efficient building construction.

However, the smaller financial Institutions targeted by Kuafi Fund (FSPs) are key to mobilising climate finance in markets underserved by FSPs (mainly referred to large banks). These small institutions are essential for providing access to credit for small and medium-sized enterprises in low-income economies and that larger banks typically do not serve because of their perceived risk. (ii) the smallerholder farmers segment, which is one of the most vulnerable populations to climate change and will need to adapt rapidly to mitigate its effects. The Funded Activity will support the development and deployment of innovative solutions for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), and (iii) the SME segment, which accounts for 60%-70% of the industrial pollution and a significant share of global carbon emissions.

Kuafi Fund will also work with the innovative companies - to increase the number of available adaptation and mitigation solutions (mitigation solutions for SMEs and adaptation solutions for farmers) - and Financial Service Providers (FSPs) that can provide financing, technology, and services to help implement climate mitigation and adaptation solutions. The Funded Activity's investments will be maximised with the support of a Technical Assistance Facility, which will advise on how to integrate climate change considerations into financial institutions' business models, opportunities into their systems, and launching and expanding green and climate-friendly products and services.

Regarding the investments to be financed by Kuafi Fund-CDF, from an SME perspective, the Funded Activity will finance assets or activities such as solar roof panels, high efficiency heat pumps, insulation, low emission transport, energy efficient machinery, methane digesters, among others, and the smallholder farmers may

### Kuafi Fund's Structure

The Kuafi Fund structure includes the Kuafi Sicar-ESEF® (the SICAR1) and a Special Purpose Vehicle incorporated as a Securitisation Service Vehicle (SV) and is divided into two funding divisions: Kuafi Fund-GCF and Kuafi Fund non-GCF. The total size of the fund will be EUR 205 million, with an initial investment from GCF of EUR 119.5 million in total commitments to be used for investments and EUR 5.91 million for Technical Assistance (TA) EU funding. This request is made for Communication, Audit and Evaluation purposes and an additional EUR 2.4 million from the GCF for Monitoring and Evaluations Data Collection.

### Kuafi Fund-GCF

- **Liquidity**: Participation in equity, insurance or any type of financial product demonstrated green because it will finance the acquisition of renewable energy assets, the purchase of electric vehicles, the installation of solar panels to reduce CO2 tons, the acquisition of new equipment that reduces the carbon footprint, low emission transport, appliances, power generation and distribution systems, water treatment plants, waste management facilities, flood risk mitigation, flood risk systems, green agriculture solutions, etc.
- **Compliance**: The investments will be aligned with validated mitigation and adaptation, namely 'Agri-tech' and "Climate-tech".
- **Contribution**: The total life of the Fund will be 10 years, with the possibility of extending it for a further 2 (years).

### Kuafi Fund non-GCF

**Note:** The GCF contribution to the fund is included in section B.4 of the Funding Proposal. Kuafi Fund non-GCF will include contributions from the EU and private resources to finance investments in countries, including the 8 countries that are part of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) pilot project. In addition, it includes a GCF contribution where the NCD has not been issued (e.g., for GCF contributions, up to EUR 205 million). The GCF will be involved in these transactions.

Kuafi Fund-GCF is expected to catalyze private and institutional investments at scale through blended finance instruments (EU and GCF contributions) in the first loss tranches of Kuafi GCF (EUR 125 million and GCF: EUR 25 million) as key to mobilizing private investor participation by reducing their exposure to the perceived risk of the SME and smallholder farmer sector. The GCF, EU, FONPROSE and Spanish Government will provide a total of EUR 300 million in blended finance instruments (EUR 175 million from GCF: EUR 0.57 million from EUR; EUR 0.38 million from FONPROSE and EUR 0.04 from Kuafi).

As Kuafi Fund-GCF will invest in 7 countries, 100% of the GCF resources will be allocated to NOL countries, and 95% of the EU resources will be allocated to NOL countries. The remaining 5% of EU resources (EUR 6 million) contributions in TA resources will be allocated to NOL countries, leaving 85% of its resources to cover the non-NOL countries.

The Securitization Committee will issue notes that will be subscribed by the Spanish Agency for International Cooperation and Development (AICD) through the FONPROSE Sovereign Fund managed by the Spanish Government. The notes will have a maturity of 10 years, with an interest rate linked to the LIBOR. Kuafi Fund-GCF, with a maturity corresponding to the duration of the Funded Activity (10-12 years). These notes will enhance the return on private investments.

The resources are expected to come from the private sector (EUR 72.8 million) and a potential contribution from GAVIA Capital (EUR 1 million).

The Subsidiary Agreements that CORDIES + AE, will prepare for the implementation of this proposal are: i) for the investment component, the Investment Memorandum (PM), the Commitment Agreement, and the Side Letter; ii) for the TA component, the Fiduciary Agreement.

Kuafi Fund-GCF will use part of these resources to transform both. Innovative companies and FSPs into innovative companies and FSPs in order to reduce their exposure to the financial sector to reduce CO2 emissions and adapt to the impacts of climate change. The Funded Activity will select the instrument that (i) best meets the financing needs of the investor, (ii) where possible, provides the target risk-adjusted return and (iii) for which there is a market demand. The following types of investments are expected to be used:

A. Debt-like instruments: Approximately 60-75% of the portfolio, mainly invested in FSPs (including French companies), but could also be invested in innovative companies.

B. Equity: Approximately 25-40% of the portfolio for investment in innovative companies.

C. Mezzanine: Approximately 10% of the portfolio for investment in innovative companies and loans.

D. Growth-capital equity investments with a significant minority stake (less than 20%), a board representation, and a clear and/or pre-negotiated exit.

The investment horizon is expected to be 5-7 years, and most investments are expected to be made in the first 3 years, implying 3 to 6 investments per year.

Ultimately, Kuafi Fund-GCF must have the capacity not only to invest in innovative companies and FSPs, but also to provide a financial service that is more attractive than current market practices, while helping to reduce the current financing gap for SMEs and smallholders and the perceived risk of providing financial services to these final beneficiaries. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 7

## Green strategies follow a progression that will be measured with the Green Index 3.0®. This Index analyses the institutions according to their level of green strategy and their capacity to manage environmental risk and transform their model into a greener one. In this context, Kuali will invest approximately $15 million in three phases.

### *Green Investments* are those institutions that belong to Phases 1-3 according to the Green Index 3.0®:

#### Phase 1: FSP has a green strategy and systems in place to implement it, including roles and responsibilities, policies, procedures, and processes.
- **Phase 2:** FSP identifies the vulnerability of its clients/portfolio and the institution itself, the negative environmental impacts generated on ecosystems caused by clients and the institution, and clients' needs and demands. It also identifies opportunities for the institution itself.

#### Phase 3: FSP has a green strategy and tools in place to analyse and respond to the identified vulnerabilities, negative environmental impacts, and client needs and demands.
- The institutions translate the vulnerabilities, negative environmental impacts, demands into actual risks and opportunities.

These investments are financed at market rates.

### *Super Green Investments* are investments in institutions that belong to Phase 4 according to the Green Index 3.0®. These are innovative companies with innovative products and services and companies with innovative climate solutions.
- In the case of innovative companies, which are all Super Green Investments, Kuali will use its investment and technical assistance to expand their operations, establish alliances with FSPs for co-financing, and provide them with a financial buffer to help them increase their investor readiness to attract new investors in subsequent fundraising rounds. These funds will aim to increase the FSP green and climate portfolio of FSPs at below market rates.

## Kuali Fund-GCF will also be a key instrument in the context of the COVID-19 pandemic and its subsequent economic crisis.
The Funded Activity will contribute to securing financial support for innovative companies and FSPs, especially the latter operating on SMEs or smallholder farmers segments, which are particularly affected by the pandemic. The Funded Activity will provide the necessary financing, which is essential to alleviate the current post-COVID liquidity crisis. This will in turn help FSPs to secure renewals of current funding or access to new funding.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

B.1. Contextual Information (500 words, approximately two pages)

Main impacts of climate change

The countries of Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) - especially in Central America and the Caribbean - are already severely affected by climate variability, higher temperatures, deforestation, sea-level rise, ocean acidification, and extreme weather events. These changes have a significant impact on the livelihoods of millions of poor and indigenous people.

Coastal areas, where most people of the Caribbean region live, are experiencing an accelerated increase in coastal flooding due to sea level rise and surface water temperatures. More than 80 million people in LAC have inadequate access to clean water and basic sanitation, and some 14 million people in the region live in areas at high risk from natural disasters.

LAC countries can reduce their CO2 emissions by better protecting forest against climate risks and thrive in the long term. LAC accounts for less than 10% of total global greenhouse gas emissions but is particularly vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change, which are projected to increase significantly as a result of higher risk and low adaptive capacity. Projections show an increase in mean temperatures of up to 4.5°C by the end of this century compared to pre-industrial levels across LAC. Associated physical impacts include changes in precipitation regimes, strong increases in heat extremes, higher risks of drought and increased aridity.

In addition, the average intensity of tropical cyclones and the frequency of the most intense storms are projected to increase significantly over the next 30 years as a result of climate change. The region's global warming not only affects the right to health, food, water, culture, development and adequate housing, but also threatens people’s very survival and their right to life and physical integrity, particularly in vulnerable communities.

According to the United Nations*, temperatures will rise by at least 1.5°C by 2030 and 100 million people will be displaced by extreme poverty by 2050. Climate change is projected to increase energy demand for 63% of carbon emissions from power generation, increased access to energy and rapidly growing energy demand in countries therefore there has a key role to play in enabling the necessary transition to a low-carbon economy needed. The region's climate change vulnerability is also linked with its economic growth and innovation in emerging economies, but they also drive energy demand and CO2 emissions growth.

Climate change affects every country on every continent, but it does not do it equally. People already living in poverty are the most vulnerable to climate change because they have fewer resources to adapt (and often their livelihoods, making them the most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change), while having the least capacity to cope with them. It is estimated that climate change will force 25 million people to migrate and will cause 100 million people to be displaced by 2050. Small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) are particularly vulnerable to risks associated with climate change, as they have limited resources to adapt and cope with the impacts of climate change. More frequent and intense storms, floods, droughts, and changes in rainfall patterns are already affecting these communities, making it harder for them to secure a decent livelihood.

*Source: UN, December 2019.
**Source: European Commission, GHG Emissions, European Commission “SMEs and the environment in the EU”.
***SMEs contribute 64% of industrial pollution according to a study by the European Commission.

BRAZIL:

- The impacts of climate change in Brazil include: loss of biodiversity in the Amazon, very wetter higher temperatures and precipitation, increased frequency of temperature-sensitive species; increased severity of droughts can severely affect the Amazon's freshwater ecosystems and people who depend on them; changes in precipitation and temperature can affect the spread of diseases, and sea level rise and storm surges will increase coastal erosion. These impacts are exacerbated by deforestation, land use change, and environmental degradation.
- These impacts pose a major threat to the Amazon’s natural resources and biodiversity, as well as to the livelihoods of millions of people who depend on them for food security, water supply, and income generation.

In response to these challenges, Brazil has implemented numerous policies to reduce deforestation rates, which fell to one-sixth of 2004 levels by 2014. However, the Amazon remains under threat of deforestation due to illegal logging, land conversion for agriculture, and cattle ranching.
- In 2008, Brazil adopted the National Climate Change Plan, which aims to address (1) reduction of greenhouse gas emissions; (2) adaptation to climate change impacts; (3) sustainable development; and (4) capacity building and information dissemination. In 2016, Brazil prepared its third National Communication under the U.N. Framework Convention on Climate Change, noting the progress the country has made and reaffirming its commitment to the Paris Agreement.
- Brazil has set a relative target of a 37% reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by 2025 compared to 2005 levels; the country estimates that it would achieve a 43% reduction in emissions by 2030 compared to 2005. However, this note states that this is only a benchmark and should not be considered as part of its official contribution.

COLOMBIA:

- Most Colombians live in the high Andes, where water scarcity and land degradation exist, and on the coastal areas, where sea level rise and flooding may affect human settlements and economic activities. Rising temperatures and changes in rainfall patterns are likely to lead to water scarcity in Colombia. This could have significant impacts on agriculture, which is a major sector of the economy, as well as the supply of water.
- Sea-level rise is likely to have localized effects, with significant impacts in tourism-dependent coastal areas. Increased flooding may lead to the loss of key ecosystems such as coral reefs and fisheries, affecting important livelihoods.

The Government of Colombia's 2010–2014 National Development Plan identified climate adaptation as a priority for the country’s National Climate Change System (NCCS). Since 2014, a National Climate Change Policy has focused on mitigation and adaptation actions to reduce greenhouse gas emissions.
- Colombia has a relative reduction target of 20% of projected emissions in 2030 under a BAU scenario. An additional 10% reduction would be conditional on access to international financing.

### Mexico

Mexico’s location between two oceans and its complex topography increases the country’s exposure to extreme hydrometeorological events such as tropical cyclones, frosts, heat waves and floods. This geography also creates some of the world's most fertile soils, which can be vulnerable to population pressure (above the national poverty line) and urbanization. Aging transport, energy infrastructure is vulnerable to damage from floods and strong winds, especially in coastal areas. Coastal tourism, an important economic activity, is also at risk. In addition, Mexico’s agricultural sector is highly vulnerable in some areas; extreme temperatures and erratic rainfall are drastically affecting agricultural productivity, including the production of livestock. Since 1990, agriculture has accounted for 86 per cent of the country's weather-related financial losses.

Mexico’s 2012 General Climate Change Law (GLCC) defines planning and policy instruments, including institutional arrangements, to address climate change in Mexico. The GLCC is a key component of national climate policy. Under the GLCC, the Mexican government, is mandated to formulate and guide national climate change policies.

Mexico has a relative reduction target of 22% of projected emissions by 2030 in a BAU scenario. An additional 14% reduction would be conditional on access to international finance. In addition, the country has set itself an absolute carbon dioxide reduction target of 50%, which could rise to 70% by 2050 conditional on external financing.

### Panama

Widespread poverty and inequality have a negative impact on the environment. Panama is considered to be one of the most biodiverse countries in Central America, with more than 140 species of mammals protected. Nevertheless, poverty pressures have led many to exploit the natural resources of the country. The country’s forests are under threat from illegal logging, and there has been a slight increase over the last decade.

Soil erosion is one of Panama's most important environmental problems. Soil erosion occurs at a rate of 2,000 tons per year. Between 1990 and 1995, the average annual rate of deforestation was 25%. Air pollution is also a problem in urban centres due to emissions from industry and transport. Only 3% of Panama’s land area is covered by forests, which are under threat from logging and deforestation from the oil industry cause much of the pollution. The country's fish stocks are threatened by water pollution.

Panama has sectoral mitigation goals. In the case of energy, 30% of the installed capacity of the electricity matrix should come from other types of renewable energy sources by 2050; and in the case of the U.S.-funded Panama Canal expansion project, 10% of the total carbon emissions will be offset within areas of the national territory. The annual contribution increasing carbon sequestration capacity by 10% compared to the reference scenario for 2050.

### El Salvador

El Salvador, along with Honduras and Guatemala, is one of the world's most vulnerable countries to climate change. The reason for this is that they are all in the Dry Corridor, a region that causes droughts and floods, which can lead to food shortages and water scarcity (June and September) that have been disappearing for decades ago due to climate change. The latest projections show that, between 2021 and 2050, annual rainfall will be reduced by between 10% and 20% and the number of days with no rain will increase from an average of 34 per year in 2019 to around 68 per year. This is expected to have a significant impact on smallholder farmers and the local population dependent on rural areas.

El Salvador has set itself a target for reducing its total emissions of CO2 per year. The NDC document does not indicate what percentage of its total emissions this represents; El Salvador has set a renewable energy production target or around 60% of its electricity generation by 2030, an increase of 50% compared to the installed capacity in 2019.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

## GUATEMALA

- Guatemala is a multietnic, pluricultural and highly biologically diverse country, but also one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change in the world. Like Ecuador and many other countries in the Central American Dry Corridor, it is extremely arid.
- Under these circumstances, Guatemala has published its Nationally Determined Contributions. Guatemala intends to commit to reducing GHG emissions by 22.6% below levels in 2015 as levels in 2030 under a BAU scenario. The country will also reduce the vulnerability of its population and ecosystems.
- With external support, the commitment is reduced to 11.2%. The main axes on which Guatemala focuses through this NDC are: (i) reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation; (ii) increasing energy efficiency in buildings and appliances; and (iii) promoting sustainable land use practices.
- Guatemala has also prepared the National Action Plan of Climate Change. It is presented as a summary of the priority actions and precautions that citizens and institutions should take into account to face the risks associated with climate change, which are mainly related to extreme weather events and their negative opportunities for sustainable development to protect vulnerable people. It is in line with the objectives captured by the National Action Plan against Climate Change Vulnerability, la Asociación Obrera ante los Efectos del Cambio Climático y la Mitigación de Efetos Inversorables (LUCO) a national law primarily aimed at combating climate change.

### Honduras

- Located at the heart of the Central American isthmus, Honduras, like its neighbours, is constantly exposed to extreme meteorological events. In recent years, these events have become more frequent and damaging due to climate change. From droughts to floods and erratic climate. The poverty rate in the country is 40%, and it is estimated that 75% of the population lives below the poverty line.
- The two main sectors in which the country intends to reduce its emissions are: (i) energy; and (ii) agriculture.
- The government has committed to reducing its greenhouse gas emission by 16% by 2030, and almost 90% of this reduction will be achieved through the energy sector.
- The Honduran government has also published a NAMA for a low-carbon livestock sector in 2018, in line with its national 2013 Climate Change Law, which demonstrates the importance of the rural sector in Honduras. This law aims to reduce emissions from the livestock sector by 5% between 2016 and 2020, and thousands of families in a sector that is facing more difficulties every year due to the erratic behaviour of the climate.

Limiting warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels will require changes in our economic system, including the way energy is consumed, the way industries operate, and the way food is produced. The investment required to achieve this goal is huge, ranging from USD 1.6 trillion to USD 3.8 trillion per year between 2016 and 2050. This is a challenge that will require mobilizing all available resources. In addition, governments have a key role in playing in mobilising the remaining two-thirds of the funding needed to close the climate finance gap.

The IFC has estimated the climate finance gap in developing countries for this decade (to 2030) at USD 23 trillion. If this target is met, climate finance will account for 30% or total lending by financial institutions by 2030.

Lending by Financial Institutions has so far been channelled mainly by large banks to finance large renewable energy projects and other infrastructure projects. However, there are opportunities for mobilizing climate finance through smaller financial institutions as they are able to mobilize climate finance to markets underserved by large banks. These institutions are essential to channel climate finance to the two key segments that larger banks typically do not serve because of their perceived risk.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## The smallholder segment accounts for 60%-70% of environmental impacts and a significant share of global carbon emissions**. This segment represents a large opportunity for mitigation for SMEs typically use energy inefficient equipment and vehicles, and unreliable sources of electricity which could be improved by investment in SMEs. If properly supported, this segment has the potential to significantly increase energy efficiency and reduce carbon emissions.

### Smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable as they are among the most affected populations to climate change, need to adapt quickly to new climatic conditions by adopting climate-smart agricultural practices and equipment. It is estimated that 70% of smallholder farmers in the LAC region will be exposed to at least one negative impact of rising temperatures, unpredictable rainfall and extreme weather events, pests and disease outbreaks, declining incomes, and food security**. Smallholders can also reduce their carbon footprint by adopting climate-smart agricultural practices and equipment. Addressing this segment is a key role in financing climate-smart agricultural equipment to enhance adaptation and promote mitigation.

#### Climate adaptation for smallholder farmers

The smallholder finance gap in the region is USD 20 billion**. This financing gap is exacerbated by lack of capital and risk appetite of financial institutions which makes selecting the right business model in terms of technology, products, and services to properly serve the smallholder farmers segment with great difficulty. The segment has a high potential for growth as it produces most of the region’s agricultural products. However, this segment represents a significant challenge for SMEs, as the financing of their operations needs come from their expansion into rural areas, as the penetration of financial services in urban areas is already high throughout the LAC region.

The smallholder farmers segment is important to climate change mitigation, but pales in comparison to the adaptation and resilience needs and impacts on the sector. Five out six farms in the world are less than two hectares in size, occupy around 12 per cent of the world’s agricultural land and produce about 35 per cent of the world’s food42, but the contribution of smallholder farmers to climate change mitigation is minimal compared with large-scale agriculture. Smallholders represent a highly vulnerable population on the planet. Their livelihoods are entirely dependent on the environment and are extremely sensitive to changes in weather patterns and extreme events. As a result, climate change threatens to make smallholders the most climate vulnerable population on the planet.

The main practical risks faced by smallholder farmers are:

- **Drought:** A temporary anomaly in which the availability of water at below the availability falls over the normal range for that time period. Drought is not enough water to meet the needs of plants, animals and people. It is mainly caused by a lack of rainfall or precipitation.
- **Desertification:** Decrease in the volume of the final product, whether agricultural or livestock, caused by sub-optimal production conditions.

Loss of production resources: Total or partial damage to the ecosystem in which production activities, whether agricultural or livestock, take place.

Increase in production costs: The need to cover higher costs of purchasing inputs for agricultural and livestock production.

Increase or occurrence of pests: Increase or occurrence of diseases in crops/organisms of plants in agriculture or livestock. This can be due to a change in the climate or other factors that affect the life cycle of animals.

Landscapes changes or movement of land masses, caused by the instigation of a slope.

Flooding: Occasion by water of areas that are normally free of water, due to overflowing of rivers, torrents, lakes, and seas. Flooding can also be occasioned by heavy rainfall.

Fires: A large scale fire that burns or provokes and destroys vegetation or crops that are not intended to burn.

Erosion: Wear and tear and modelling of the earth's crust caused by the action of wind, rain, fluvial, marine and glacial processes. Erosion also affects the length of living organisms.

Decreased availability of water: A decrease in the amount of water available, either due to less water being absorbed by the soil or a decrease in rainfall.

Reduction in biodiversity: Loss of species diversity that is essential for food to food and the biological use of food.

Building climate resilience for these farmers requires a massive improvement in agricultural value chains by adopting climate-smart practices, crops and assets. The improved value chains will expand smallerholder access to markets and reduce their vulnerability to climate change through better adaptation strategies. These crops and practices will restore nutrients into depleted soils and enable farmers to achieve sustainable yields with less water use and lower greenhouse gas emissions. This will also ensure that new lives practiced and organic matter generated will sequester CO2, helping to mitigate climate change.

Every adoption of low-emissions agriculture in developing countries is an opportunity for more efficient use of land and water resources and water. However, substantial funding may be needed to adopt new technologies, given diverse and dispersed smallholders and costs of monitoring.

Climate finance for SMEs:

SMEs facilitate economic inclusion and livelihood benefits for the world's marginalised by offering employment as well as goods and services to rural and urban communities, thereby contributing to socially inclusive, sustainable development.

MSMEs account for about 99% of all enterprises and employ about 67% of all workers in Latin America®. However, due to a particular productivity gap, they account for only a quarter of the region's total output value.

SMEs face several specific challenges regarding CO2 emissions and climate change like their difficulty to measure and manage the emissions they produce and increasing the efficiency of the resources they use.

Left unaddressed, these challenges will have significant negative impacts on SMEs' economic growth by increasing operating costs, limiting resource availability, causing shifts in demand among supply chains, which in turn introduces new challenges to a fragile and vulnerable enterprise system.

It is in the SME's self-interest in finding ways to both mitigate emissions and to increase resilience—helping future-proof their business. This will also help them retain market access and enhance their competitive edge and thereby make it a profitable business for them.

## B

### Rusk and GCF will work with smaller Financial Service Providers (FSPs) such as microfinance institutions, cooperatives, non-bank financial institutions, Tier 2 banks and Finnish companies aiming at deepening green and climate financial inclusion to SMEs in underserved farmers.

Based on the above, it is clear that there are already a number of innovative climate companies that already use technology solutions to prevent climate change, by offering climate mitigation and resilience solutions to reduce GHG emissions or addressing the impacts of global warming. These innovative companies that use technology solutions include agri-tech companies, tech-companies, renewable companies, or ecosystem-based companies. For example, AgroEnergy is a Finnish company that has developed a biogas system for farmers with an easy-to-install and use prefabricated modular biogasifier package that includes a full suite of biogas appliances and connections. The system takes organic waste and transforms it to a renewable biogas and a process heat, which can be used as heating energy in the farmer’s home or as electricity for the farm. It can also be used as mechanical energy for farm productive activities or as a heating system for the farmer's home.

To meet the climate change mitigation goals of most emerging markets, there is an urgent need to finance innovative companies that use technology for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

## Adaptation:
- **Reduced crop yields, income generation and food security due to unpredictable rainfall, extreme weather events, and disease incidence, etc.**
  - Invest in FPS so that they can provide financing and foster adaptation to climate change through the adoption of resilient-based solutions such as tree intercropping, agroforestry, abandoned farmland restoration, improved water management, fertilizers, regenerative annual cropping systems, etc.
  - Achieve increased smallholder/indigenous farmer resilience and adaptive capacity.

## PROGRAMME STRUCTURE: COMPONENTS AND ACTIVITIES
The emissions reductions that need to occur before 2030 can largely be achieved in the 2020s by existing technologies such as solar, while the post-2030 emission reductions depend on innovative technologies. A report from Boston Consulting Group (BCG) states that the cost of achieving these reductions will be USD 5 trillion to USD 10 billion annually.

Therefore, Kiwi Fund-GCF’s investments in these innovative companies will be essential to the climate change mitigation agenda. However, additionally, in the context of COVID-19 pandemic and its subsequent economic crisis, many of these innovative companies have suffered lack of funding and are now facing financial difficulties. The Kiwi Fund-GCF has therefore provided USD 50 million to help them recover their financial stability and increase their investment readiness so that they can attract further funding.

Finally, Kiwi Fund-GCF will foster alliances between FPSs and the innovative companies so that FPSs can finance the acquisition of the solution that the company implements. To achieve successful alliances, the FPSs need to leverage its knowledge of FPSs to work with them and with the innovative company to understand their incentives, manage their expectations and reach a partnership which is beneficial for both.

## Climate finance best practices
Countries, especially the finance industry are under pressure to adopt climate finance best practices, driven by regulation and by market dynamics, in order to reach the international commitment to greenhouse gases reduction. As financiers, FIs have the potential to substantially move the scale toward mitigation and adaptation to climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

There are four main areas via which FIs can engage in more sustainable practices, as identified in IFC’s 2018 survey:

- **Green strategic commitment:** engaging with top management to implement green finance practices, cross-departmental policies and procedures, and integrating environmental issues into a core pillar of core strategic decisions. In our methodology this corresponds to Green Index Phase 3.
- **Environmental Risk Management system:** adapting current risk management system to include environmental risks, as well as incorporating environmental factors in credit analysis. In our methodology this corresponds to Green Index Phase 2.
- **Green products and services:** building a diversified set of products which incorporate environmental benefits into the financial product design. In our methodology this corresponds to Green Index Phase 4.

IFC’s survey shows that FIs are already taking steps to adopt more sustainable banking models, but still need support in the four areas highlighted above. Many banks mention that “sustainable finance models need access to new sources of financing, in particular, for growing responsible investors that exercise a strong demand for green investments.” The role of financial institutions (FIs) is crucial in this regard. Fintechs are playing an active banking and actively influencing the decisions and strategies adopted by companies and banks on climate change. However, while some FIs have already started to engage with fintechs, most traditional financial institutions and Fintechs (denominated as FIs) are key to serve SMEs and smallholder farmers, but they are lagging in the adoption of these sustainable practices. Capital offered by the Funded Activity is therefore essential.

Another key constraint the financial sector faces to serve the SMEs and smallholder farmers segments is performing an efficient detailed credit analysis of these final beneficiaries. Fintech sector is developing very interesting solutions for this purpose, such as using big data and AI to perform credit scoring. The Fintech sector will be essential in Kufuand-GCF investments, by supporting green investments to SMEs and smallholder farmers (e.g., through the use of digital tools to improve access to finance, including balance sheets) and Fintech companies. Fintech represents not only an opportunity to deliver green financing to SMEs or farmers, but also an innovative channel to serve SMEs and smallholder farmers more efficiently and in underserved areas where only technology can reach them.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## PROBLEM STATEMENT

Kuall Fund GCF2 target countries face serious difficulties in achieving greenhouse gas reduction goals in countries with permanent growing demographic expectations. The financial sector worldwide is under pressure to embrace climate finance best practices. Likewise, the reduction of activity due to the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic has increased the need for access to capital and credit. The impact on the cost of market liquidity is even more severe with regard to SMEs (USD 90 billion gap) and smallholders (USD 20 billion gap). In addition, women-owned microenterprises face significant barriers in their ability to access the market in relation to available margins, heterogeneity of the market, impediments to SME and smallholder farmers growth and productivity and regulatory constraints.

The gap between the financial needs and the supply of financial services is anticipated to remain significant. In Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC), 70% of adults have access to a bank account, but only 45% of adults in average had an account in a financial institution or mobile money provider (89%-100% in high income economies). Account ownership disparities are even more pronounced for women.

Financial access is more challenging for women. In LAC countries, women are 7 percentage points less likely than men to have an account. A financing gap worth USD $5 billion has been identified for women-led micro-business alone and an additional shortfall of USD $3 billion for women-led SMEs*. Female labor force participation in the region is 10% lower than that of men, and women are more likely to be employed in informal sectors where access to finance is limited. Gender norms around property rights, human capital, and social capital which are key constraints for the growth of their business. These limitations are exacerbated by lack of awareness about financial services and gender norms limiting women’s ability to access this finance without legal ownership of assets and guarantees. Participants in the financial system need not only be aware of and understand how gender norms limit women's access and use of financial services.

Finally, despite their socioeconomic importance, smallholder farmers, the hardest hit by climate change, are the most underserved in financial services. Women in agriculture have less access to key assets, fewer land rights, and lower levels of education than men. They also face greater barriers within family and the community. On top of that, women are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change.

The vulnerability to environmental risks and climate change of SMEs, smallholder farmers, women and poor households in general is linked to their reliance on expensive, unreliable, polluting, and potentially harmful energy sources. They also lack access to affordable, reliable, sustainable, modern, efficient, and clean climate smart technologies and practices to reduce emissions, gain resilience and increase their yields.

## SHIFT IN THE DEVELOPMENT PATHWAY

Financial inclusion and environmental protection are critical issues for people living in poverty and key levers to achieve inclusive and sustainable economic growth. Financial services are central to the lives of everyone, allowing people to participate in the economy, access services, seize green opportunities and build resilience. When financial institutions adopt a climate lens, they can provide more support to clients through investment and adaptation, education and manage risks. When the financial sector manages environmental risks and fosters green and climate products, it can improve clients' resilience to climate change.

There are financial service Providers (FSPs) deepening financial services to SMEs and people underserved by traditional banks. Besides, there are technology solutions and climate smart practices available for implementation, offered by Innovative Companies (ICs) to prevent climate change.

ICs can provide the necessary technical assistance and capacity building in key areas of focus for poor segments and support reliable climate solutions to SMEs and farmers, thanks to the provision of dedicated financing and technical support. The proximity of microfinance institutions to their clients and their expertise in credit products and services make them well positioned to offer financial services to SMEs and farmers, and ICs need to implement appropriate environmental strategies, risk management systems and deploy, provide in turn, climate smart financial and non-financial services to SMEs and smallholder farmers.

If Kuafi Fund and GCF are able to increase the availability of green financial services and promote business practices in green Innovative Companies (ICs) and financial service providers THEN due to the reduced financing gap and perceived risk of lending, SMEs and smallholder farmers will have access to green financial services. This will enable them to adopt climate smart technologies and practices, which are not yet ready for the new environment with potential higher credit cost, disruption of operations, etc., the gap between the financial needs of smallholders and the supply of financial services; the gender barriers in accessing finance; the lack of access to information on climate risks and adaptation technologies and practices at an affordable cost for SMEs and smallholders to manage climate risks; the limited availability of low carbon technologies; the limited regional cooperation, knowledge management and finally, the fact that most promising current climate technologies are still at a prototype phase.

Once capital from private investors is raised by Kuafi Fund in addition to public funding, debt and equity investments, it will be possible to scale up the financial services provided by FSPs to SMEs and smallholder farmers. The investment will also support the development of new products and services for operational (output) and SMEs and Smallholder farmers will receive, in turn, green financial services from Kuafi Fund.

In parallel, other activities such as the setup of the governance structure and management of the associated Kuafi’s Technical Assistance will entail the launching of the tendering processes to contract the right service providers for the delivery of the technical assistance. The outputs of this activity are the following (output): TA Projects will be devoted to Green Enhancement for SMEs and smallholder farmers; Green Readiness with FSPs, Social Performance and Environmental Risk Management Systems for FSPs and ICs; Capacity Building for SMEs and Smallholder Farmers; Knowledge Sharing with Kuafi Fund, stakeholders engagement and gender actions and, finally, Knowledge Sharing activities.

On the investment side, Kuafi’s financing will result (output) in greater availability of green financial services allowing SMEs and smallholder farmers to adopt climate smart technologies and practices for Climate

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

## Smart Agriculture and adoption of Climate Solutions will be enhanced in smallholder farmers and financial ecosystem actors' capacities will be strengthened, enabling green and inclusive finance.

As a result, it will be achieved (outcomes) the Adoption of climate solutions and improvement management of agricultural land contributing to reducing GHG emissions, the improved resilience in vulnerable people & communities to the Environmental & inclusive financial ecosystem (technology and market development), as well as knowledge generation.

Greater availability of finance together with capacity building will also drive co-benefits such as Improved Financial Inclusion (as a result of the gender action plan) and efficient Technology adoption by SMEs together with CSA practices promoted by farmers.

### BARRIERS AND RISKS

The poor mitigation and adaptation results from the AFOLU & MSME/SME sector in the past has been caused by several barriers:

#### A. Financial Barrier

A large portion of the current finance provided and the need by the AFOLU sector to realize its mitigation potential. This gap is particularly important for small-scale agriculture (SSA) where limited financial flow is applied. The estimated financing gap for SSA alone is estimated at USD 240 billion annually.

Besides, most of the finance directed toward the sector in general comes from public sources (84%) from multilateral and national (IFD alone)** and it is usually channeled through large banks, public institutions and commercial financial intermediaries. This creates a barrier for SMEs to access finance.

Regarding SMEs, in the developing economies, the finance gap from formal MSMEs is valued at USD 5.2 trillion, while the informal sector faces an even larger financing gap of USD 10.7 trillion. The majority of these firms are below the current level of MSME lending in those countries***. Approximately 40 percent of all enterprises are credit constrained.

A global multilateral climate finance reveals that the explicit integration of SMEs within multilateral climate fund projects still remains marginal and that climate finance flows have only marginally enlisted financial for SMEs. This is due to a lack of awareness among policy makers and stakeholders in discounting the importance of these financial actors for maintaining climate finance in the financial sector****.

FSPs are key to mobilize climate finance to markets underserved by large banks, companies and even public institutions. However, FSPs face several challenges such as: (i) limited access to capital; (ii) that smaller banks do not usually serve because of their perceived risk (smallholders and SMEs). However, FSFs struggle to scale up due to the lack of a clear regulatory framework for climate finance and lack of financial products due to lack of assessment frameworks for green tech & business models.

This finance gap for SMEs hinders the capacity of the whole SME sector to realize its contributions to inclusive economic growth and poverty reduction. The Kualt’s innovative blended finance structure and target investors can effectively channel climate finance and drive investment into sustainable AFOLU and MSMEs, mobilizing important quantity of private resources.

The proposed FSP will address these barriers by providing a solution for the physical, economic and eligibility barriers and FSFs will implement activities to address them to the possible extent.

B. Knowledge Barrier

Financial Services Providers must play a major role in catalyzing clients' plans to mitigate and increase their resilience by providing adequate finance for the required assets and investments. Additionally, FSPPs will also need to adapt their business models to address higher levels of climate risk and opportunities.

However, their business model is not ready for it as FSPPs will need to adapt its risk management systems and business mode to address these new challenges (i.e., climate maps, more resilient operations, better disaster preparedness).

The main barriers that FSP face are the lack of knowledge about climate change (mitigation and adaptation) and the associated risks. This is due to a combination of factors such as limited access to information, a lack of data, tools and methodologies that can manage climate risks, in the form of management systems and "green and climate lending". The regulators and the ecosystem in general are on their way to create a conducive environment for FSPs to adapt.

Kuali through its Technical Assistance Facility will strengthen their FSP investees green and climate products design skills, promote risk management technology, and employee capabilities.


### Innovation Barrier

The so-called Climate Technologies are technologies that are explicitly focused on reducing GHG emissions or increasing resilience. These technologies can be either new or existing technologies that mitigate or negate emissions, help us adapt to the impacts of climate change and enhance our understanding of the climate.

It is estimated that by 2050, almost half of global CO2 emissions reductions will come from technologies that are not yet commercially available. The challenge for FSPs is to identify which technologies are most needed across the innovation life cycle to ensure that commercially ready solutions are able to scale within this next decade. Besides, commercial-ready technologies also need important levels of funding to scale.

Likewise, the allocation of the fundings is heavily skewed in favor of the United States and Europe or China and is also disproportionate directed toward the Mobility and Transport sector receiving 61% of all investments, followed by the diverse energy efficient equipment, industry or food, agriculture and land use are lagging behind in funding.


### Programme Structure: Components and Activities

Kuali Fund-GCF investment strategy aims at supporting smaller Financial Institutions in overcoming these barriers by offering:

- **Capital:** In a form of debt to finance FSP expansion as they transition from Phase 1 of the Climate Change Action Plan. This will be used for new investments, acquisitions or to promote or foster the green and climate portfolio if it already exists. In the case of innovative companies, finance will be used to support their expansion so that their climate solutions can reach scale.
- **Technical assistance:** To implement projects that help FSPs to become greener and eventually more resilient. This technical assistance is provided through a blended model. In the case of innovative companies, technical assistance will be used to strengthen their social performance systems, their business model and increase their investor readiness.

The Theory of Change of Kuali Fund includes the transformation of FSPP's business models to become greener, sustainable and more inclusive for smallholder farmers. Kuali Fund-GCF investments are expected to be transformational in the following areas:

- eIA, Net Zero by 2050, 2021: https://www.iea.org/reports/net-zero-by-2050
- PWC State of Climate Tech 2021

## Implementation of new processes & technologies such as efficient client origination models, credit processes or portfolio risk management systems. Establishing these tools to monitor and mitigate agriculture and SME/smallholder farmer related risks following the adoption of eco-efficiency practices. This will include the implementation of data analytics and client segmentation as tools to reduce credit costs.

## Training for financial institution staff including credit officers, loan officers and managers so that the institution can make strategic commitments of green and climate finance in their lending portfolios.

### Implementation of Environmental Risk Mitigation Systems (both internal and external) to reduce the institution's carbon footprint (reduction in waste, paper, water or energy consumption) and to mitigate environmental risks. This will include the implementation of tools to assess and manage environmental risks during the credit approval process.

## Implementing green and climate products directed to the SME and smallholder farmers such as loans to finance more efficient new machinery or equipment or smart agriculture loans.

After this first phase, the ICs and FSFs invested by the Funded Activity will be greener and more inclusive so that they can continue with their environmental and social impact even after the investment is exited and possibly crowding in more Financial Institutions that have not been investees to launch new climate focused financial products. This will include the implementation of Technical Assistance to FSFs and innovative companies that will strengthen their green and climate SME and smallholder farmers product design & offering, processes & risk management, technology, and employee capacity building.

### Processes: reflects the policies, tools, mechanisms, procedures and frameworks that innovative companies and FSFs (FinTechs) adopted use to identify, assess and manage environmental risks. Kulli Fund-GCF's approach is to support these companies in their efforts of implementing environmental risk management systems to monitor and manage their own ecological footprint and their clients' ecological footprint by implementing changes in the loan approval process to include the environmental risk level of the client.

### Products: the ICs and FSFs progress towards sustainability begins in many cases with the adoption of eco-efficiency practices, but Kulli Fund-GCF's purpose is to make them reach a strategic green commitment. The products will be intentionally and differentially marketed as green and climate to the FSF's client base. Climate financing products include loans to finance energy efficient equipment, solar panels, hybrid vehicles or waste management systems. These products can also include other types of projects such as ecosystem-based adaptation solutions, recycling, waste management or climatic microinsurance products that will help these beneficiaries become more resilient to environmental shocks and climate change.

### Governance: People: environmental lending products are potentially more complex in their appraisal and reporting. Staff will often require training to recognize potential customer opportunities, communicate the benefits of green finance and understand how to assess and manage environmental risks on key indicators. Support will be provided in the form of Technical Assistance that allows innovative companies and FSFs to build capacity for environmental risk management and lending. This includes the creation of in-house specialist teams. Over time, these skills can be mainstreamed into the organization. Technical assistance will also be used to design and launch training programs to SMEs and smallholder farmers on how to access green finance products.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23

## Technological recent advances in digital technologies and innovative business models represent a game changer that can help close the SME and smallholder farmers finance gap by enabling smaller firms to tap alternative sources of funding, thanks to the data generated by their digital footprint. The COVID-19 pandemic has exacerbated this problem, with millions of small businesses being at risk of closing permanently if the crisis persists, while others are suffering from reduced sales and cash flow. This is particularly true in developing countries and emerging market countries where there are limited public resources to help small businesses. The use of digital technologies is proving to be a game changer for these firms by providing them with access to credit and the delivery of financial services can be done through several players such as financial technology (FinTech) firms, which generally focus on a particular financial product or service and utilize new technology platforms to deliver their products and services. These FinTech firms may partner directly with SMEs and smallholder farmers, or through financial institutions that adopt these new products and services or may partner with FinTech firms in order to provide the necessary credit and financial services. This can be a faster process by automating the underwriting and loan servicing tasks making it significantly cheaper, faster, and easier to provide financing to SMEs and smallholder farmers. In addition, the use of alternative data sources such as social media, mobile phone records, and other digital footprints will help provide additional information to assess the credit risk of SMEs and smallholder farmers. The data from digital footprints can be used by FinTech firms to better understand their customer needs and patterns, thereby helping create new products and services.

These strategies aim to transform the FSP's operations to increase SME and smallholder farmers' access to more affordable lending for climate-related loans. It benefits both levels of beneficiaries FSPs (diversification of income sources, increased access to credit, improved financial inclusion, greater liquidity, more resilient, reduction of their energy costs, or increase access to global value chains). These strategies are aligned with the Fund's three main goals: (i) Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation; Financial Inclusion and gender outreach; (ii) Employment preservation and labor women inclusion (the latter two specially fueled by the gender action plan) and (iii) Efficient Technology adoption by SMEs together with smallholder farmers.

The Funded Activity will provide loans, equity, and grants (through the Technical Assistance Facility) with a gender approach as well as increase the availability of inclusive financial products for women led SMEs and women-led smallholder farmers and indigenous women as the majority of indigenous communities in the region works as smallholder farmers.

Kuali Fund-GCF's commitment in environmental and social fields is reflected in the Portfolio Impact Score (PIS), a tool designed by the Fund Manager that has already been successfully employed in previous funds. The PIS measures the likelihood of each investment to generate positive or negative social and/or environmental impacts generated. The methodology of this tool consists of applying a score to the performance of each project, which is then averaged for all investments within an individual fund. The Portfolio Impact Score will be measured at the beginning of each investment during the social and environmental due diligence process. At the end of the investment period, the Fund Manager will provide the Investors Advisory Committee will select an independent social auditor that will measure the Audited Impact Score. A Kuali Fund-GCF's Audited Impact Score will be obtained as the average of all the investor's Audited Impact Scores for each investment.

The PIS adopts the Impact Management process ant it is the weighted average of two main dimensions: (i) Outcome/Output metrics, measured at investee level, and summed to give an aggregate score at the Funded Activity level; and (ii) Transformative metrics, aimed at measuring to what extent the investees are

GREEN CLIMATE FUND GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 24

B


developing a business model to better serve smallholder farmers or SMEs and these metrics are measured and scored at investor level and applied as a weighted average to give the Funded Activity's score.

Transformation metrics are made up of two sets of metrics: (1) Green Index® — which measures how well an FSP is aligned with the goals of the Green Climate Fund (GCF), the International Finance Corporation (IFC), the Global Inclusive and Climate Smart Finance Action Group (GISCAF™) and (2) Social Performance Score — which measures how effectively an organization achieves its stated social and environmental goals and climate change adaptation.

Outcome/outcome metrics are made up of two set of metrics for a FSP: (1) Outcome/Output Map — which is designed to measure impact, defined as the change in an outcome/output caused by FSP activities and (2) Impact Metrics Scorecard — which measures the extent to which the FSP has contributed to achieving the change in an outcome/output at the investor level that is not being captured by the Funded Activity level performance.

The score is calculated as a weighted average (investment value) of the Transformative Metric Score and Outcome/Output Metric Scores for FSPs and Innovation Companies. All scores range between 0 and 1.


The chart below summarizes the process to calculate the Portfolio Impact Score:

| Physical Social Potential | Transformational Innovation |
| --- | --- |
| **Physical Social Potential** (25%) | **Transformational Innovation** (75%) |
| **Investment 1** (25% of Physical Social Potential) | **Investment 3** (75% of Transformational Innovation) |
| **Scenario 0.0** | **Scenario 0.0** |
| **Scenario 0.25** | **Scenario 0.75** |
| **Scenario 1.0** | **Scenario 1.0** |
| **Investment 2** (50% of Physical Social Potential) | **Innovation Complete Output Scorecard** (25% of Transformational Innovation) |
| **Scenario 0.0** | **Scenario 0.75** |
| **Scenario 0.25** | **Scenario 1.0** |
| **Scenario 1.0** | **Scenario 1.0** |
| **Final Portfolio Impact Score = 75% of FSP Transformational Innovation Score + 25% of FSP Output Scorecard Score** |


The result will be established within a range of 0-10 and will be analyzed and justified by external auditors. The carried interest will be linked to this green and climate performance, so if the global portfolio score is positive, the Fund Manager will benefit from a better carried interest. In this way, the Manager's interests are aligned with the results achieved in environmental, social and climate issues.

It provides a framework to assess the performance of FSPs against a set of criteria that are aligned with the goals of the GCF, IFC, GISCAF™ and other relevant stakeholders. The Green Index 3.0 complements the Green Index by providing investors indicators to improve their green inclusive finance. SPAs aimus complements the Green Index 3.0 by providing investors indicators to improve their green inclusive finance performance.

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

It should be noticed that the PIS is not used during the identification phase. This phase relates to pipeline construction and the preliminary investment committee. At those stages, more macro-level variables are being taken into account (e.g., financial ratios, overall green portfolio, average loan, social mission and targets, etc.). Then, the PIS is used in the Due Diligence stage as a baseline for the project’s performance against the GCF Fund’s targets. During this Due Diligence, an initial PIS is constructed to serve as a baseline and also to establish a line of benchmarking for the project. The PIS is then updated during the investment committee meeting to reflect the project’s progress towards achieving the GCF Fund’s targets. At this stage, the PIS is used to make an investment decision and if favorable to establish a line for monitoring.

B. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

Kuali Fund will support the transformation of the LAC (minimum of 70% of Kuali Fund resources) and India (at least 30% of the total resources) financial sector to become more inclusive and greener by providing climate finance for SMEs and smallholder farmers, fostering systemic adoption of climate finance best practices in the financial sector, and promoting the development of a green bond market. The LAC countries have committed to reduce greenhouse gases and to adopt best climate practices. Kuali Fund will invest in 15 countries in LAC and India. Nevertheless, since no Objection Letters from the whole universe of countries have been received, the total amount of resources allocated for each country will be determined by the share of NGL countries in the total resources available from Kuali Fund. Therefore, there will be separate contributions, there will be also separate cash flow statements and revenues.

The size of Kuali Fund-QCF investments in NGL countries is expected to reach EUR 119.5 million in total commitments to be used for investments EUR 5.91 million in Technical Assistance. An additional EUR 20 million will be allocated as a contribution to the Trust Fund for Monitoring and Evaluating Data Collection. Kuali Fund-QCF is expected to catalyze private and institutional investment at scale through a blended finance structure. The EU and GCF contributions to the fund are expected to total approximately EUR 135 million, of which EUR 80 million will be used to mobilize private investors: participation by reducing their exposure to the perceived risk of the SME and smallholder farmers. The remaining funding is expected to come from the Trust Fund (EUR 24.9 million) and the SIFC (EUR 30.1 million). EUR 8.6 million from the SIFC itself would be channeled through a Securityization Vehicle (SV) pari passu with private investors, which will be used to finance the Technical Assistance component of the blended finance strategy, and it will be managed by the same General Partner. AECID contribution to the SV will be in Senior Notes form, and it will be managed by the same General Partner. The remaining funding is expected to come from contributions from other donors (EUR 10 million) and from the Trust Fund (approximately EUR 73.8 million), which will be raised in collaboration with top private banking institutions. Cofides will also invest as a private investor (approximately EUR 5 million).

The QCF, EU, FONPROIDE and Kuali Fund itself will also contribute to the funding of the Technical Assistance component of the blended finance strategy. The EU contribution is EUR 0.3 million from FONPROIDE and EUR 0.4 from Kuali Fund). Since Kuali Fund will invest in 15 countries, 100% of GCF resources will be allocated in NGL countries, and a 15% of the sum of EU contributions in first loss and EU, FONPROIDE and Cofides will also invest as private investors (approximately EUR 5 million).

## Kuali Fund Activities in TA resources will be allocated in NOL countries (Kuali Fund-GCF), keeping 85% of their resources to cover the no-NOL countries of Kuali Fund.

The objective of Kuali Fund-GCF is to transform SPS in their transition to become greener and more inclusive. The Fund Activity will support companies with innovative solutions (dominated as innovative companies) to expand the number of available mitigation and adaptation options that could eventually be financed by GCF. This objective will be met through the economic growth, poverty reduction, and social inclusion.

### Financial Support

- **Component 1:** EUR 0.4 million is requested to GCF for Capacity Building (CB) activities in four areas: Monitoring and Evaluation Data Collection; Monitoring and Evaluative Data Collection.
- **Component 2:** EUR 5.7 million is requested to GCF for TA support related to four Focus Areas.

Both components will be implemented by the Fund Activity, with a focus on capacity building and commitment. The TA Manager will make its best efforts as that all can develop TA projects in Q3.

### Kuali Fund-GCF

- **Outcome 1:** Improve resilience of vulnerable people and communities (especially in smallholder farmers) to climate change.
- **Outcome 2:** Increase employment and labor income inclusion (specifically funded by the gender action plan).
- **Outcome 3:** Reduce GHG emissions through the adoption of climate solutions and improvement management of agricultural lands.

### Financial Support

- **Component 1:** EUR 0.4 million is requested to GCF for Capacity Building activities in four areas: Monitoring and Evaluation Data Collection; Monitoring and Evaluative Data Collection.
- **Component 2:** EUR 5.7 million is requested to GCF for TA support related to four Focus Areas.

### Financial Support

The Fund Activity will invest in Financial Service Providers (Tes 2 banks, MFIs, cooperatives, non-bank financial institutions and Fintech companies) to drive transformational change that will generate a positive environmental impact. The Fund Activity will focus on two main areas: the identification of target segments (SMEs and smallholder farmers), supporting its investors in their green transition to develop climate solutions; and the development of a strategy for the Fund Activity to invest in these FSPs, to build collaboration and synergies between these traditional FSPs and in line with emerging Open Banking trends in Europe and in Emerging Markets. Kuali Fund-GCF will transform these institutions to adopt and implement innovative solutions at scale.

The index divides the investments into 4 Phases:

- **Phase 1:** FSP has a green strategy and systems to implement it.

        - Detailed goals, mission, documented strategy.
        - Alignment with relevant laws, regulations, and regulation.
    - **1.2:** Implementation of the green strategy.
        - Management, governance, and processes.
        - Monitoring and evaluation.

- **Phase 2:** FSP identifies environmental risks and opportunities.
    - **2.1:** Identification of direct risks & opportunities.

With respect to Component I and II, COFIDES as Accredited Entity will be the Executing Entity for all activities relating to evaluating data collection and communication audit.

**Kuafi Fund-GFF** will invest approximately **60% of the capital in FSFs (either Green or Super Green Investments)**. The remaining capital will be invested in a mix of green and climate technologies, and eventually offering financing for mitigation and adaptation solutions to its clients, an attractive and underpenetrated market opportunity. Kuafi Fund-GFF will invest the remaining capital (**approximately 30%**) in innovative companies that provide or implement products and services with a direct positive impact on climate change resilience.

Kuafi Fund-GFF will support FSFs which, in turn, might finance SMEs in assets or activities such as solar roof-top panels, high-efficiency heat pumps, insulation, low-emission transport, energy-efficient machinery, methane digesters, among others; and the smallholder farmers may be supported in activities such as free planting of trees, agroforestry systems, intercropping, crop rotation, drip irrigation, organic farming, direct cropping, silvopastoral systems or tree intercropping. As regards to innovative companies, Kuafi Fund-GFF will support them by providing financing for their operations and/or investments in assets such as a financial service provider, biodigester manufacturer companies, EV charging network) and in adaptation (farm-to-business mobility supply chain: small, medium farmers). Financing as a series of syndicated credit facilities.

Kuafi Fund-GFF is expected to build a portfolio of 12-30 investments. Each investment is expected to range between EUR 5 and 20 million, with an average of EUR 8 million. As mentioned above, debt-like investments might have an automatic renewal in the case that certain financial and environmental milestones are met.

### Eligibility and Investment criteria

The potential beneficiaries of the Kuafi Fund-GFF are defined as follows:

- **Potential investors:**
  - Financial Service Providers (FSPs): An institution focused on the provision of a diverse set of financial services to individuals, small and micro enterprises up to micro-, small and medium enterprises including savings, loans, insurance, leasing, and factoring products. These institutions are oriented towards providing mainly enterprise financing (versus consumer financing which is dominated by banks) with a close client relationship, often based on deposit-taking banking and a close lender-borrower relationship. The institution can take the form of a variety of legal entities, including banks, NGOs, microfinance institutions, cooperatives, finance companies, credit unions, etc.
  - Innovative Companies: Companies that provide or implement products and services that have a direct positive impact on climate change resilience.

### Final beneficiaries

- MSME/SME: enterprise qualifies as a micro, small or medium enterprise if it meets two out of three criteria of the IFI MSME Definition (employees, assets, and sales), OR if the loan to it falls within the relevant MSME loan size proxy.

## IFC MSME Definition

| Indicator | EMPLOYEES | TOTAL ASSETS (USD) | ANNUAL SALES (USD) | LOAN SIZE AT ORIGINATION |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Micro enterprise | <10 | <10,000 million | <100,000 million | <10,000 million |
| Small enterprise | 10-49 | 100,000 -3 million | 100,000 -3 million | <100,000 million |
| Medium enterprise | 50-300 | 3-15 million | 3-15 million | <1 or 2 million |

### Smallholder farmers (IFC's definition): marginal and sub-marginal farm households constrained in size and resources. Some sources define smallholder farmers as those who cultivate between 1 hectare to 10 hectares (for more semi-and areas) of land. While they will vary in terms of their level of technology adoption, the common defining characteristics of smallholder farmers are that they have low access to technology, limited resources, and often engage in subsistence farming with a focus on food production activities, and have limited capacity in terms of storage, marketing, and processing.

The definition of smallholder might differ from investment to investment, depending on the investment's specific context. The definition will be established at the beginning of each investment in the Social Due Diligence Memorandum.

## Kuali Fund-GCF will originate potential investing opportunities according to the following criteria:

1. **Social and Environmental Impact Criteria:** Kuali Fund-GCF will assess the potential social or environmental impact of its investees through a tool called Portfolio Impact Score (PIS). This tool applies a score to the performance of each project based on social aspects - gender-included- and environmental objectives previously set.

2. **Financial criteria:** The Fund's Activity's financial eligibility criteria will be further defined in the Policies and Procedures Manual that will be developed in the following phase. In particular, in general terms and subject to the specific characteristics of the country, potential investees need to meet the following financial investment criteria:

    * **For Financial Sector Providers:**
        * Minimum total assets of EUR 20MM.
        * A positive Return On Assets as in 2021 after the COVID-19 impact.
        * Capital Adequacy Ratio (CAR) around 15%.
        * Portfolio At Risk <30 days + rescheduled loans less or around 10%.
        * Write-off ratio not less than 2%.
        * Operating profit margin of either or around 100%.
        * At least 2 years of audited statements and 3 years of operations.

    * **For companies with innovative climate solutions:**
        * Positive EBITDA.
        * EBITDA break-even or projection for EBITDA break-even in following 12 months with review of debt-to-equity and debt service coverage ratios.

## Sustainable and scalable sources of revenues (€UR 500k per year).
