Finally, according to the investment selection process, Kuafi will follow the next phases for any deal:
* A preliminary report is developed to check the initial criteria and needs.
    * This report will be presented at a public internal meeting.
        * If the Committee agrees to move forward with the project, then a Due Diligence will be addressed.
            * Once duly finalized, a final report of the operation will be developed.
                * This report will be submitted for approval by Kuafi through a final internal committee to decide whether the operation is subject to be financed or not.
                    * If the committee has agreed to move forward, then the legal and financial documentation must be harmonized with the Green Fund and the beneficiaries.
                        * Signature of the contract.
                            * Disbursement.

## Selection of financing instrument: Kuafi-GCF will select the instrument that (i) best fits the financing needs of the investor, (ii) provides, in most cases, the expected risk-adjusted returns and (iii) in case of equity, has a clear liquidity event. More specifically, it is expected to invest through the following instruments:
* Debt investments usually with a maturity of 2-3-2 years, with automatic renewal in the case of continued profitability and/or if certain Key Performance Indicators are met; all investments will be in local currency and any foreign exchange risk will be offset through swaps.
    * The typical debt investment will be a FSD.
* Equity investments: equity investments can take common or convertible equity, royalty financing and other mezzanine schemes in FSP or innovative companies. Common or convertible equity will be used when a liquidity event for the investee (FSP, secondary sales, etc.) is expected to occur within 2-3 years of investment; royalty financing will be used along, mandate sale or right to sell on a capital expansion). Mezzanine investments can be structured as debt or equity and are usually made in companies that have already met certain Key Performance Indicators of the investee such as revenues or net income, which are proxy for how well the company is doing. The advantage of mezzanine instruments is that they can provide more flexibility to the investor than a pure debt instrument; however, it is expected to be used for innovative companies where the best financing option would be equity but where exit might be an issue as liquidity events could be difficult.

Kuafi will originate potential investing opportunities according to the following criteria:
* For FSPs: Kuafi must have expressed interest in developing a project (maximum amount of investment, country). In the case of FSPs, investee management must have expressed interest in advancing towards green and climate opportunities and products. In the case of innovative companies, Kuafi must have expressed interest in supporting clear social and environmental strategy and plans to strengthen and expand its operations.
* Basic ratio analysis: profitability (ROA or Contribution/EBITDA Margin), Capitalization rate (Debt/Equity Ratio), Portfolio quality and revenue growth rate.
* Quality of management team
* Country risk assessment
* In FSPs, there are certain Social Performance criteria that can directly exclude an opportunity, such as high interest rates (relative to sector), elevated levels of profitability or excessively aggressive business plans.

### Besides all the above criteria, Kuali Investment team will ensure that every investment will also have a
adequate management team with a track record and knowledge of FSPS and appropriate Management Systems and Credit Policies. Also, the investments in FSFS need to comply with the
Anti-Money Laundering (AML) and Counter-Terrorism Financing (CTF) requirements, including the list
of exclusion and are not involved in OFAC official and ultimate beneficiary lists. Climatic Requirements will be considered by Kuali Fund as a priority for investment opportunities with a track record in their green transition by incorporating these solutions into their processes and business,
with the ultimate target of assisting on its transformation. The Investment portfolio will balance a mix
of entities where the majority of their activities are green and entities that are still not advanced in their
green transition.

### Use of funds

The Funded Activity guidelines for the use of GCF funds are as follow:

#### Kuali Fund-GCF Portfolio

Kuali Fund-GCF's portfolio will be built during the investment period (5 years from the first closing) complying with the following milestones:
- Kuali Fund will use GCF funding for investments in eligible Innovative Companies or for lending to FSPS. Kuali Fund undertakes to use GCF funding only for building Climate Portfolio following the eligibility criteria.
- FSFS will be allowed a period of 3 years to build a Climate Portfolio. If an FSFS is not able to build this Climate Portfolio by the end of Year 3, then the AE/Kuali Fund will request the FSP the total amount disbursed from the GCF funding and the interest on it.
- GCF funds will not be used by Kuali Fund FSFS for financing assets or projects other than the climate
projects as per eligibility criteria.

- On an ongoing basis, Kuali Fund commits on a commercially reasonable best effort basis to build an aggregate Climate Portfolio equal to one hundred percent of the aggregated amount disbursed by co-investors and/or lenders from GCF funding.
- Ramp-up period for building Climate Portfolio: for co-financing in 3+3=50% of the Climate Portfolio by end of Year 3 and 100% by end of the following 3 years. In 2+2+2, 30% of end by Year 2, 65% by
end of the following 2 years and 100% by the end of the following 2 years.

#### Equity Investments

GCF funds will be exclusively used for equity investment in Innovative Companies that are companies that manufacture or market products and services that have a direct positive effect on mitigation and adaptation and related to climate change.
These Companies will not have any other relevant business lines and therefore the funds will exclusively support the advancement of climate solutions and impact.

The sectorial scope is wide-ranging, but it should relate to the sectors, technologies and specific criteria depicted in the technology list.

**Page 39**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 35

## Equity investments in FSP and other Innovative Companies with mixed climatic and non-climatic portfolios and solutions will remain an alternative for the rest of investors. Even in these cases, the funds will support the advancement of climate solutions and impact.

### Debt Investments
The main objective of Kuali Fund-GCF resources is to transform FSPs to start developing products and a loan portfolio directed towards climate finance. In this regard, Kuali Fund-GCF commits on a commercially reasonable best effort basis that by the exit of the investments 100% of its investees shall have expanded their product lines to include either already a climate finance portfolio or at least will have piloted climate finance products for their clients.

FSPs will start with different climate readiness levels, a few with no climate lending yet; however we expect that FSPs’ climate readiness profiles will increase over time as they adapt their business model in terms of product development and process to provide assets with climate solutions.

Kuali Fund-GCF commits to building a portfolio of debt investments in FSPs. The companies in this portfolio will have the obligation to increase their total aggregated Climate Portfolios during the life of the investment.

In order to ensure an adequate path towards relevant climate products and portfolios at the investee level, Kuali Fund-GCF will include covenants in the loan agreements that will prevent the renewal of the deals if a clear path toward climate finance products has not been achieved. The climatic lending scope is wide-ranging but it should relate to the sectors, technologies and specific criteria for adaptation and mitigation solutions (solution and adaptation) are countries. During the life of the fund new solutions are expected to be added as robust data and peer-reviewed research becomes more available and accessible.

### Requirements
- Climate lending relates to the sectors, technologies, and specific criteria for adaptation and mitigation depicted in the technology list attached as Figure 1 and if of The Term Sheet.
- The Kuali Fund will not finance energy efficiency investments that extend the lifetime of fossil fuel assets.
- The Kuali fund has selected a wide-reaching list of technological solutions that can be frequently implemented by the prototypical clients of the FSP and Innovative Companies in the developing countries that Kuali will serve. The solutions listed proposed by the Kuali Fund as extensive as possible, however, it is expected that the most relevant solutions for each country’s specific climate solution (mitigation and adaptation) are countries. During the life of the fund new solutions are expected to be added as robust data and peer-reviewed research becomes more available and accessible.
- The Exclusion List (attached Annex 3 of the Term sheet) shall apply to investments under the Funded Activity. Based on updated baseline studies, the exclusion list must be revised in consultation with GCF.

## Expected target returns and other terms

Equity or quasi-equity targets return: Kuali Fund-GCF will seek in its equity or quasi-equity investments a relevant minority stake (usually between 15 to 25% ownership) and a board seat and a clear and/or pre-negotiated exit route from the Shareholders Agreement's liquidity rights. Typical multiples range between 3x-4x, but can be higher up to 6x-10x IRRICDA, depending on the opportunity and on the country. Kuali targets returns exceeding 15-20% p.a. in EUR.

Debt investments target returns: The Funded Activity targets debt rates to FISPs between 4 and 8% in Euro after tax (or equivalent in local currency) from country to country. To the maximum extent possible loans will be done in local currency and FX fully hedged to EUR with the use of swaps or forwards.

## Expected holding periods

According to holding period, the senior or subordinated debt with a tenor of either 2-2½ or 3-3½ years, where an option for early repayment will be negotiated so that at year 2 or 3 the investment would be repaid in full (green strategy). The Fund could also consider 2-3 year tenors, with the commitment to extend by 2 or 3 more years if the investor is moving forward with the commitment to advance in its green strategy and the financial performance of the project.

## Role in financing rounds: will the Funded Activity act as a lead, co-led, follower or sole investor

In debt investments, Kuali Fund-GCF normally acts as at least an co-led lender, investing in companies where other high-impact investment firms have already invested. In equity investments, the Funded Activity prefers making co-investments with other carefully chosen investors sharing the same impact principles and vision.

India plays an important role in Kuali Fund because the country shows a robust development steadily maintained through the last years in the FinTech sector even during the pandemic context. The country has the largest population and is one of the most populous countries in the world, which makes it attractive for investment due to an enabling policy environment, a sizeable talent pool, a large unbanked population eager to have financial services at disposal, an improving internet access tendency combined with a digital literacy increase and a growing middle class. The Kuali Fund will be able to contribute to India’s development by increasing the financial sector, both broadening its potential client range and increasing its efficiency by lowering costs of providing services. This will also help in reducing the carbon footprint of the country through increased investment by providing services to people historically unattended by the sector because of social and geographical conditions (spaces which are tricky to access, remote rural areas populated by groups at risk from social exclusion).

Latin America and the Caribbean would benefit from the Indian sector’s maturity avoiding overlooking risks such as over-regulation or under-regulation. The Kuali Fund will be able to contribute to Latin America’s regulation to a new and changeable FinTech contest come handing in a developing market such as the American. Either too lax or too restrictive regulation can imply cost-related challenges. Avoiding them is extremely important for the region, which has a large unbanked population eager to have financial services at disposal. The Kuali Fund will be able to contribute to Latin America and the Caribbean to break their endogenous barriers. The American continent has a well-developed traditional banking sector whose reluctance to embrace this new technology acts as a barrier, also slow data transmission speeds in some areas.

### Hydrological changes:

## Impact on coastal and marine zones:

According to the Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC), the countries suffering the sea level rise most (by number of hectares) would be mostly Brazil and Mexico. However, percentage wise, the countries with the highest vulnerability are El Salvador, Panama, Honduras, and Guatemala.

### Extreme events in coastal zones:

The Caribbean countries are the main ones affected by this type of catastrophe due to water warming in the Caribbean Sea and Mexican golf. In top of the mangroves' solution, Kuali Fund-GCF can help vulnerable people and SMEs living in coastal areas by providing a reliable source of electricity in areas which the grid generally does not reach.

### Solutions on agriculture:

This area will be affected in many different ways depending on the socio-economic and geographical reality of each country as shown previously. Climate change effects (temperature increase, rainfall pattern changes, etc.) can produce several effects such as soil moisture, soil loss or reduction of yields. Kuali Fund-GCF provides solutions to these problems by providing a reliable source of electricity in areas which the grid generally does not reach.

## Traditionally larger Financial Institutions have not served the segment of SMEs and smallholder farmers and, as a result, SMEs in LAC region are facing a finance gap of 900 billion USD. The gap increases by additional USD 745 billion if considering demand from informal SMEs. About 31% of formal SMEs in LAC have access to financial services, while only 28% of informal SMEs do so. In the agricultural sector, lack of information or disparities, the large proportion of informal business practices, high market entry

## B

### Barriers in the available margins, heterogeneity of the market, hurdles to SME growth and productivity and regulatory limitations. Serving efficiently the SME segment requires the adaptation of fit business models, as the credit assessment, risk management, marketing strategies and products are different from more traditional ones.

Based on Cerise SP4 "Green Index", Kuali Fund-GCF will analyze the "green readiness" of the different innovative companies and FSPs and will identify gaps that its investees will need to improve in order they wish to transition towards a more sustainable business model. The Green Index is based on the Cerise SP4 and environmental auditors.

The Green Index has been conceptualized by a MEF Green Inclusive and Climate Smart Finance Advisory Group (GICF-AG)1 and it is moreover aligned with the new Environmental Dimension of the Universal Standards for Social and Environmental Performance Management (USESPM), developed by the joint work of the GICF AG, SP4T and CERISE.

The Green Index will:

* To assess and understand the current environmental performance of the investees,
* To compare its performance with its peers,
* To identify environmental policies, actions and strategies that could be implemented in the investee,
* To track progress on environmental performance over time.
* To distinguish other environmental performance to Acai investors.

The Green Index will determine which innovative companies and FSPs/Companies will be financed. The FSP/Companies that will be financed are determined by their capacity to deliver impact (mitigation, adaptation) in the context of a sustainable business model. As part of this "green readiness" process, potential improvements to be implemented through TAP projects will be identified.

Kuali Fund-GCF will ensure systematic and consistent application of the ESM framework to its investment and lending activities. Activities that are considered as environmentally sensitive or high-risk activities included in the IFC's Exclusion List or with a record of E&S issues will not be considered for financing.

Besides, Kuali Fund-GCF will categorize the investees according to their existing and potential portfolio activity, based on the level of environmental risk (E&R) associated with each activity. The higher the risk, the more exhaustive the evaluation is needed; requiring the entities to establish an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS). In addition, the General Investment team will also conduct an Environmental and Social Due Diligence that ensures such procedures are in place in the potential investees and design an Action Plan to solve any minor issues.

Additionally, Kuali Fund-GCF will tackle the SME and the smallholder farmers segments funding gap in LAC from three different angles:

* Address financial constraints by investing in and transforming small Financial Service Providers to efficiently serve the SME and the smallholder farmers segments -including women-owned SMEs2

1 <https://www.cerise.org/en/our-solutions/index.php?lang=en&tool=green-index#financing-financial-performance-gap>

and women farmers, expected to represent 30% - and to support collaboration between FSFs and FinTechs, leveraging an increasing deployment of open banking solutions by banks and regulators. SME clients will benefit from such collaboration because they will have access to wider and more innovative financial products and services. The TAF will also help SMEs to access a broader range of innovative companies and FSFs' products, risk management systems, processes, people and technology. This will enable them to improve their business performance by developing new or improved products by overcoming main challenges in lending to these two segments (including credit risk assessment, underwriting and technology adoption, capacity building and market literacy). It will also be used to improve the implementation of the Social Performance Management and Environmental systems companies and FSFs.

* Productivity: Financial investments in FinTech companies focused on SME and smallholder farmers lending, improving the productivity and digitalization processes of these beneficiaries while providing them with access to financial services. This will enable SMEs and smallholder farmers to invest more efficiently and effectively. The TAF will also help SMEs and smallholder investors could be established for FSFs and also innovative companies to improve their risk assessment by leveraging on-line SME and smallholder farmers data, while FinTechs can broaden their market reach and access new segments of the population.

* Increasing the access to green and climate finance, so that SMEs and smallholder farmers can contribute more effectively to the transition towards a low-carbon economy and support the green economy and meeting sustainability standards (required by larger firms integrated in global value chains). The TAF will also help SMEs and smallholder farmers to train themselves and others to train also SMEs and smallholder farmers in the economic and financial aspects of renewable energy and energy efficiency projects and to train smallholders to adopt climate smart agricultural practices.

## Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) Structure and Governance

The TAF will be established in parallel with the Funded Activity to provide institution building support to investees in enhancing outreach and reducing risk. It will be set up in parallel to the Funded Activity being a fiduciary Agent. It shall be managed by the TAF Manager, GAWA Capital Partners SGR, which is also the manager of the Funded Activity. The TAF Manager will be supported by the TASC which has a strategic role and will provide overarching guidance to the TAFM. The TAFM shall operate within the guidelines and procedures prescribed by the TASC and in accordance with a Fiduciary Agreement.

The Technical Assistance resources shall be made available through the TAF Manager (hereinafter referred to as "the TAFM") or "TA" or "TAF". Therefore, the TA Facility will be governed by a Fiduciary Agreement between: i) the Donors/Contributors to the TAF (including COFIDES on behalf of GFC and the EU, ACID and the SCiAR); ii) the fiduciary Bank (the fiduciary) and iii) GAWA Capital Partners SGR as TAFM. The Luxembourg Trust Company Law 2003 provides the legal basis for the creation of this type of contracts.

The contributions and/or donations of assets made to the TAF are referred as the Fiduciary Assets. The fiduciary Bank shall open a separate account in its own name in Luxembourg (the Fiduciary Bank) which will act as the holder of the Fiduciary Assets in its capacity as Fiduciary.

10 During the investment period, the SCiAR will also make a one-off contribution of EUR 250.000.
It is common practice that the custodian Bank of the SCiAR is also the fiduciary of the TAF.

The TAF will be expected to reach a size of EUR 5.9 million for Kuafi Fund-GCF (a total of EUR 12.25 for Kuafi Fund) from GCF (EUR 5 million), EU (EUR 0.57 million), FONPRODE (EUR 0.3 million) and Kuafi Fund (EUR 0.04 million). The TAF contribution provided will be ultimately deployed to address key barriers that are expected to impede the success of the innovative companies and FSFs supported by the Funded Companies, in order to achieve the expected outcomes. GAWA Capital will manage the TAF.

The TAF shall apply the contributions in accordance with guidelines (the "TA Guidelines") drafted by the TAF Management Team (TMT) and approved by at least 3 TA Operations Directors (see initial draft in Annex 21), that will refer to TA Policy and TA Procurement Policy, and the TA Annual Plan (TA Operational Project Budget).

The key barriers for TAF and Kuafi Fund-GCF will be guaranteed by (i) the innovative companies and FSFs supported by the Funded Activity will also be a target for the TAF and (ii) the TAF will be GAWA Capital, which is also the manager of Kuafi Fund-GCF.

The TASC shall at any time be constituted by at least three (3) members and a maximum of five (5) members. Each member must be an independent entity per contributor (GCF or EU as representative of GCF) and one independent member ("the Initial Member", and others e.g., COFIDES), with expertise on SMEs/smallholder farmers segments, financial inclusion, market access, and/or other relevant thematic areas, including private sector actors. The Fund manager can attend the TASC meetings but will have no voting rights. The TASC will hold at least 3 meetings a year and will have a strategic role (approval of TA Guidelines). However, the TASC will not be able to make any decision on day-to-day management decisions or other matters that are within the simple majority of its members.

Day-to-day management decisions on TAF deployment will be made by the TAFM. The TAFM will be responsible for implementing the Annex 21 TA Operations Manual, structuring the TA services, managing tender procedures, negotiating, and signing agreements with service providers, coordinating and monitoring TA Projects, drafting monitoring reports and preparing the TA Guidelines

Key barriers:

At the level of innovative companies and FSFs, the following key barriers are expected to be addressed: 

- Understanding of the addressable market: Kuafi Fund-GCF expects to conduct specific country market research to identify key opportunities/clients' needs for innovative companies and FSFs to address.
- Designing financial products that meet clients' demand: supporting innovative companies and FSFs in designing appropriate financial products and building robust partnerships with manufacturers, understanding mitigation and adaptation potential. 
- FSFs able to properly serve SMEs and smallholder farmers segments by providing effective training, capacity building, and other support services.
- Ensure that creditworthy clients are able to receive a loan at affordable rates by:
    - Adapting credit pricing to SMEs and smallholder farmers segments by using data analytics and/or market research.
    - Supporting open-banking solutions and identifying synergies with FSFs in Kuafi's portfolio. 
    - Leveraging digitalization.

At SMEs and smallholder farmers level, the following key barriers are expected be addressed with TA:

## Increase awareness about the potential to increase income and reduce CO2 emissions through new energy-efficient equipment by running workshops/ preparing marketing material

## Areas of Impact

The TAF will support the implementation of new processes that reduce innovative companies and FSPs' environmental footprint. At a result, approximately 55% of this amount is expected to be delivered to FSPs and 45% to innovative companies. The total amount allocated for TA activities is 10%, with 90% allocated to innovative companies and 10% to ecosystemic projects. The remaining amount will be allocated to TA management (for further information, please see Annex 16).

The TAF will also support the following project components with SMEs and smallholder farmers (Activity 8.2.1); Green Readiness projects with FSPs (Activity 8.3.1.1); Social Performance and Environmental Risks Management Systems projects (including gender and SEAR assessments) with FSPs and ICs (Activity 13.1.2); Gender and SEAR assessment with innovative companies (Activity 13.1.3). Business development support with Innovative Companies (Activity 13.1.4); Ecosystemic Projects, stakeholder engagement and gender activities will be supported by the TAF (see Annex 8 for further breakdown among different activities is provided on Annex 4 Budget Plan and the TA Operation Manual).

All these activities together contribute most to the Outcomes 2 (improved resilience in vulnerable people & communities) and 3 (Enhanced green and financial ecosystem opportunities).

Investment readiness will be supported by a TAF investment readiness tool for their Environmental Risk Management System (Activity I.13.2.), to monitor and mitigate climate and agriculture-related risks, aligning it to TFCD and reducing innovative companies and FSPs' environmental footprint. TA will also be used to improve innovative companies' and FSPs' business models (Activity 8.4) and the TAF's investment readiness tool at improving the ability of investors to achieve their social mission and adequately manage Social Risks by following a TFCD approach.

The Green Index 3.0 tool used during due diligence will also be used under the TAF. The tool will perform a "green-readiness" analysis of the innovative companies and FSPs and identify gaps that these institutions need to improve in order to transform to a more sustainable business model.

This tool includes the following parts, to provide a full picture of the environmental opportunity of the potential investment:

- **Qualitative questions** to understand the global environment performance of the potential investor. This will help to benchmark the potential investor against other financial institutions and identify areas of improvement in the potential investor's environmental performance. The qualitative questions will also cover internal/external environmental risk management, and specific actions being taken by the investor to foster green growth.

- **A quantitative part** with detailed data on energy, water, paper, gasoline consumption; number and value of employees; waste generation; and the use of renewable energy and microinsurance products. This will help keep track of the progress in the investors' green activities.

Furthermore, TA will be allocated to innovative companies and FSPs in order to improve their processes and provide a comprehensive product offering to SMEs & smallholder farmers as well as supporting these

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagrams)

Kuafi Fund-GCF expects to build a portfolio of about 12-13 investors in the beneficiaries' countries[1]. Each investment will be on average around EUR 2.5 to 20 million with an average of EUR 8 million[2]. The average investment size is expected to be higher for FSPs than for foreign companies.

As already explained, the following instruments are expected to be used:

- **Debt-like investments** (approximately 60-70% of portfolio) for FSPs with a maturity of 3–10 years, which automatically converts into equity after five years. The investment will be made in local currency and have the option of calling back the capital if certain conditions are not met. All investments will be made in local currency and any foreign exchange risk will be offset through swaps. Innovative companies might also be financed with debt-like instruments.
- **Equity** approximately 30–40% of portfolio for financing innovative companies.

### Main characteristics of equity investments

- **Maturity period**: 5–6 years
- **Growth stage**: Equity investments with a relevant minority stake (less than 20%), a board seat and a clear and/or pre-negotiated exit. Expected holding period: 6–7 years.
The investment period of the Funded Activity is 5 years, and most investments are expected to be deployed over this five-year period. The equity investments will not be used for business expansion of the investors and not for refinancing. Investments will be recorded at Fair Value through Profit or Loss (FVPL), according to IFRS 10 Exception to Consolidation. The implementation period for the TA will be the 10–15 years of activity of Kuafi Fund-GCF.

### Organizational set-up:

As already detailed in section A, Kuafi Fund-GCF's structure encompasses the Kuafi SICAR-EuSEF (the Sicar) and a Special Purpose Vehicle incorporated as a Securitization Vehicle (SV), together "Kuafi Fund"[3], which are two different vehicles with a separate legal set-up[4].

[1] TBC – number could vary depending on the final size of the Funded Activity.
[2] FSP total loan portfolio is an average leverage at 20-25% with own resources and the rest with several local and international financial institutions.
[3] Kuafi Fund will contribute from EU and private resources to finance also investments in 8 countries (Ecuador, Peru, Bolivia, Costa Rica, Nicaragua, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras).
[4] Kuafi SICAR-EuSEF is a fund of independent vehicles. Both will have their respective governing bodies, although FONRIPIDE will only be able to participate in them to a limited extent (observer), given its status as a debt holder and not as an equity investor.

Kuasi S.G.A., SICAR EGEEF: a corporate partnership limited by shares (Société en commandite par actions) qualifying as a société d'investissement en capital à risque (SICAR) pursuant to the Luxembourg 2004 Law and a Social Entrepreneurship Fund (EuSEF) pursuant to the Luxembourg 2013 Law. The SICAR will be subject to the same rules and regulations as risk capital funds for investments in private equity and venture capital. The SICAR is an externally managed Alternative Investment Fund Manager (AIFM). Kuasi S.G.A., as the AIFM, will play a passive role and commit to drawdowns upon requests meeting certain formal requirements.

Kuasi S.G.A. will be incorporated pursuant to Article 10 of the Luxembourg 2004 Law on Securities and Cooperation and Development. This SV, regulated by the Luxembourg securities law, will not be subject to the supervision of the Commission de Surveillance du Secteur Financier (CSSF), but by making pari passu co-investments with the SICAR, its activity will be subject to the CSSF rules. The first loss contribution from EU and GCF, and also the resources in concessional conditions from FONPROIDE will enable to attract private investors' participation, as this catalytic contribution will provide a function of de-risking the private investments.

Kuasi S.G.A. will be managed by LAVIA Capital Partners, and its sole shareholder will be the General Partner of the Kuasi SICAR. Kuasi SV will issue senior notes to FONPROIDE and subordinated notes to Kuasi SICAR, whose participation in the SV will be limited to 10% of FONPROIDE's contribution. Kuasi SV will not be subject to the supervision of the CSSF but by making pari passu co-investments with the Kuasi SICAR, its activity will de facto be subject to the same rules and regulations as risk capital funds for investments in private equity and venture capital under the CSSF. The first loss contribution from EU and GCF, and also the resources in concessional conditions from FONPROIDE will enable to attract private investors' participation, as this catalytic contribution will provide a function of de-risking the private investments.

### Tracking Sharea Methodology and distribution waterfall

### B. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

GCF participation will be essential for Kuali Fund-GCF for several reasons. The funds will be used to support the two sectors in Latin America which are the most vulnerable and potential contributors to reduce CO2 emissions.

SMEs are the backbone of the emerging markets economies, representing the vast majority of businesses. Estimates suggest that small businesses contribute to 60-70% of total industrial pollution in developing countries. The GCF funding will be used to support SMEs in Latin America to gradually include environmental practices in their business processes, rethinking the production model and adapting the use of energy in companies. Making SME green will lower production costs as it is more efficient and sustainable.

The GCF funding will also be used to support the development of new markets and to international value chains which are under increased pressure to mitigate the climate footprint of their operations.

### Positive outlook

Agriculture accounts for 30% of global emissions and farmers are the most vulnerable segment of society to the consequences of climate change. Recent research shows that climate change is already affecting food security in Latin America, where smallholder farmers in the region are experiencing food insecurity and producers of essential products for the region such as maize and coffee are experiencing drops in productivity as a result of climate change.

On a positive look there is an increasing number of nature-based solutions that, in addition to providing substantial mitigation benefits reducing the carbon footprint of SMEs and farmers, are also contributing to building resilience among these vulnerable segments. When a company invests in a mitigation solution it


---
**Page 56**
---

makes its ecosystems more resilient to climate change. Similarly, many of the nature-based adaptation solutions that can be implemented by farmers regenerate the soil producing carbon sequestration.

The Kuali Fund-GCF aims at ensuring that the MSE and agricultural sector can easily implement these solutions by facilitating financing and advisory by MFIs and other financial service providers. These institutions will provide a wide range of services to MSEs, including credit, insurance, technical assistance, and businesses. The Kuali Fund-GCF will help the institutions to make their green transition so that they start offering or expand their lending or channeling of climate mitigation and adaptation solution to their clients.

Apart from investing in MFIs as well as providing financial support to these MFIs, the Kuali Fund-GCF will also invest in firms and insurance companies that are providing professional service and insurance to both MSEs and farmers in innovative ways; reducing operating costs and tapping new clients in neo-geographical areas or in other sectors. The Kuali Fund-GCF will provide a catalytic investment of EUR 12 million for these financial market and will be invested by the Kuali Fund-GCF to start offering climate finance solutions to their clients.

In addition, Kuali Fund-GCF will also invest in innovative companies which produce products and services related to mitigation and adaptation. Some examples include alternative energy sources such as solar roof systems, energy efficient machines, adaptive/integrated water resource management, climate related decision support tools, and cold chain technologies that can help MSEs and farmers adapt to new climate conditions. These solutions are often implemented by climate-tech and ag-tech companies.

The GCF’s contribution to the first-loss tranche will have a catalytic role. GCF investment will provide a tangible signal of commitment from the public sector to private investors, which in turn will attract private investors. This cushion will address perceived risks by the private sector; hence, the proposed structure will allow Kuali Fund-GCF to catalyze private sector participation at scale. Besides, the GCF’s contribution will lower the risk for MFIs and other financial service providers to invest in MSEs and farmers, which will lead to less and less need for current VLR, catalytic capital in the future. To this end, a first-loss tranche of EUR 12 million will be provided by the GCF. The Kuali Fund-GCF’s investment will also provide, as a well-respected institutional investor, enormous added value, boosting confidence and acting as a stamp of approval to private investors.

GCF’s contribution is expected to have a catalytic effect on impact investing and/or Climate finance, giving additional security and boosting environmental awareness in the investing community. GAWA’s latest fund (Huruuma Fund), focused on boosting financial inclusion and strengthening ag-value chains, has received EUR 12 million from GCF. This is the first time that GCF has committed EUR 12 million committed by private investors came from investors who had never invested in impact investing or agricultural finance before. The Kuali Fund-GCF’s investment will have a similar catalytic effect on the impact impact via investments, but built by key catalytic capital injected in the fund thanks to a similar structuring of blended finance.

Accordingly, besides, once financial institutions have undergone the proposed transformation and managed to begin to cater to the SME and smallholder farmers / climate segment, they will continue doing so after Kuali Fund-GCF, creating a systemic shift of capital from financial institutes to SME and smallholder farmers (and other segments) in the long run. This will be an important contribution towards the Fund’s Activity’s viability and will foster the replication of the project among the countries in LAC.

The TAF will be key to significantly increase the impact of the financing provided to the FSPs, supporting their ability to scale up their lending activities and thereby increasing the outreach in terms of reach in scale and depth of SME and smallholder farmers financing and ensuring the sustainability of the project.


## EU total contribution for First Loss investments will be EUR 12 million for Kuali Fund.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 54

## Kuafi Fund-GCF

Kuafi Fund-GCF will have a duration of 10 years, with a possible extension of a maximum of 2 additional years subject to the approval of the Investment Advisory Committee. However, the objective of Kuafi Fund-GCF is to ensure that investors buy into risk-based investments in renewable energy and climate loans beyond the life of Kuafi Fund-GCF.

Kuafi Fund-GCF will return investors' capital with a yield in a form of distributions which will take place as and when the underlying assets generate cash flow. The Fund Manager (GAWA Capital Partners SGEIC) has a strong pipeline[1] in the region and is well positioned to execute on its investment strategy, which includes investments in renewable energy and climate loans beyond the life of each investment, ensuring the financial sustainability of the Funded Activity. The Fund Manager has developed sophisticated financial models which allow for the detailed follow up and management of Kuafi Fund-GCF's portfolio.

The fact that GAWA Capital Partners SGEIC is a private equity fund manager with a strong track record in the robustness of their business models, financial and operating environments, implies that the inability to provide liquidity to investors is not an issue. The ability to provide liquidity to investors is also ensured by the Fund Manager's ability to sell debt financing (including mezzanine transactions) which are self liquidating and may be rolled over once during the investment period.

Debt financing (including mezzanine transactions) is self liquidating and may be rolled over once during the investment period. Debt investments have an average tenor of 6 years, so that at the end of Kuafi Fund-GCF's life, there will still be a significant amount of debt financing outstanding which can be refinanced or sold on to another investor.

Debt investments are typically structured as mezzanine transactions, which in certain cases can be endorsed to another investor. In the case of equity investments, from year 6 onwards the manager starts planning the exit of the investment, having enough time (4 years) to try selling it to another investor or listing it on a stock exchange.

The decision whether to sell an investment is made by the Fund Manager and is not subject to any other party's approval. The decision to sell an investment is a key decision point during investment evaluation. Several options are typically listed in the Shareholder Agreement, including:

- Right to sale on future liquidation
- Initial Public Offers (IPOs)
- Put rights for minority shareholders (the right, but not the obligation, to sell an asset to another party)
- Buy-in rights of the majority shareholder sells his stake, minority holders have the right to join the deal and sell their stake in the same terms and conditions as would apply to the majority shareholder
- Mandatory sale right with drag-along if the majority shareholder sells his stake, minority holders are forced to join the deal).

Any investment will include implementation of gender policies of FSPs and innovative companies, aimed at ensuring non-discrimination and empowering women as employees and entrepreneurs. The specific gender interventions will make investors more inclusive and strengthen women capacities, knowledge, sustainable skills and self-confidence.

[1] Founded in 2009, GAWA Capital is the leading impact investor in Spain. GAWA Capital has directly reached 418,000 low-income households through its investments, fostering their access to financial services and enabling families and communities to improve their living conditions. The company has also indirectly impacted over 3 million people.

- 256,000 families directly affected
- 70% of clients are women
- 50% local clients
- 100% of investments closed
- 52 impact investments, supporting 32 entities

In addition to its investment activities, GAWA has also developed a range of financial products and services that have helped to improve the financial inclusion of low-income households. These include:

- Microfinance loans: Since inception in 2009, GAWA has pioneered the introduction of the impact investing asset class in Spain, having launched several microfinance funds with Mitea Capital and Macroimpact Funds. Each of these funds invests equity and debt directly into microfinance institutions (MFIs) to provide them with capital for their operations.
- Microinsurance: GAWA has also developed a range of financial products that have helped to improve the financial inclusion of low-income households, including microinsurance policies.

To date, GAWA has advised over EUR 204 million, with investments in 18 countries and a positive track record of 5.34% annualized return on equity.

**Kuali Fund-GCF provides capacity building to financial institutions to help them become “greener” and to lend to SMEs and smallholder farmers with the same goal. The main key objective of the project is to develop and strengthen the capacity at bank level to serve SMEs and smallholder farmers, with a specific focus on climate lending, by implementing the following best practice technologies:**

- Innovative underwriting methodologies which make lending to SMEs and smallholder farmers more efficient and effective.
- Financial products aimed at financing assets that promote mitigation and adaptation.
- Internal environmental best practices.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 58

The TAF is intended to cover the following types of capacity building activities:

- **Consultant Services**, i.e., hiring experts and consultants for tailor-made technical assistance for FSP, Innovative companies and SMEs.
- **Training**: evaluate prior experience or training in agriculture and establish induction and training programs for staff of FSP, Innovative Companies and SMEs related to Goods and Services related, i.e., hardware, software and other goods that the beneficiaries may need to implement their projects.
- **Ecosystemic technical assistance (TA)** that will directly benefit the FSFs, Innovative Companies and/or the SMEs smallholder farmers.

Indeed, to maximize the Funded Activity impact in the agriculture sector, the TAF will support projects seeking improvements in infrastructure, value chain-level infrastructure, and other activities that, in addition to tailored TA to investees, could be implemented under a Value Chain Development (VCD) project. The VCD projects will be designed by the FSFs and/or Innovative Companies. Projects of TAF will address specific needs and market failures identified during the investment period of Kuali Funded Activity and indirectly help the Funded Activity key beneficiaries, i.e., producer organizations, agribusiness SMEs and Kuali’s direct investees.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section reviews the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Kuali Fund-GCF aims at transforming Financial Institutions to better serve the unbanked SME and potentially women-owned SMEs and smallholder farmers in accessing climate financing. As a result of better access to financing, SMEs and smallholder farmers will have access to more energy efficient equipment and vehicles, clean energy production such as solar panels or assets that allow entrepreneurs and smallholders to adapt to climate change. The project/programme aims at increasing the number of women-owned enterprises, support women empowerment and in the global fight against climate change, supporting a large share of SMEs and smallholder farmers in LAMIVAT and avoiding 2,970 tCO2 equivalent ton of CO2 equivalent in 10 years expected lifetime.

Adaptation and resilience to climate change will be assessed evaluating the changes in vulnerability of farming communities, the adoption of more sustainable practices, increased adaptive capacity, improved and adaptive capacity. Climate information will be derived using available platforms to set up customized baselines and technology/practice adoption will increment their adaptive capacity.

To this end, Kuali Fund-GCF will support more than 12 FPSs and Innovative companies financed to reach a total of approximately 300,000 beneficiaries. These beneficiaries represent approximately 0.05% of the population of the target countries of Kuali Fund-GCF.

The Funded Activity expects to support smallholder farmers in financing assets that foster adaptation to climate change such as solar panels or farming inputs better adapted to changing weather conditions – assuming a total investment of EUR 2,100 million. FPSs and Innovative companies from Kuali Fund-GCF's funding in FPSs/innovative companies and potential SMEs/smallholder farmers will have greater access to financing, increased competitiveness and improved resilience to climate change. This will lead to the adoption of sustainable practices and an increase in their quality of life. Also, the total population of these countries will benefit from the reduction of poverty and increasing their resilience to the effects of climate change, in line with the commitment of the Paris Agreement.

In addition to the above benefits, the potential impact of the transformation of the financial system in FPSs and innovative companies, SMEs and smallholder farmers towards greener and more innovative models will also be assessed. This will include the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions through the digitalization of beneficiary companies and integrate these final beneficiaries into the value chains thanks to their greater competitiveness, which will contribute to strengthening the sector and the economic activity at large.

Moreover, the flow of climate finance towards smallholders will help filling up their financing gap, resulting in resilience to climate change by this vulnerable population, increase in their productivity and their livelihoods.

Finally, gender targets have been established considering existing data regarding the gender finance gap. Kuali Fund-GCF propose to target at least 30% women-owned SMEs and women smallholder farmers to enhance and expand their participation in innovative projects and participating in capacity building and training programs. Additional activities are described in the gender action plan.

# D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Kuali Fund-GCF will be an innovative investment fund in the region, benefiting from the Fund Manager's long-standing experience in LAC. The benefits that this Fund Activity will bring to the SMEs and the smallholder farmers can be easily replicated in the sector by other smaller or new FSPs once the Funded Activity has divested, as Kuali Fund-GCF’s main objective is to change the trend of business models in the financial institutions investing in SMEs and smallholder farmers. This will be achieved through the technology, appropriate products. After this transformation process, the financial institutions invested by Kuali Fund-GCF will have a strong track record in the climate resilient investments and will have a positive social impact even after the investment is exited and possibly crowding in more Financial Institutions that have not been investors to launch new climate finance initiatives.

In this regard, the contribution of paradigm shift of Kuali Fund-GCF will be channeled through FSPs and intermediaries. The FSPs will implement (i) operational strategies and risk management systems with the support of Kuali Fund-GCF; then (ii) SMEs will reduce their carbon footprint and (iii) smallholder farmers will invest in assets that support climate smart agriculture, increase agricultural productivity, increase the resilience of their enterprises and reduce their carbon footprints. From this point on, Companies and financial companies who implement these strategies and risk management system, they then will benefit from higher productivity, a greater ability to withstand shocks and stressors, and more sustainable business models. This will allow for the transformation of their processes and business modes into a more sustainable and ecological models.

This replicability is directly related to the potential for knowledge sharing and learning. The transformation that Kuali Fund-GCF will make in the target sectors will generate lessons learn for the future development of other FSPs, financial institutions, and governments. This will be possible through close collaboration with partnership from the GCF; this Funded Activity will demonstrate that the technology, processes and products that are being used by Kuali Fund-GCF are not only cost-effective but also have a positive impact on the value chain and in the business structure that is expected to have a high impact on the sector, having the required scale and replicability. The success of Kuali Fund-GCF will be an example for other FSPs and private capital has the potential to support businesses that build climate resilience for smallholders, while also supporting overall growth of SMEs and smallholder farmers sectors. This will allow Kuali Fund-GCF to develop a strong track record in the region and will encourage more financial institutions to invest or continue to invest in more companies that are aligned with the GCF’s impact goal. In this regard, by investing in for- profit enterprises, Kuali Fund-GCF will also be able to generate returns on its investments. This will allow the enterprises that continue to operate through internal profits and follow-on investment capital even after the Funded Activity has divested. The expected success of Kuali Fund-GCF, due to an experienced Fund Manager, will have a significant impact in the region by creating more opportunities for other FSPs and SME/smallholder farmers, will build further expertise and a strong track-record in the climate resilient investments. This will allow Kuali Fund-GCF or other investors in the region to raise more money in the future and make more investments.

Kuali Fund-GCF’s investments will develop, prove and scale innovative inclusive finance, reduce the impact of climate shocks on the incomes of SMEs and smallholder farmers. Success stories from such investments will be shared with other FSPs and financial institutions that are interested to invest or continue to compete, which ultimately will serve to increase the scale and impact of the final beneficiaries.

In this context, Kuali Fund-GCF will develop its investments within an adequate environmental and governance framework, in order to ensure that they are sustainable and can be transferred to the invested entity. The potential investments will have significant impact within the specific identified countries. Such investments will create employment opportunities, local institutional knowledge that can be shared within

# D3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

SMEs are the backbone of the emerging markets economies and contribute 60-70% of total industrial pollution. SMEs need to include environmental practices in their processes, which will lower production costs and increase international value chains. On the other hand, the agricultural sector is responsible for 30% of global emissions and is one of the most vulnerable sectors to climate change. 32% of smallholder farmers in LAC are experiencing food insecurity and producers of maize and beans are facing a decline in yields.

From the perspective of mitigation measures, preventing and reducing the emission of greenhouse gases into the atmosphere will make the impacts of climate change less severe. Kufi-GCF Fund will seek to reduce human interference with the climate system in order to ensure that food production is not threatened and to encourage sustainable development. The Fund will also support the implementation of adaptation strategies to support climate mitigation. Several initiatives are being developed to cut greenhouse gas emissions, and to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change. In addition to supporting the adaptation to climate change side, it is really important to invest in supporting farmers and communities to better adapt to climate change. This can be done by providing them with access to information, tools and services for growing harder yields and raising livestock. This includes helping farmers diversify their crops and redesign their farmland to improve their productivity and protect the soil, so they are better equipped to withstand food insecurity.

Kufi-GCF Fund will be deeply committed to creating sustainable development in developing countries, as evidenced by previous activities and track record of both the Fund Manager and the Accredited Entity. The Frontier Activities Component (FAC) is a key component with high potential for promoting innovation and growth. The connection with these local partners will create innovative value-added processes that provide a sustainable and durable model for the future. Each investment decision will be made on the basis of a triple bottom line methodology, considering the economic, environmental and social sustainability view.

- From a global perspective, Kufi-GCF Fund will also promote greater competitiveness in the target sectors, a modernization of the regional financial sector and the contribution to economic growth.
- From an economic perspective, the benefits identified are: increase in the number of jobs (over 460,000), increased access to credit for SMEs and smallholder farmers that have not traditionally been supported, access to financing of SMEs and smaller farms that have not traditionally been supported, the strengthening of the financial sector, the reduction of the gap between traditional banking and these FSPs and innovative companies, the synergies that can be found with traditional banking and the increase in purchasing power of smallholder farmers. The Fund will also contribute to the creation of new jobs and a significant increase in agricultural resilience through climate adaptation, which will result in an increase in agricultural productivity.
- From a social perspective, as a result of better financing, SMEs and smallholder farmers will be able to grow, increasingly engaging with international value chains, create more quality jobs and increasing their income. The Fund will also contribute to the creation of new jobs and a significant increase in livelihood. The increase in smallholders' productivity will result in higher household income for this population. This will provide SMEs with greater access to credit and better financial services that will provide the FSPs/innovative companies and final beneficiaries with a greater knowledge and sustainable skills that will be useful after the Funded Activity's investment. These processes will

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 62

## D

### N.D. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The climate gap in the LAC region is USD 2.3 billion. Large financial institutions in the region have been channeling green lending towards the financing of large renewable energy projects, large industrial power plants and large efficient building construction.

However, there are two segments that are left out: SMEs and smallholder farmers. These institutions are able to mobilize climate finance to markets underserved by large banks. Nevertheless, capital doesn’t flow into these two segments naturally. As already mentioned, according to the IMF Green Finance Assessment, 15% of firms in LAC are considered “underfunded” (i.e., they have less than USD 900 billion – 3x the current level of SME lending). Financing demand increases USD 745 billion if informal and smallholder farmers are included. In addition, 28% of SMEs in Latin America have unfilled financing needs. Additionally, smallholders’ finance gap in the region is USD 20 billion.

Therefore, these smaller institutions need to transform their risk management systems, processes, technology, staff and products to be ready to mobilize green and climate products for SMEs and smallholder farmers.

Given the strong adoption of sustainability values among multinationals, the SMEs and the smallholder farmers that are proactive on sustainability issues and transparency are in a better position to gain access to new markets. They can also benefit from the increasing number of Green Bond Issuances by Multinational Companies and many FSPs sector with innovative and ecological practices that will transfer this new knowledge to their supply chains. This will allow SMEs and smallholder farmers to increase their competitiveness in the market in a sustainable way. This may lead to an increase in opportunities for SMEs and smallholder farmers in global value chains.

The Fund-GCF will support beneficiaries by increasing their financial resources to build resilience against climate change (e.g., by improving irrigation systems, buying more suitable seeds, etc.). Smallholder farmers are often more vulnerable to impacts of climate change, especially women farmers. This population will need to rapidly adapt to new climate conditions by adopting climate

The lack of access to new equipment such as biodigesters and interesting degraded areas. KfW Fund-GCF supports is aligned with the political strategy of the LAC region countries, as evidenced by their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) targets submitted under the Paris Agreement. KfW Fund-GCF will contribute to climate change mitigation goal and will provide technical assistance in the areas of renewable energy, energy efficiency, FSPs, and the promotion of green and smallholder farmers in the target countries. Every targeted country* should promote the transformation of the "food system" with greater practices and the flow of green and climate products to SMEs and smallholder farmers.

COFIDES, as an international private-public company and as a European Development Financial Institution (EDFI), is highly committed to various International Climate Change mitigation and adaptation initiatives in LAC countries. Over years, COFIDES has worked with various public and private sector organizations in sustainable development, and climate change projects has also built a significant experience in supporting the implementation of climate change projects in Latin America through its network of local institutions based in target countries. Moreover, the Fund Manager's previous experience and its know-how in development and sustainability projects in Latin America, such as Huruma, will be a key factor in the potential success of Fund-GCF.

### BRAZIL

- **The National Policy on Climate Change (2009) - PNMC**, by means of Law no 12.187/09: In accordance with this law, Brazil will pursue voluntary actions for the mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions with a view to achieving the targets set in Article 36 of the Kyoto Protocol. The PNMC is implemented through several action and sectoral plans for the mitigation and adaptation to climate change, in compliance with the principles and provisions of the Convention: Action Plan on Deforestation Control and Forest Burning in the Amazon (PPCDAm in the Portuguese acronym).
- **Action Plan on Prevention and Control of Deforestation and Burning in Gerrado (PPCerrado in the Portuguese acronym).**
- **The Ten Year Energy Expansion Plan (PDE in the Portuguese acronym)**
- **Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change for a Low Carbon Emission Agriculture (ABIC Plan in the Portuguese acronym).**
- **Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change for Biodiversity.**
- **Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change for the Consolidation of a Low Carbon Emission Economy in the Manufacturing Industry (Industry Plan).**
- **Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change for the Low Carbon Emission Mining Sector.**
- **Sectoral Plan on Transport and Urban Mobility Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change (PSTM in the Portuguese acronym).**
- **Health Sector Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change (PSMAC-Saúde in the Portuguese acronym).**

Several components of these plans, such as the use of renewable energies and the fight against deforestation, are also essential parts of the Brazilian sustainable development strategy. Submitted to the Conference of Parties (COP) of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change provides that the country will adopt voluntary actions to mitigate greenhouse gas emissions with a view to achieving the targets set in Article 36 of the Kyoto Protocol, aiming for 38% by 2020. The goals achieved are recognized, including by non-governmental entities, as one of the most ambitious commitments undertaken by a single country to fight climate change.

### COLOMBIA

- **The National Policy on Climate Change - Plan Nacional de Adaptación al Cambio Climático (PNAACC):** aims to incorporate climate change management into public and private decisions to advance in a climate-resilient and low-carbon development path that reduces the risks of climate change for all Colombian citizens, as well as for productive activities for the country to be carbon neutral.
- **The Colombian Low-Carbon Development Strategy (ECCRC):** was launched in February 2012 as a tool to deliver the objectives of the National Development Plan for 2014 and the NDC/UNFCCC Document on Climate Change by promoting efficient low-carbon growth.
- **National Disaster Risk Management Plan and the Development Strategy:** for the period 2015–2030 (DSSNDR), which sets out the actions of the State and civil society regarding risk awareness, risk reduction and disaster management in compliance with the National Policy on Climate Change. The DSSNDR also contributes to people's safety, welfare, quality of life and the sustainable development of the country.
- **2017-2022 Indicative Action Plan of the Rational and Efficient Energy Use Programme (PRIURE):** defines objectives and targets for energy efficiency, actions and sectoral measures and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 65

## Mexico

- General Law on Climate Change in 2012: Specifies different responsibilities of the Federation.
- Mexico's pledge under Copenhagen Accord: In terms of committing the country to an emissions reduction target of 30% below Business As Usual (BAU) by 2020, subject to the availability of financial resources and technology transfer, as well as 50% GHG emissions reduction by 2050 compared with BAU.
- National Climate Change Policy: Includes a 10-20 year outlook for achieving climate change goals. Sets a target of 30% GHG emissions reductions from the base year.

## Special Programme on Climate Change - Programa Especial de Cambio Climático (PECC): Aims to reduce vulnerability and increase adaptive capacity, together with medium-term goals for adaptation and mitigation.
- Incorporates the planning for each administration considering social, productive, and environmental aspects.

## State Climate Change Programs: Focuses on the planning of each state, considering their specific competences, resources, and climate state level regulations.

### 2008 Laws for Sustainable Energy Use and for the Use of Renewable Energies and Funding the Energy Sector: Encourage and regulate renewable energy and low-carbon electricity.

### 2002 General Law for Sustainable Forest Development: Promotes a variety of initiatives to reduce deforestation and forest degradation.

## Panama

- National Climate Change Strategy: One of the lines of actions leading to meet this objective is to reduce vulnerability of the communities with the highest exposure to adverse climate change effects through financial instruments and specific national funds for adaptation to climate change.
- Nationally Determined Contribution to the Mitigation of Climate Change (NDC): Before the UNFCCC Conference in 2015, Panama will show its intention to decrease its vulnerability in the face of adverse effects of climate change by implementing specific adaptation measures.

### Governmental Strategic Plan (PSG) for 2015-2018 priorities: Among other measures, to consider the impacts of climate change and its effects as a fundamental focus of the governmental action, by stopping deforestation and restoring the vegetative cover to prevent desertification and by reducing the country's vulnerability through the development and application of adaptation and mitigation measures.

### Integrated Development Plan for Aboriginal Peoples of Panama: Promotes actions related to public participation in human development plans; conflict prevention; poverty reduction; access to information, among others.
- 2008 Biodiversity National Policy Strategy and Action Plan: Integrates the group of 7 national policies of the Participative Strategic Plan, as well as the Climate Change Policy.

**Page 70**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 66

## EL SALVADOR:
- **Updated Nationally Determined Contributions 2021:** The document outlines a set of actions that will allow El Salvador to have a consistent strategy in order to face the adverse effects of climate change. These include three strategic areas: i) Ecosystems recuperation through social inclusion, ii) Highly degraded lands recovery through agroforestry systems and sustainable agriculture and iii) Synergic development of physical and natural infrastructure.
- **El Salvador Sustainable Vision Plan:** Published in 2018, this Plan focuses on identifying paths to create economic opportunities for the population. The implementation of the plan has followed consist in four thematic axes: i) Strengthening institutions regarding sustainability ii) Transforming productive sectors to be more competitive iii) Enhance sustainability education iv) Holistic approach to development in terms of climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction.

## GUATEMALA:
- **Nationally Determined Contributions Guatemala 2021:** This document synthesizes the country’s context in order to understand why and how Guatemala aims to develop its sustainable development strategy. The document, after being updated, gives special emphasis to four specific sectors in order to generate mitigation impacts: i) Land use change of land use and silviculture, ii) Energy iii) Agriculture iv) Forests. It also provides an adaptation component aligned with the national adaptation plan (PANCC).
- **National Adaptation Plan (PANCC):** This Plan presents the different strategic directions Guatemala wants to develop in order to implement its climate change strategy. The National and local governments and institutions should take into account to continue the risk intrinsic in the climate change taking the IPCC’s scenarios as a reference. The plan is divided into three main sections: i) Vulnerability of vulnerable people protection, it focuses in the following six sectors: i) Coastal zones ii) Agriculture, farming and food security iii) Forestry resources, ecosystems and protected areas iv) Hydro resources v) Water vi) Urban areas.
- **Adaptation, Mitigation and Vulnerability Reduction Regulatory Law:** Law primarily designed to fight climate change. It also guarantees the correct procedures to develop the PANCC and the NDC strategies.

## HONDURAS:
- **Updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC 2021):** The document has incorporated five key elements in guide to the work on it: i) Social inclusion ii) Greenhouse gases mitigation iii) Climate Change Adaptation iv) Financing v) Report and verification mechanisms. The energy and the agriculture sectors are the two fundamental areas in which the country wants to reduce its emissions.
- **NAMA:** It has low carbon intensity, high social benefits and is a good example of the rural sectors in Honduras and the necessity of a quick transition to a sustainability path order to stabilize the climate change.
- **Honduras Climate agenda:** Published in 2016, it provides an institutional and programmatic framework to develop the National Adaptation Plan and the National Mitigation Plans.

## D

will see that commitment risk reduced, so the proposed structure of Kuafi Fund-GCF will allow catalyzing private sector participation. The de-risking resources will represent 22.2% of the total investment.

Concerning the return of capital to First Loss Shares, GCF would be integrated into Class E Shares and will receive 10% in proportion to their Capital Contributions until distributions equal four percent (4%) per annum above their capital committed to Kuafi Fund-GCF, compounded annually, on their aggregate Capital Contribution.

The minimum concessionality of GCF resources is justified as it is the necessary level of leverage for the project. It also ensures that the project will be viable and that there are adequate returns for institutional investors. Likewise, it also ensures the viability of the project and ensures an adequate return for institutional investors.

Moreover, the Portfolio Impact Score methodology designed by the Fund Managers fosters a positive environmental and social performance, since a good performance in this field within the global SME and smallholder farmers sector is associated with lower default rates.

According to the available technologies and practices, the Funded Activity will use best of art impact management techniques, by supporting banks to implement best practices in terms of integrating technology in their business model, such as:

- Open banking. Kuafi Fund-GCF will ease the banking transactions for the clients.
- Synergies between FSPs and traditional banking
- Innovative credit rating methodology leveraging machine learning.

Fostering banks to lend money to SMEs and smallholder farmers so they can buy the best energy efficient equipment

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 93

## Kufri-GCF represents a significant initiative to address current environmental and climate challenges. With the aim of ensuring effective implementation and transparency in project implementation, a comprehensive Monitoring Plan has been developed to span the entirety of the Fund's lifespan.

This plan not only seeks to fulfill regulatory obligations but also aims to maximize the positive impact of the Funded Activity.

### Annual ex-ast third party verifications:

These verifications will be carried out by a third-party validation and verification body. The Programme will appoint a third-party agency to verify the GHG emissions reductions generated by Kufri-GCF investments. By conducting these independent verifications, Kufri will be able to report GHG reductions to GCF in an open, transparent, and consistent manner during the project implementation.

### Monitoring:

The plan encompasses a range of monitoring activities designed to ensure the achievement of funded activities. Examples of monitoring activities include the assessment of key performance indicators (KPIs) that measure progress in implementing each project. Periodic reviews of progress reports projected by project stakeholders. On-site visits to verify the status of projects and address any outstanding issues.

Additionally, the AE will undertake the following requirements as monitoring, reporting, and evaluation covenants:

- **Contractually require, monitor and enforce** that the FSPs receiving funding under the Funded Activity apply such amounts towards meeting the agreed portfolio targets described in the paragraph below.
- **Ensure that any capital call or a disbursement (other than a capital call or a disbursement under the relevant debt instrument) or otherwise from Kufri GCF to an FSP is based on evidence of the FSP having met the agreed portfolio targets during the Ramp-up period, where such FSPs are subject to monitoring and reporting covenants.**
- **Contractually require, monitor and enforce** such requirement that the verification of targets shall be based on annual sub-loan reporting to the Kufri Fund by each FSP and certified by an independent verifier, which will inform the Kufri Fund GCF on the type (number and volume) of loans disbursed and the corresponding emissions reductions achieved.
- **Ensure** that the targets referred to in paragraph above are verified by an independent verifier based on terms of reference and the approach agreed with the Accredited Entity and the Secretariat.

### Impact Assessment:

Impact assessment will be an integral component of our monitoring plan. Examples of activities include: Analysis of collected data to assess the real impact of interventions in terms of emission reduction and social benefits; Surveys and stakeholder consultations to gather valuable feedback on perceived impact at the local level; Comprehensive assessment reports are to be shared with all stakeholders, fostering transparency and accountability.


---
**Page 98**
---

The AE Fund will monitor the EE’s (General Partner or GAWA) in two different scopes: (i) ESG performance and compliance, and (ii) impact reporting both on an annual basis and by the corresponding evaluations. Furthermore, the General Partner is available on an on-going basis for any questions the AE may have regarding Kuafi-GCF assessment and impact reporting/methodologies.

The value of the EE’s impact indicators should be based on reliable source documents, such as latest audited annual reports or consultant reports. If none of those are available, then data coming directly from client may be used. The information will be referred for verification purposes to the original source document. For the ESG performance and compliance, GAWA will be responsible for monitoring the EE’s performance on an ongoing basis for the rest of the fund life. To verify data, Partnerships for Development (area in CDFBES in charge of the GCF accreditation and identification, analysis, monitoring, and reporting) will perform a verification process by cross-checking the information with other sources such as the finance department and internal audit department. For measuring the impact data correctly, GAWA will develop an impact measurement tool to enable portfolio companies measure its GHG emission reduction; enhancing capacity of portfolio companies to measure climate impact. GAWA will be in charge also of all the verification processes.

GAWA Capital is focusing not only on measuring its environmental and social impact, but also to actively managing the investments in order to maximize the potential environmental and social impact. To do so, the Fund Manager has integrated a social performance management system to the investment process.

The Audited Score will be measured at the beginning of each investment during the social and environmental due diligence. It will be measured and monitored on a quarterly basis. After the end of each investment, the Investors Advisory Committee will select an independent social auditor that will measure the Audited Impact Score. An Audited Impact Score will be averaged as the average of all the investor’s Audited Impact Score. This Audited Impact Score will be reported to Shareholders.

The Fund Manager shall report to Shareholders on a quarterly and on an annual basis.

The quarterly reports shall be delivered to Shareholders 45 days after the end of each quarter and shall include, at least, the following information:

- NAV of the Fund Activity by share class and unaudited financial statements.
- Economic and markets information of the invested countries
- Information about the assets portfolio and breakdown by geographies
- Financial performance of investors as well as ESG metrics and gender metrics (including, if available, the reporting indicators).
- Impact score evolution
