**Page 99**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 95

- Information about the investments in pipeline.
- Expected capital drawdowns and distributions to Shareholders during the following quarter.
- Capital invested, capital to be called and total capital committed.

On an annual basis, the Fund Manager shall deliver to the Shareholders:
- Audited annual accounts of Kuali (including Kuali to FRS) and the auditor's report
- Main milestones achieved during the year and expected achievements for the following year.
- EAS Report including gender issues and GAP implementation.
- TAF Annual Report.

The aggregated quarterly and annual reports are constructed by the sum of individual reports at the investees level. Data will be captured and recorded through the LEVELE software a tool that streamlines data collection, portfolio monitoring, analytics, valuation, and reporting. At the Due Diligence and initial investment phases (sometimes with the help of dedicated consultants that will guide the institution) specific Climate lending eligibility requirements, CO2 baselines, CO2 mitigation calculations, Adaptation baselines and all expected targets will be embedded in the software.

When generating pipeline, GAKA always seeks investments with the potential of servicing the SMEs and the smallest farmers' groups and with willingness to become "green" opportunities as potential investors are screened for their environmental performance. The Fund is committed to improve their environmental practices.

External evaluations will assess twice (mid-term [year 5] and final [year 10]) progress across each of the three paradigm shift dimensions (scale, replicability, sustainability). Assessments will be scored based following the IRMF and supported by a qualitative, narrative assessment. Enabling environment scorecard assessments for the interim and final evaluations will also be reported (core indicators 6, 7 and 8). A brief qualitative report of the progress against the selected enabling environment indicators will also be provided whenever possible.

The TAF Manager shall report to the TA Steering Committee (TASC) on a quarterly and on an annual basis. The governing documents of the TAF shall determine the specific contents of the quarterly and annual reports.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### Political instability (Transfer restriction, expropriation, currency convertibility, war and civil disturbance, economic turnarounds)

#### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Local | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Political instability (Transfer restriction, expropriation, currency convertibility, war and civil disturbance, economic turnarounds).

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The top-down due diligence approach starts with country selection. Kuafi Fund-GCF will select target countries analyzing the political, macroeconomic, and social factors and the risk of political intervention in the financial system.

- **Political intervention in the financial sector** can bring new regulation that may hinder the development funding mission and more specifically, the SMEs and the smallholder farmers.
  - Before investing in any country, GAWA Conducts a detailed analysis and due diligence of the political, economic, social, and environmental factors. This includes the risk of expropriation, transfer restriction, and convertibility risk. Part of the analytical review entails: (i) discussions with sovereign rating analysts; (ii) meetings with local government officials, including central bank governors, finance ministers, and embassy officials or other relevant third parties (including government bodies). Emerging economies are more likely to have a higher level of political intervention in financial sector regulation and related deficits which, as a result, can negatively affect the financial and agricultural sectors. Commodity prices and energy impact the economy and further exacerbate currency volatility.
  - This risk is mitigated in two ways:
    - **Top-down investment approach**, picking countries which are less likely to suffer macroeconomic events or political instability. The country selection process will be completed before any investment is made out before making an investment.
    - **Picking companies that have the financial strength to be resilient to a potential shock**. For example, companies with strong balance sheets capitalized and have demonstrated the ability to raise capital in difficult times (e.g., owned by shareholders that can inject more capital if needed or which are profitable enough to raise capital internally).

- Concerning foreign exchange risk, thanks to its partnership with hedging providers, Kuafi Fund-GCF can lend in current currency and hedge EUR for all debt investments. In equity investments, where hedging is not possible, the fund will take into account the potential impact of foreign exchange risk as part of consideration of the historical performance of the currency against the EUR and after a thorough analysis of the country’s economic outlook.

- Another risk has been identified regarding possible economic turnarounds due to global fluctuations or specific crisis like COVID-19. At a lower level, credit risk would also be related to specific risks linked to the stability of the financial system in the countries where investments are made.


---
**Page 102**
---

## Selected Risk: 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Medium | Medium |

### Description
- Specific risks linked to the SMEs and smallholder farmers sectors.
- Credit Financial intermediary risk.

### Mitigation Measures
Kusi Fund-GCF will seek investments with a low risk profile activity in order to create a portfolio with an adequate quality, according to the experience and credit record of the Fund Manager and the accredited entity in developing markets. There will be also a diversification policy in the portfolio investments in order to reduce the concentration risks. The Kusi Fund-GCF will have a strict selection process (and the Fund Manager expertise) in the SMEs and smallholder farmers' adaptation process.

Credit risk is extensively analyzed during the on-site due diligence. In addition to this, there will be a quarterly monitoring of the portfolio quality. If any credit risk arises from the investments made by Kusi Fund-GCF or if a portfolio quality worsens or any covenant is broken. If the situation persists, an additional on-site due diligence may be arranged to visit affected branches and assess the situation.

## Selected Risk: 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fiduciary | Medium | Medium |

### Description
- Money laundering.
- Terrorism financing.
- Prohibited Practices.
- Sanction lists.

### Mitigation Measures
No activities will be undertaken in any UNSC sanctions regimes. No individual or entity that is listed on any UNSC sanctions list will be involved in any manner with the project or its activities, either as a counterpart, implementer or beneficiary.

There is no intention to distribute or disburse to beneficiaries, either directly or indirectly, cash, vouchers, commodities or other forms of aid.

An integrity channel has been set up to enabling the report of complaints and allegations of impropriety, wrong-doing or other related issues to the projects or its activities. The channel is publicly available (both in Spanish and English) at www.greenclimatefund.org.

Regarding jurisdictions that are listed by the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) such as Panama, a reinforced due diligence will be carried out, in line with the requirements of COPIDES/AMLODIFC procedure. The legal entities declared by the potential investors and the identification of the holder will be closely looked at.

# Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

## Description

- **Social and Environmental risks:** SMEs and smallholder farmers specific risks (i.e. Client Protection Principles, financial consumers' rights) including implementation and operational risks.
- **Others:**


## Mitigation Measures

The due diligence will include a social and an environmental risk analysis that will cover SMEs and smallholder farmers specific risks (i.e. Client Protection Principles, financial consumers' rights) including implementation and operational risks.

Regarding the implementation risk, an adequate and thorough selection process for FSPs and their employees would mitigate the potential lack of experience in managing direct operations with Finnish companies.


The operational and fraud risk is mitigated via field audits performed during the on-site due diligences, involving both the head office and the local branch offices. The audit will be conducted by an independent auditor.

Regarding the asset-liability management, it will be mitigated by an initial ALM analysis and the corresponding monitoring of the investor's liquidity position.


For corruption related risk, there will be background checks on the key individuals of the FSP and innovative companies, including the OFAC list, analyzing the existing shareholders and their affiliations and monitoring the transactional activity of the FSPs and innovative companies.

Regarding the asset-liability management, it will be mitigated by an initial ALM analysis and the corresponding monitoring of the investor's liquidity position.


For the operational risk, a thorough due diligence has been identified, which will be mitigated by the support on technology use, both in the field and in the head office, recommending potential technology vendors and providing training to FSPs. The TA will also act as a strong mitigant of some of these risks. TA will be used to help investors in improving aspects such as technology adoption or product improvements.


Finally, regarding the loan cycle of the investees, it is important for FSPs to adjust their sourcing requirements to the cash model of their loan products' cycles. In the same line, it is essential to adapt their loan products to the business cycle of their clients' productive activities (i.e. longer term for agricultural loans). The TA will provide guidance on the appropriate financial models of invested FSPs and may advise the management in achieving the optimal funding structure.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### RISK CATEGORY
Kuali Fund-GCF (and Kuali Fund) is categorized as I-2, though investments mostly will be rated as I-3. The Fund activity is expected to finance or invest in innovative companies with exposure to high Environmental and Social (E&S) risks and/or exposure to IFC standards. FSPs are also expected to bring High Green Climate Fund EAS Standards in their Environmental and Management Systems (ESMS). For Innovative Companies (IC), the risk associated is C, while for TA clients, the risk associated is B.

#### ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT INSTRUMENTS
GAWA has developed an operational ESMS according to the Green Climate Fund EAS Standards. (See Annex 6). It will be applied into Kuali Fund-GCF’s own operation process and is focused on the E&S assessment of the FSPs’ innovative companies. The risk-based approach in the E&S due diligence and monitoring of the clients will be based on the project life cycle and will comprise the following:

##### STEP 1: SCREENING AND CATEGORIZATION
Kuali Fund-GCF Portfolio and the expected project activities will be screened by the investment analyst against IFC exclusion list to ensure no activity on this list will be financed. The E&S Officer will carry out the EAS risk categorization according to the ICF Categorization List.

##### Kuali Fund-GCF’s investments in FSPs will normally fall into the I-3 category (low risk) and the I-2 category (medium risk). As for Innovative Companies (IC), the risk associated is C or B, rated according CIFG Risk Categories. The E&S Officer will carry out the EAS categorization of the FSPs’ innovative companies that fall on the A category for ICs or 1-I category for FSPs will be screened out and will not be considered for further analysis and investment.

The Investment Officer will write down in the Preliminary Memorandum the conformance with the technical requirements. The Officer will include the key EAS issues, the proposed investee’s E&S risk category, the proposed scope of the ESMS and any anticipated E&S requirements.

##### STEP 2: ESD AND SEED DUE DILIGENCE (ESDD)
During the due diligence, Kuali Fund-GCF’s team will gain an insight into the nature and scope of the FSPs’ innovative companies. The Environmental and Social Due Diligence is conducted in parallel with the business due diligence process when evaluating a potential investment.

A ESDD due diligence and an on-site visit is conducted in parallel with the business due diligence process when evaluating a potential investee. This ESDD will be carried out by the investment team in collaboration with the E&S Officer.

The level of ESD actions and requirements depend on the E&S risk of a potential FSP’s innovative companies. In all cases, the management of the E&S risks-and the EAS risks associated to its investments-of an innovative company will be assessed. An Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) will be established in order to strengthen the ESMS of the FSP Innovative Company and it will have

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 101

## To be executed along the loan tenure. In this stage, the TA could be used to address the ESAP (please refer to Annex 6).

### A particular case within social risks is SEAH risk management. The ESDD will include an assessment of the situation and the need to include actions and requirements from training, zero-tolerance of SEAH Guidance (including awareness raising), and other measures (such as involving women in decision-making others) in a survivor-centered and gender-responsive manner. The objective will be avoided, and where avoidance is impossible, mitigate the risks of SEAH to people impacted by Kuali Fund-GCF.

Despite the fact that non-compliance with the above-mentioned requirements may result in the project rated low-medium due to the AE experience in microfinance and MSE investments (according to UN criteria “UN Protocol on Protection of Sexual Exploitation and abuse involving IP, 2019” for considering SEAH high-risk), it is important that the Fund GCF does not have any direct control over the implementation of these actions and involve neither the Fund Activity management company nor the FSPs and ICs having direct contact with the beneficiaries. Therefore, the Fund GCF will only be responsible for ensuring that such actions take place in countries where sexual and gender-based violence is prevalent (therefore medium rate is present).

It is required that every single subproject/investee company (FSP or IC) has a SEAH risk diagnosis and a mitigation plan in place. The ESDD will include an assessment of the situation and need to include actions from SEAH risk management, which will be evaluated by the GCF’s Secretariat.

### SEAH risk management is at first being systematized and understood within the corporate world. This implies that for every single Kuali Fund-GCF as a pilot project in this program, the first measure that will necessarily have to be established is a specific staff training plan with a top-down flow (Kuali Fund-GCF → FSPs/Fintech companies/investors). It will cover:

1. The underlying company will only acquire the necessary knowledge and skills in this area, including general aspects and country specifics (i.e.) adopt an explicit commitment to combat the SEAH and in favor of zero-tolerance of SEAH, and it has to be reflected in a code of conduct and a mitigation plan.
2. The EAS officer will include in the contracts with the underlying entities, clauses of commitment to Kuali Fund-GCF’s SEAH risk management policy and its implementation (including training), which may be the same as that of Kuali Fund for the better centralization of data and its subsequent evaluation by the GCF Secretariat.
3. Within this contractual framework, the underlying companies of Kuali Fund-GCF shall undertake to receive training in the field of SEAH, to increase their capacities transversally in the organization and to appoint a specific person (or persons) responsible for the implementation of these actions (Resource Officer).
4. The EAS will monitor and monitoring all these acts: Kuali Fund-GCF’s commitment and capacity; incorporation of clauses in contracts with underlying companies; training and assignment of management of underlying companies, aggregate and integrated monitoring by the Fund Activity of all these issues in an annual report that will reflect actions taken, incidents, resolution of the same and lessons learned.

The management system shall not only be preventive to avoid SEAH but include survivor-centered and gender-responsive measures.

Additionally, the GRM of each underlying entity will be adopted not only for EAS claims but also receive SEAH complaints in an anonymous way. Kuali Fund-GCF will be informed quarterly about SEAH incidents and other relevant information that will be processed and incorporated as annual EAS report to the AE.

Besides, the AE will also be able to view all received SEAH complaints in its Program Index. Those complaints will be evaluated by the AE Sustainability and Impact Division

## Kufi Fund will use several tools/guidelines to support this process: IFC Performance Standards, IFC EHS Guidelines (including GRI with Green Index model and Global Map Supply Chain Risks in Agro-Commodity Production), UN SEAH Policies and Protocols (UN Position on Allegations of Sexual Exploitation and abuse involving (P), among others.

The result of the ESDD will be documented in an ESDD Report which will include all the strengths and weaknesses identified by the FSP/innovative company, in addition to the areas identified that could be improved with TA interventions.

### STEP 3: E&S Conditions of Investments

The Investment officer and director of the Fund Manager will negotiate and agree with the FSP/innovative company on the ESDD conditions as part of the investment sheet and investment agreement. These consist of standard E&S terms applicable to all Fund investments, and specific conditions identified during the ESDD of a proposed investment (ESPD with SEAH requirements will be included in the legal document).

The Funded Activity's Fund Manager should ensure that any conditions of investment tied to disbursement of funds, as specified in the investment agreement, are met before disbursing funds.

### STEP 4: TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE

The ESDD will help identify potential TA projects with regard to the green readiness, E&S risks management and gender approach integration of FSPs and Innovative companies.

Examples of Green Readiness projects:
- Implementation of green products directed to the MSME or smallholder farmers such as loans to finance more efficient new machinery or equipment or climate smart agriculture loans.
- Training people on climate finance, including product specialists, risk officers, loan officers and man. For more details about the Technical Assistance Facility and its use please refer to the Kufi Fund TA Operations Manual.

### STEP 5: MONITORING AND REPORTING

The Investment officer and E&S Officer should actively supervise and monitor compliance with the E&S norms included in investment agreements and the E&S performance of each of its portfolios investments. For this purpose, FSP/innovative companies will fill out an E&S Performance Report on a yearly basis. It will include at least the following:

- Portfolio breakdown by industry sectors, E&S risk category and product lines, and sample ESD reports.
- Cases of non-compliance with E&S requirements
- Serious E&S accidents or incidents related to a transaction, including SEAH information.
- Information about any other material changes to the E&S Management System.

Besides, Kufi Fund GCF will prepare an E&S monitoring report for all investors on an annual basis with aggregated information of the FSP/innovative companies:

- E&S risk category of each investee.

### GRIEVANCE MECHANISM

The grievance mechanism ensures the right to be heard for complainants who feel affected by Kuafi Fund-GCF activities. In order to enable resolution of disputes and assist the Fund Activity in drawing lessons learned for current and future operations, any person or group may lodge a complaint and it can be submitted in any language by letter, e-mail and/or web form (e-mail and web to be determined). Kuafi Fund-GCF expects all investors to have a GRM in place in which they complete all complaints and follow up their resolution.

## DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION

After the ESD of a potential investee and before the financial legal agreement is signed, the E&S Officer will disclose an "Executive Summary & E&S Assessment" (includes the E&S category of this investment) and a detailed E&S report to the investor. The E&S Officer will also provide a link to the E&S assessment on the Funded Activity's webpage according to the requirements of GCF's Disclosure Policy.

## CAPACITY IN TERMS OF HUMAN RESOURCES

### Senior Management

The CEOs of GAWA Capital as representatives from the upper management are responsible for Kuafi Fund-GCF's overall commitment to E&S objectives during the whole investment process. In particular, the CEOs establish and maintain a culture that promotes compliance with E&S policies and procedures, ensures resolution of unresolved E&S issues or non-compliance associated with a transaction that cannot be resolved by the investor, and provides support for the investor in the relevant country office to achieve better understanding of the financial institution's potential exposure to E&S risk, which may include taking legal action against the client/investee.

### E&S Officer

The Technical Assistant Manager of GAWA Capital will act as the E&S Officer of the Kuafi Fund-GCF. The E&S Officer is responsible for developing and implementing the E&S Action Plan as well as for communicating with senior management on E&S issues and concerns.

The E&S Officer is responsible for developing and updating the procedures and documents that are part of Fund-GCF's E&S Management System (E&M) to ensure consistency at all levels, and provides assistance to Investment Officers in evaluating and monitoring the E&S performance of clients/investees.

The E&S Officer in coordination with the Gender Officer will be involved in the solution of all E&S claims (including SEAH) received through the Funded Activity's GRM.

The E&S Officer will also lead the communication with the Members of the Technical Assistance Committee and, when necessary, with the management of the TA implementation partners.


---
**Page 108**
---

The E&S Officer receive annual training of E&S issues, including SEAM in order to keep up-to-date and will integrate and share the updated knowledge with the investors. Given the multi-country nature of the programme, the training will need to collate cross-cutting material subjects with countries E&S capacities.

## Investment Officer

The Investment Officer is responsible for following the procedures of the ESMS at the transaction level. They discuss and negotiate possible E&S mitigation measures with the client/investee.

The Investment Officer is also responsible for evaluating the E&S risks at the level of individual transactions and make a recommendation to the Credit/Investment Committee on whether to proceed with a transaction.

The Investment Officer ensures that the Funded Activity’s E&S requirements are incorporated in legal obligations, including any conditions attached to the loan. Failure to comply with the terms of the loan with E&S clauses constitutes a breach of contract and is considered an Event of Default under the terms of the loan agreement and requires follow-up by Senior Management.

## Investment Committee

The Investment Committee is responsible for deciding if E&S risks are acceptable to Kuafi-Fund-GCF overall exposure to risk before proceeding with a transaction.


### G.2. Fund-GCF aims at improving access to financial services with a special focus to support women entrepreneurs. In addition, specific gender interventions through TA funding will enable for more empowered women in the LAC region.

The Fund-GCF aims at improving access to financial services with a special focus to support women entrepreneurs. In addition, specific gender interventions through TA funding will enable for more empowered women in the LAC region.


### Target countries have made substantial progress in recent decades, particularly in the topics of access to education and health care, as well as in the legal framework.

Target countries have made substantial progress in recent decades, particularly in the topics of access to education and health care, as well as in the legal framework. However, social norms limit the practical impact of the legally recognized rights of women. Gender gaps are still large in areas such as access to employment opportunities, decision-making power within households, control over income and assets, and distribution of household and caring tasks. Gender-based violence continues being a major social issue in all countries. An external consultant will be hired to carry out country assessments.


### Women in LAC have a strong history of entrepreneurial activity as the females have led TIA ratios show; 0.84.

The higher female entrepreneurship rates are not only due to women being more driven by necessity than in the case of men, for this reason, supporting them via financing and other mechanisms gains further importance. Women entrepreneurs face several constraints that limit their access to financial resources, human and social capital, key constraints for the growth of their business. These constraints are exacerbated by weaker levels of financial literacy and lower legal ownership of assets or guarantees. However, women have shown a high capacity to adapt to these challenges and have demonstrated their ability to overcome them.


### Regarding agriculture, a high proportion of rural women employed in this sector are also unpaid family workers which puts them at a disadvantage.

Gender gaps accentuate the vulnerability of women to the impacts of climate change, limit their adaptation capacity and reduced their potential contribution to reduce emissions of greenhouse gases.


---
**Page 109**
---

The project will mainstream gender equality and women’s empowerment at the Funded Activity level, the FSPs and Innovative Companies’ level and their portfolio. Women-led organizations will be identified in each country and consultations with them will be conducted to incorporate their knowledge and views in the implementation of the Gender Action Plan. The target companies will be identified in the target countries and dissemination of information on gender violence as well as resources available for women’s empowerment will be provided to these companies, including women-led companies for both internal and external dimension. A gender approach management system has been established at the Funded Activity level with a specific gender action plan. To ensure that gender equality is addressed, specific covenants will be introduced in loan agreements.

Tracker indicators include, among others, the number of trainings about business skills and financial literacy and % of participation in capacity building activities (see Gender Action Plan for further information).

Regarding human resources, a Gender Officer who will receive periodic training will monitor the investee’s compliance with the gender action plan. The Funded Activity will also have an E&S officer to manage its gender management system. An E&S officer with expertise in gender will also be hired and work in coordination with the Fund Manager. A gender scorecard will be developed, including a gender diversity score and social performance score which will be linked directly to carried interest of the fund managers – if objectives are not achieved, the fund manager will earn a lower performance fee. Annual report will include information on the implementation of the Gender Action Plan.

### Financial Management

Kuafi CDFI will apply best practices in its financial management and procurement practices. The Funded Activity will benefit from the extensive experience in fund management of the Fund’s Manager, GAVIA Capital Partners SICAV SEIC, in addition to the support of external service providers such as a Fund Administrator, Consultant for internal audit and an independent auditor. The Funded Activity will also have access to book-keeping and preparation of the quarterly NAV and Annual Accounts of Kuafi CDFI, supported by documentation from the Fund Manager. The Funded Activity will undergo annual external audits, to be realized by an independent auditor appointed in accordance with the terms of reference agreed upon between the Fund Manager.

A detailed Financial Model enables the Fund Manager to overview all operations and convert cashflows into revenues and GCF’s after fee structure management. The Funded Activity’s Financial Model will include the consistent logging of cashflows in line with real bank cash movements and balances, whilst projecting the future cash flows for the next 12 months. This will enable Kuafi CDFI to provide a return on capital/dividend payments to investors whilst ensuring the timely payment of all service provisions.

Disbursements will be carried out on an investment-by-investment basis, involving a Capital Call procedure following the 10% call-up of each investment. Capital Call notifications are sent out by investors to the Administrator who will then notify Kuafi CDFI via email. The Fund Manager will credit the funds in their totality to Kuafi CDFI’s Transaction Account (as per industry best practices for disbursement). Prior to any disbursements, the Fund Manager will provide a detailed transaction documentation outlining the nature of the transaction and the amount being disbursed along with relevant documentation backing the transaction. In the case of debt financing, a disbursement schedule will be established in accordance with the terms agreed upon between Kuafi CDFI and the Borrower and this will be accompanied by the corresponding request for release through capital call(s). Commitments from Component I (loans and equity for investments) and commitments from Component II (grants for TA) will be separate and there will be therefore different calls for either financing capital and TA funds.

As long as CDFIDES is a limited partner, CDFIDES will not provide any no objection to the disbursements. CDFIDES will ensure that all disbursements will comply with the legal documentation that will be established the eligibility criteria and the rights granted to investors.


---
**Page 110**
---

To ensure financial sustainability of the Funded Activity’s investments, close monitoring of each investment is carried out by the Fund Manager’s investment team and CRO. Monitoring is typically carried out on a quarterly basis, involving the review of a detailed Reporting Fact Sheet to be filled out by investors, Financial Statements prepared in accordance with IFRS standards, and an assessment of the risk profile of relevant third parties. Country risk is managed on-the-go, with the team keeping actively informed of political, macroeconomic, and financial developments that may impact the Funded Activity’s investment environment. Monitoring is up-stepped to a higher frequency where relevant (for example, in COVID-19 situations). Fund Manager’s monitoring of current investments has been heightened.

The Technical Assistance Steering Committee (TASC) will be responsible for overseeing the funding of the Funded Activity's operations (e.g., Administrator, Custodian, Auditor, Insurance, Technical Assistants such as Consultants), etc. It will entail the hiring of renowned and trusted specialists in each field, which will be selected following a transparent tender process. The TASC will also oversee the selection of the technical assistance providers for the Funded Activity. This evaluation is based on cost, adequacy and scope of services provided in each provider’s proposal. In the case of the Technical Assistance Steering Committee, the TASC will have full decision-making power over the process; this will involve a tender procedure, with decision-making in the power of the Technical Assistance Steering Committee during at least the first year of Kufi Fund-GCF’s life. The CDFIES’s approved procurement and hiring processes will be followed for all technical assistance providers.

The Grant Recipient will be responsible for implementing the Funded Activity. All procurements, including those carried out by Grant Recipients themselves and procurement plans will be agreed with the Grant Recipient.

COFEES will carry out constant monitoring of the project. COFEES will elaborate monitoring reports for the Steering Committee on a quarterly basis. The Steering Committee will also monitor the implementation of the Funded Activity to ensure that it is being implemented in accordance with the approved project document and using the proper use of its resources. GAWA will conduct frequent on-site missions during the investment period and after completion of the project, as well as other missions at the request of the Steering Committee. COFEES and the management team. COFEES will also receive information from each Investment Committee of the Funded Activity. Likewise, Kufi Fund will have independent evaluations and annual audits. It will also have an internal audit function to ensure that all resources are used in accordance with the approved project document.

Finally, thanks to the joint experience of GAWA with COFEES in GFF Fund and Huruma Fund (where resources come from CDFIES, FonPRODEP, and the EU through CDFIES is invested), a highly developed knowledge base has been built up. The same applies for the Kufi Fund. In addition to this, GAWA’s experience in reporting and compliance with EU regulations is also essential for the proper use of GCF resources.

### Disclosure of funding proposal

The General Policy (GP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme documents are subject to confidentiality if they contain sensitive or confidential information. The redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one of the most important elements of the IDP. If a document contains specific segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm:

- Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidentiality features.

□ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full to all parties involved in the GCF.
□ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is required to redact any such information from the document if the document contains specific segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

- full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy;

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP230

## Proposal name: Kuali Fund-GCF

### Accredited entity: Compañía Española de Financiación del Desarrollo (COFIDES)

#### Country/ies:
- Brazil, Colombia, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Panama

#### Project/programme size: Medium


## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

1. Latin America is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its exposure to acute climate events, chronic climate risks and limited adaptation and mitigation capacity.
2. High-exposure to climate change in combination with low adaptive capacity makes Latin American countries extremely vulnerable.
3. Adaptation investments are insufficient partly due to increased public debt levels and a weak domestic revenue base.

### 1.1 Overview

1. Agriculture is the dominant economic sector and source of employment in Latin America, especially in rural areas.
2. The agricultural sector's structure and scale vary significantly between countries.
3. Smallholder farmers who have limited access to resources are particularly exposed to climate change.
4. South American states with a significant agricultural sector, which has been hit hard, especially by severe droughts.
5. Climate change damages farm assets and reduces the productivity of small-scale farms in Latin America.

### 1.2 Overview

1. The region's financial service providers (FSPs) are indirectly exposed to climate change since a significant proportion of the lending portfolio is in sectors exposed to climate risks, for example, agriculture.
2. FSPs have been increasingly focusing their lending portfolios towards increasing climate resilience and greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation activities.

### 1.3 Overview

1. Micro, small, medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) are an important source of employment and generate around 60 per cent of formal productive employment in the Americas.
2. The Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) has identified that Latin America is one of the regions with the highest concentration of MSMEs compared to other world regions.
3. Like smallholder farmers, small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) have been increasingly focusing their lending portfolios towards dealing with the effects of climate change due to limited resources and know-how.

### 1.4 Overview

1. The programme realises investments and provides technical assistance (TA) in seven countries: Brazil, Colombia, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Panama.


---
**Page 129**
---

# Kuali Fund-GCF

The Kuali Fund-GCF will increase the adoption of low-carbon technologies and improve the resilience of smallholder farmers in Latin America and the Caribbean through financial service providers to green their portfolios and finance investments of smaller-scale farmers and SMEs in agriculture, forestry, and other land uses. The fund will also directly support companies that provide climate solutions with the aim of growing climate-related products and services around the world, including those now in萌芽through FSPs.

## Kuali Fund-GCF

The Kuali Fund-GCF is an endowment fund to build a Climate Finance Platform. More than 90 per cent of this fund will be used for this purpose, while the remaining part can be used to invest in innovative companies, as indicated in the response to question 13 raised by the independent expert panel. The Kuali Fund-GCF will provide financial support through variable loans to SMEs (around 20 per cent of the total fund) and farmers (about 80 per cent of the fund). Loans to SMEs will be provided for a period of up to five years, while loans to farmers will have a term of two years to finance assets or activities such as solar rooftop panels, high-efficiency heat pumps, irrigation, low-carbon transport, energy-efficient machinery, etc. Loans to smallholder farmers give them the opportunity to diversify their income sources and invest in climate-smart farming activities such as tree planting, composting, forested restoration, farm irrigation systems, etc. FSPs investing in SMEs will be required to provide a return of 5 per cent on average for the same amount of time. All debt investments of the Kuali Fund-GCF will be in local currency and will have an interest rate of between 4-7 per cent, with expected returns from debt investments of 4-7 per cent in euros after the cost of hedging.

The primary purpose of this programme is to grow small financial service providers. A successful implementation would lead to a significant increase in the volume of loans and an influx of private capital into climate investment. The progress of FSPs will be measured with the Green Index, which measures the performance of FSPs on their green lending and climate products. FSPs shall grade their performance using this index over time due to the investments made by the Kuali Fund-GCF. The Kuali Fund-GCF will also invest in innovative companies that provide climate solutions. The Kuali Fund-GCF allows FSPs a ramp-up period to build their climate portfolio with an initial loan to FSPs of up to 10 per cent over three years, followed by 50 per cent at the end of the first year and 100 per cent by the end of the third year.

The Kuali Fund-GCF will also invest around 10 per cent of its resources in agritechology and climate technology companies with innovative solutions in mitigation (e.g., rural solar energy systems, agroforestry, etc.) or adaptation (e.g., farm-to-business supply chain models) and in adaptation (e.g. farm-to-business supply chain models, smart farming), with an average ticket size of up to 50 million euros per company. These investments will be made over a tenor of six years, on average. Two investments are expected for the Kuali Fund-GCF. The fund has budgetary targets of 15-20 per cent per annum for equity investment projects in innovative companies.

## Around 50 per cent of investments

Around 50 per cent of investments are expected in adaptation and resilience-focused technologies and 50 per cent in mitigation technologies.


---
**Page 130**
---

10. **GCAF** Capital Partners, a private limited liability firm specializing in impact investment management services, organized as an alternative investment fund manager and supervised by the Spanish Exchange Commission, will act as a fund and TA fund manager and is the executing entity (TE) of the GCF project. The TE will be responsible for the day-to-day operational management across four funds investing in various Asian and Latin American and Asian countries. The TE will have an independent board of directors and a separate investment advisory committee comprised of large investors, but with a purely advisory role, and a strategic advisory group to make recommendations to the fund manager of a strictly advisory nature.

11. **The EUR 128 million project** has a financing source (i) EUR 25 million from the GCF as equity at an annual interest rate of zero percent; (ii) EUR 70.4 million from the Spanish Agency for International Development Cooperation (AECID) as senior loan with maturity of ten years and a 3.75 per cent cap; (v) EUR 18 million from the Raúl Fund, indirectly from AECID, as a subordinated loan with an interest rate of zero percent and a maturity of five years; (vi) EUR 20 million in senior equity from GCAF Capital; (vii) EUR 72.6 million in private investor senior equity at ten years with a cap of 3 per cent; (ix) EUR 15 million in private investor subordinated equity at six years and a cap of 4 per cent; (x) EUR 0.3 million in grants from AECID and EUR 0.04 million in grants from Raúl Fund, indirectly from AECID. The project will be implemented by GCAF Capital. The funds (EUR 72.6 million) will help mobilize private investors’ participation and reduce their perceived risk of investing in the renewable energy sectors; it is expected that Compañía Española de Financiación del Desarrollo (COFIDES) and GCAF Capital will invest in the programme to align their interests and perceived risks.

### 1.2 Impact potential

**Scope: Medium**

13. The programme claims an adaptation impact of 242,000 direct and 642,000 indirect beneficiaries (0.28 per cent of the total population). The GCF mitigation impact is estimated at 2.7 million tonnes CO2 equivalent (tCO2e) avoided based on a calculation of EUR 8.4 million in investments.

14. Direct beneficiaries are farmers and SMEs which receive a loan from FSPs. Indirect beneficiaries include the household members of the beneficiary. The calculation of beneficiaries is based on (i) the number of loans extended by FSPs to SMEs and farmers, assuming that each loan will benefit one farmer or SME; (ii) an average loan size per group, and (iii) an average tenor per group with a fund investment period of six years. Beneficiaries are considered to be overestimated in the original funding proposal for two reasons: (i) the number of loans is based on the total amount of loans for insulation or solar hot water with no direct resilience impact; and (ii) the beneficiary number calculation does not take into account that 50 per cent of the investment targeted towards mitigation are given for climate investments, while in the funding proposal (page 35), it is stated that the climate project will be implemented only by the end of six years. On average, only 50 per cent of loans would have been given with a direct climate impact. Therefore, beneficiary numbers of farmer-related loans need to be divided by two to account for this overestimation.

15. Beneficiary numbers are considered to be overestimated in the original funding proposal for two reasons: (i) the number of loans is based on the total amount of loans for insulation or solar hot water with no direct resilience impact; and (ii) the beneficiary number calculation does not take into account that 50 per cent of the investment targeted towards mitigation are given for climate investments, while in the funding proposal (page 35), it is stated that the climate project will be implemented only by the end of six years. On average, only 50 per cent of loans would have been given with a direct climate impact. Therefore, beneficiary numbers of farmer-related loans need to be divided by two to account for this overestimation.

16. The mitigation benefit is calculated based on 50 per cent of the investment targeted towards mitigation and an estimated average cost per tCO2e avoided based on a calculation of


---
**Page 131**
---

total CO2 mitigated and total investments made. Calculations are based on typical investment projects, differentiating between types of projects and sectors (buildings/industrial energy generation and access, transport, and forestry and land use) depending from investments from FSB to the Kuali Fund. The largest share of the total mitigation comes from forestry and land-use companies. Approx. 83 per cent of mitigated CO2 is based on forestry and land-use change (50 per cent for forests and 33 per cent for land-use change). The second largest share, approx. 14 per cent for buildings and industrial appliances, 2 per cent for energy generation and less than 1 per cent for transport. The remaining 6 per cent is based on investments in innovative firms, and 80 per cent is due to loans from FSB.

The mitigation benefit is considered to be overstated in the original funding proposal due to overestimation of the share of CO2 emissions that can be mitigated by forestry and land-use investments, with a higher share going to innovative firms with a higher GHG impact per invested unit. The Kuali Fund invests 100 per cent into FSB, while the Kuali Fund Invests less than 10 per cent of the total fund in forestry companies, while the Kuali Fund Invests more than 80 per cent. The mitigation numbers of the Kuali Fund-projects are lower than initially estimated based on a (HPP) approach. The share of CO2 emissions from energy generation is approx. 50 and 30 per cent being channeled through FSB.

The impact calculations for energy generation follow standard methodological approaches, using factor coefficients based on the methodology and dataset as published by the UNFCCC / International Financial Institutions. For buildings-and energy-generation, an assumption of a 20 per cent reduction in electricity consumption is made (e.g., for light-emitting diodes (LEDs) of electric energy can be saved). It also assumes a capacity factor of 35 per cent for wind power and 40 per cent for solar power. These assumptions are not deemed as conservative. However, the impact of these assumptions on the total mitigation impact is considered to minor, as the share of energy generation in the total mitigation impact is only 14 per cent.

The impact calculations in the transport sector are overly optimistic and overstate emissions reductions due to a number of factors: (i) high levels of vehicle fleet efficiency improvements, which are very optimistic; (ii) baseline emissions of vehicles, which are very high and not representative for the average fleet; (iii) the lack of information on the technology requested by GHG transport methodologies. A project alternative of a new vehicle with a higher fuel economy and low CO2 emission for electric vehicles is considered instead of applying grid electricity to power electric vehicles. Using this more conservative approach, expected GHG reductions in this area are deemed to be 20-30 per cent of the amount suggested by the AE-Fund methodology. The share of transport in total mitigation impact is marginal as the share of transport in the total mitigation impact is less than 1 per cent.

The approach used to determine agricultural, forestry and land-use impacts is based on technical guidelines provided by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) for estimating GHG emissions from agriculture. The methodology varies depending on activities. Other sources[2] and/or other GCF-financed or co-to-be-funded programs are also used to estimate agricultural, forestry and land-use impacts. For example, Project Drawdown GHG data, as one example, shows two tonnes per annum for humbo calibration of the impact of deforestation. The baseline emissions from agriculture were considered, while other sources show 3.13 tonnes (see Tree Plantations on Degraded Land) [Project Drawdown]. The emission factors therefore do not seem to be on

# Paradigm shift potential: Medium-High

## 1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium-High

### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium-High

The AE considers, in general, an impact period of 20 years for agriculture, forestry and land use based on FAO EX-act guidelines. However, the investment period is one year for the average credits and six years for innovative companies. Various types of projects are considered, such as those that have a high level of certainty regarding the amount given, and others, such as terraces and rainwater reservoirs, require continuous maintenance. A more detailed discussion on the impact period can be found in Section 2.3. The TAP projects to assume the impact only over the invested period; that is a maximum of six years.

### Multiple agricultural investments, for example in forestry, have potential impacts of several decades or even centuries. However, the independent TAP team notes that mitigation numbers need to be taken with caution as they refer, to a certain extent, avoided and sequenced emissions rather than new emissions.

Based on above considerations, the independent TAP estimates a more realistic adaptation and mitigation benefit for this programme: 121,000 direct beneficiaries, $321 million indirect benefits, and $4.5 billion in total benefits. The TAP team notes that the GHG mitigation impact is partly based on sequenced emissions with the risk of non-permanence.

The programme allows for some investment areas which can be considered to lock in fossil technologies, such as investments in fossil-efficient trucks or hybrid vehicles. Technology lock-in is a critical issue and should be avoided where possible by ensuring long-lasting permanence. Electric vehicles in trucking are also already cost-competitive in most markets. Climate change mitigation is not the only objective of this programme; it aims to achieve a maximum climate impact and achieve the target countries' decarbonisation goals.

Neither the EE, GAWA Capital, nor the fund management's designated investment committee has any influence over which projects or companies are selected for investments or projects. This endangers the potential impact of the programme. GAWA Capital has lower GHG mitigation impacts per invested euro than the other two funds on board. As this is considered critical not only for the programme impact but, for programme success, independent TAP approval is conditional upon fulfilment of this point.

The former has higher immediate GHG mitigation impacts per invested euro; the latter interventions give rise to a high level of indirect and long-term impacts in terms of fossil fuel investments, thus multiplying the impact of the initial investment. The indirect implications of this thus favour investments in FSPs and not in innovative companies.

Technology lock-in is also an issue with some investments which have a potentially high multiplication impact, resulting in private capital flowing into this sector and multiple instances of lock-in being driven by the greened FSP. Thus, the medium and long-term indirect impact is considered higher or equal to that of the direct impact.

Appropriate monitoring of the achieved impact in terms of resilience and mitigation is critical for programme success. The TAP team notes that it will be difficult to monitor during programme implementation by avoiding investments with low input and strengthening those with high input FSPs must also be provided with simple-to-manage monitoring tools to track their climate change impacts.

Overall, the independent TAP considers the project to have a medium impact. The short-term indirect impact is regarded as potentially high, conditioned upon successfully mitigating climate-related know-how into programme execution.


---
**Page 133**
---

28. The KfW Fund-GCF targets FSPs. The greening of FSPs and their shift towards financing investments with favorable climate impact is paramount for moving large amounts of capital from the private sector towards investments with an adaptation and/or mitigation impact. Small FSPs are particularly important in this regard, as they can provide a bridge between microfinance and climate-related lending, and unlocking this potential is a crucial paradigm shift.

29. The investee companies are intended to be financially sustainable enterprises that continue to operate even after the Fund's support has ended. This is an important element to attract additional investments, thus expanding their impact. Successful loans from FSPs and exits of the fund in successful projects will provide evidence for investors and other stakeholders concerning the profitability and risk of investing in this area, thus supporting a paradigm shift.

30. The KfW Fund-GCF intends to transform small FSPs to adopt and implement green strategies. This is an important element to achieve the greening of FSPs as described above. This is considered an appropriate approach to assess the progression of the FSP in accordance with the Fund's strategy. The assessment of the progress made up to the end of the portfolio in the climate change area, also considered proper, as FSPs are now too the topic. Together with the other elements mentioned above, this will lead to a paradigm shift.

31. The intermediate assessment of target achievement in year three provides the required pressure to stay on track.

32. This paradigm shift is achieved through greening the loan portfolio of FSPs. The area of direct investment in companies which provide climate solutions is less transformational. Also, while only small investments are made by the Fund, the impact of these investments will be critical as it is achieved, and no transformational impact can be predicted. Investments in individual enterprises can be justified to diversify risks and increase the fund's profitability, which are important elements for the Fund. However, the amount invested in this area should be limited to allow a clear focus on FSPs.

33. High-level engagement with FSPs regarding climate lending will be crucial to achieving the target of greening FSPs. The TA strengthens the FSP and is considered essential and acknowledged for attaining high climate impact and reducing risks to FSPs. GAWA Capital Partners (GCP) has a long history in providing technical assistance to FSPs, has an excellent background and experience working with financial institutions and investment instruments, and has a strong network of contacts that can be mobilized to ensure the transformational potential is achieved.

34. Ecosystem issues and failures are also significant barriers to greening FSPs. The AE has not yet identified any specific barriers. The TA strengthens analysis of the ecosystem and activities to remove identified barriers. A further strengthening of this area and collaboration with other institutions in the same sector and good outcomes will be a key step towards achieving transformational change. However, the effect of this programme alone will be insufficient to achieve the greening of FSPs. It is considered essential that the Fund takes two steps: focusing on FSPs, collaborating with other institutions in the same sphere and good outcomes with a high-quality knowledge management system are essential to ensure and strengthen the transformational change.

1.4 Sustainable development potential

* The project is aligned with several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth), SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities), SDG 13 (Climate action), and SDG 15 (Life on land).

* Environmental co-benefits are related to organic farming and reduced fertilizer use, reducing soil erosion and improving water quality. This leads to increased crop yields with less post-harvest loss, and reduced pressure on land. On the mitigation side, environmental co-benefits include reduced energy usage, which can again transition towards lower pollutants.

* Economic co-benefits are related to the introduction of new technologies that will improve the incomes of smallholder farmers and SMEs in the host countries. Farmers can expect increased access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, and other input materials, increased productivity and yields, and improved sales prices of agricultural products through enhanced quality of products and market information. SMEs and invested companies can improve their competitiveness by adopting new technologies that will allow them to compete with a high-growth potential.

* Social co-benefits are realized through improving the livelihood of farmers by increasing crop productivity (through the adoption of climate-smart agriculture) and incomes, resulting in enhanced quality of life. The project also expects to create around 1,000 jobs over the life of the fund.

* The project seeks to invest in and support agribusinesses and SMEs through a lender. It also provides TAs to the invested companies and FMs to enhance their gender expertise.

* Overall, the project is expected to have significant positive social impacts. It can make contributions in the area of gender and have positive social, economic and environmental impacts.


1.5 Needs of the recipient

* The countries included are vulnerable to climate change, and many have a significant agricultural sector that is particularly exposed to climate risks.

* Many countries face high levels of private sector credit risk through lending, among other things, to the agricultural sector, which has high climate risks. Also, SMEs experience above-average exposure to climate risks due to their lack of adaptive capacity.

* Many countries involved have significant and persistent fiscal deficits, which have led to public debt sustainability concerns, including borrowing, and private sector credit risk. Limited access to credit results in low levels of private sector investment. High-level government borrowing from the financial sector crowds out private sector financing and has kept the financial system underdeveloped. This limits the availability of finance for agriculture or SMEs. It also limits government financing in the area of adaptation to climate risks.

* The independent TAC considers the recipient's need medium to high.


1.6 Country ownership

* The programme is consistent with the nationally determined contributions (NDCs) of the involved countries, which is unsurprising in itself as the programme intends to cover two large sectors and a wide range of technologies. It also aims to provide access to an actual range of technologies. These technologies, as well as the sectors, are included in the NDCs.

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Score: Medium

### Kuali Fund-GCF has a blended finance structure.

The European Union and GCF contributions to the first loss-tranche (EUR 126 million from the European Union and EUR 25 million from the GCF) are expected to be leveraged by private capital, including loans from IFC and equity from COFIDES. The aim is to attract private capital to the agriculture and SME sector.

The All intends to establish a EUR 119.5 million disbursement facility (DF).

### The European Union (EUR 126 million in equity and EUR 0.57 million in grants) and AECID (EUR 17.6 million in loans, EUR 1.8 million indirectly through Kuali Fund-GCF and EUR 0.3 million directly from GCF) are expected to provide the first loss-tranche.

COFIDES is expected to invest EUR 5 million from its balance sheet and GAWA Capital is expected to invest EUR 26 million in equity, with a further EUR 17.4 million of investment from private investors, including from COFIDES as equity. Current investors in the Buruma Fund managed by GAWA Capital have expressed interest in investing, including large institutional investors such as pension funds and family offices.

Considering the challenging market conditions and high interest rates, the target for the disbursement facility is EUR 105 million. If this target cannot be achieved, then the total fund would be smaller than expected. To ensure that private capital leverages the investment of the GCF, the DF should be tied to a higher share of equity than envisaged.

### The All requests EUR 25 million in equity capital from GCF with a 3.9 per cent equity return.

This vehicle, and its GCF's anchoring investment, should enable the fund to raise EUR 176 million in total. This is expected to be leveraged by loans from Kuali Fund-GCF (indirectly from AECID), EUR 1 million in equity from GAWA Capital and EUR 25 million in equity from COFIDES.

The GCF participation is considered material to provide de-risking in attracting additional capital. Thus, the GCF equity investment could be leveraged by nearly fourfold, a high leverage factor. At the moment, the All has not yet identified an appropriate target for the disbursement facility. A staged or truncated investment of the GCF with clear achievement targets of private sector financing is expected to provide de-risking and leverage in achieving the amount that was expected.

10. The Fund expects to raise EUR 8.7 million in grant finance for the TA fund and data collection, EUR 7.6 million from GCF, and EUR 9.0 million from the European Union and AECID. GCF grants could thus be leveraged with a low ratio of 0.1.

The total investment amount is EUR 24.2 million, where EUR 32.8 million is from GCF, with an additional EUR 74.6 million in co-financing and a leverage factor of 2.9. This is a high leverage ratio and a high co-finance ratio. The independent TAP notes that the programme proposal is well aligned with GAWA Capital’s strategy in specializing in impact investment management services with considerable experience in fund management and the agricultural and SME sectors working through FSPs. 57. The overall independent TAP assessment concerning efficiency and effectiveness is considered medium. The co-finance ratio, if achieved, is high. To achieve this level, safeguards must be in place to ensure that the programme proposal does not result in a cost overrun more than anticipated. The involvement of GAWA Capital and COPHRES is also deemed essential to ensure the alignment with the GCF’s strategic objectives. The overall TAP assessment is considered relatively favourable. The cost per direct beneficiary and tonne of CO2 mitigated are relatively low but are, as with any programme, dependent on the final project financed.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

58. The independent TAP considers that the funding proposal to be in line with the GCF’s strategic targets. The independent TAP notes that the programme aims to invest in FSPs in the agricultural and SME sectors which are particularly vulnerable to climate change, including serious climate risks. Greeting financial service providers is critical to achieving a large-scale deployment of private capital for adaptation and mitigation projects in small FSPs. This is paramount to completing a transformational impact and moving investments from one-time project funding to ongoing business models. The independent TAP notes that the TA fund will experience attracting private capital and working with FSPs, and indirectly, smaller farmers, through the GCF’s financial instruments. The independent TAP also notes that this novel programmatic approach, the financial experience background of the managing entity, the significant impact and especially the transformational nature of this programme proposal warrant further consideration.

59. The independent TAP also recognizes some significant deficiencies in the funding proposal. These include: (i) the lack of a clear methodology for calculating the number of beneficiaries; (ii) the deficient and non-conservative calculations regarding GHG impact and the number of beneficiaries; (iii) incomplete documentation within the funding proposal; and (iv) the risk of

Needs of the recipient

One of the key outcomes of COP28 was the need to promote a joint effort by governments, international financial institutions and the private sector to align financial flows with global climate goals.

As a result of growing adaptation finance needs and stagnating flows, the current adaptation finance gap is estimated at US$194-366 billion per year. Simultaneously, adaptation planning and implementation capacity appear to have stagnated. This has severe consequences, especially for the most vulnerable.

Kuall will help mobilize private capital to finance the climate transition. In particular, it will support up to 20% of MSMEs in the energy sector by helping PSOs to transform themselves to be able to offer adapted products and solutions.


Country ownership

As the ITAP itself stated, Kuall Fund is aligned with the nationally determined contributions (NDCs) of all countries.

AE considers that the Country Ownership is higher than the level assessed by the ITAP for the following reasons:

* Kuall is based on IMIs and it will support agriculture sector, specially on adaptation, as well as mitigation with a comprehensive range of technologies. These technologies, as well as the sectors, are included in the NDCs.
* In contrast to the proposal from the ITAP, the proposal was not only assessed by the NDA but also underwent a validation process involving various ministries and institutions in all the countries concerned.
* Kuall has been developed with a high level of alignment in relation to their NDCs and policies, and have found Kuall’s strategy for achieving climate objectives (i.e. support for the PSFs) to be very consistent with their overall strategy.

As ITAP acknowledges, Kuall has established a process that includes formal stakeholder engagement once implementation has begun. Knowledge sharing is part of Kuall’s strategy and will be an important element in ensuring that assistance and capacity building on climate finance will be the tools to achieve this.


Efficiency and effectiveness

GAWA Capital and COHDES have a long track record of working in similar blended finance structures where alignment of interests is a critical condition.

The partnership of KU and ECA is granted because of their fiduciary duties, responsibilities, and economic interest. The funding request is small and the leverage ratio is high.

# Gender documentation for FP230

## GCF GENDER ASSESSMENT & ACTION PLAN – KUALI FUND-GCF

### 1. GENDER ASSESSMENT

#### 1.1 Introduction

This Gender Assessment and the resulting Gender Action Plan has been prepared following the "Gender Analysis/Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan Templates" and the guiding questions listed therein. The gender assessment is based on a desk review of existing literature, secondary databases, and primary data derived from a desk review of existing literature and secondary databases, introduced as in body and footnote citations on this document.

The results of this assessment aim to promote the transformation of the Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) financial sector into a more inclusive and greener industry by fostering systems' adoption of climate best practices by smaller financial institutions, including Microfinance Institutions, Cooperatives or other Non-Bank Financial Institutions and comparing them with larger banks. The gender assessment is divided in two main chapters: Chapter 1.2 focuses on a general overview of gender issues regarding MSMEs and smallholder farmers in Latin America and Chapter 1.3 provides a gender analysis of the Kuali Fund-GCF investment portfolio, based on national gender strategies/policies and gender issues in specific areas like entrepreneurship and agriculture.

The Kuali Fund-GCF gender targets are considered to be ambitious and of significant contribution. These targets have been defined in general terms, not for each country in specific. Nonetheless, the targets have been structured according to GIZ/GCF's Current investments including most of the countries of this multi-country proposal.

# 1.2 Country Analysis

## 1.2.1 General overview of UAC region

A general overview of the target countries regarding gender dynamics can be provided by the Gender Development Index (GDI), the Gender Inequality Index (GII) of the United Nations and the Global Gender Gap Ranking of the World Economic Forum.

| Country | Gender Development Index (GDI) | Group Rank* | Gender Inequality Index (GII) | Rank | Global Gender Gap* | Rank |

| Colombia | 0.989 | 1 | 0.428 | 101 | 0.725 | 93 |
| El Salvador | 0.975 | 2 | 0.383 | 85 | 0.738 | 43 |

Looking at GDI, disparities in development between men and women are more pronounced in Guatemala which belongs to group 3 that comprises countries with medium equality in HDI with absolute deviation of ±5–7 percent. Then, El Salvador and Mexico belong to group 2 which comprises countries with medium to high equality in HDI achievement (absolute deviation less than or equal to five percent) and men (absolute deviation of two percent). The rest of target countries have high equality in HDI achievement (absolute deviation of one percent for men and absolute deviation of less than one percent for women).

Regarding the GII which reflects inequality in achievement between women and men in three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and the labor market, all target countries range between the 62-193 positions.

* The GDI measures gender gaps in human development achievements by accounting for disparities between male and female in three basic dimensions of human development: education (measured as the ratio of female to male enrollment at primary level), health (measured using the maternal mortality rate) and income (measured using the sex ratio in the labor force). The GII is a direct measure of gender gap showing the female risk as a percentage of the male HDI.

* In this table, countries are ranked by their GDI. For example, Brazil has the highest GDI score with 0.984 and Panama has the lowest GDI score with 1.019. The GII is calculated using the following formula: GII = (1 - GDI) * 100. The GII ranges from zero to one hundred, where a higher value indicates greater gender inequality in human development achievements.

* Maternal mortality ratio and adolescent birth rate, empowerment, measured by proportion of parliamentary seats occupied by females and proportion of adult women with at least secondary education, and labor market participation are the three dimensions used for calculating GII. The GII is calculated using the following formula: GII = (1 - GDI) * 100. The GII ranges from zero to one hundred, where a higher value indicates greater gender inequality in human development achievements.

* For details on how the Gender Inequality Index is calculated see Technical note 2 at http://wdi.un.org/default.aspx?tab=2_technical_notes.pdf

Footnotes: * Countries are divided into three groups based on their HDI and GII regarding gender parity in HDI issues. **World Economic Forum, Global Gender Gap Report

The WEF ranks 132 countries in its 2013 report, placing Europe and Central America, which has closed 76.4% of its gap, Latin America and the Caribbean (72.1%), and Eastern Europe and Central Asia (71.7%). South Asia and the Pacific region (58.6%) is just ahead of sub-Saharan Africa (53.0%), while South Asia has closed only 39.5% of its gender gap, and Sub-Saharan Africa a mere 24%. ### 1.2.2 Specific Gender Analysis by country

#### 1.2.2.1 Brazil
From a strictly legal standpoint, men and women are equal in Brazil. The "Cinco" Constitution of 1988 declared equality between men and women in all legal aspects, explicitly stating article 5 of this Title that "men and women have equal rights and duties under the terms of this Constitution". Women, Business and Law Index scores 85, with legal conditions found in the areas of: workplace (law does not mandate equal remuneration for work of equal value) and assets (law does not mandate equal division). The National Plan for Gender Equality (PNEG) was approved by the Brazilian government to promote, enforce and monitor gender equality, with a focus on violence against women, are in place according to UN Women.

The National Women, Family and Human Rights (NMFHRH) has the purpose to implement, promote, and protect human rights, civic rights, and the rights of children, adolescents, women, families, the elderly, and the disabled. The OECD Development Centre's Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) which measures discrimination against women in social institutions taking into account laws, social norms and practices that restrict women's access to


---
**Page 145**
---

resources and empowerment opportunities, scores 21.6%. 95% of population think that a man should have more right to a job than a woman and 22% that men makes better business executives.

Brazil is fairly egalitarian when it comes to providing education and health rights for men and women but continues to have large gender gaps within the workforce and government representation. Gender gap in management positions, and in top management is 26% and 10% respectively.

Some representative indicators show for example that the proportion of women aged 25-49 years old who were married or in union before age 18 is 22%. Also, the adolescent birth rate is 58.6 per 1000 population as of 2016.

UN Women’s Gender Inequality Index (GII) for Brazil shows that gender equality has improved since 2009. However, data are available with gaps in key areas such as violence against women. In addition, many areas such as gender and poverty, women's access to assets including land, physical and sexual harassment, and gender and the environment currently lack comparable methodologies for comprehensive and periodic monitoring.

Regarding the program specific areas of intervention, Brazil has 16 million MSMEs, being the largest contributor in the region to total enterprises[1]. MSMEs form an essential part of the Brazilian economy, accounting for 98.5% of all legally constituted businesses and employing approximately 41% of the total workforce. In 2017, Brazil ranks 6th in Total Early-stage Entrepreneur Activity (TEA), 7th in Established Business Ownership (ERO) and 5 in Information Technology[2]. There is a significant gender gap in both TEA (23% male; 17.2% female) and ERO (42.8% male; 6.8% female)[3].

Despite their importance to economic growth, MSMEs and smallholder farmers suffer more from credit constraints than larger enterprises[4]. This is due in part to the lack of collateral for loans and the high cost of financial services by large companies. This situation is often exacerbated by the lack of financial planning and training of the manager.

Women account for 31% of the total entrepreneurial population in Brazil, but for 47% of individual micro entrepreneurs[5]. Support programmes are more likely to be targeted at women than men[6], and it has been estimated that women were to start growth-oriented business at the same rate as men if they had access to the same resources[7].

In terms of policies, there is a common perception in Brazil that special programmes for women entrepreneurs are not needed since all programmes are open to everyone. However, a lack of explicit exclusion is often not enough to build a culture of inclusion and equal opportunities. Women entrepreneurs face challenges different from those faced by other underrepresented groups in the entrepreneurial population, such as Afro-descendants, migrants, and rural women.

The finance gap in LAC is the second largest after East Asia region, mainly driven by Brazil (S$0.6 trillion). This large financial gap is especially relevant for women, around 45 percent of women-owned SMEs limited access to finance as a main constraint[8].

According to OECD SIG, regarding access to productive and financial resources, Brazil scores 16/27*, better-than other target countries of the Fund such as El Salvador (23) and Colombia (23)[9]. According to an OECD study[10] in Brazil, the gender gap is not only related to lack of access to finance but also to a lack of skills and knowledge. This can be understood as budgeting, setting goals, choosing financial products and using independent advice, where the gap is 7 points. Meanwhile, there is also a relative difference in financial attitudes in favour of women, with 52% showing positive attitude towards saving money compared with 48% for men[11]. In terms of formal education, Brazil has experiences of interest in the framework of the national financial education program[12].
