According to a study of the Interamerican Development Bank (IDB) about leadership in microfinance institutions[1], 50% of these institutions in Brazil have a female general manager and 20.83% in women senior management positions.

Despite provisions protecting women’s land ownership rights, local customs often infringe on the enjoyment of this right in practice. Women are also subject to discriminatory practices such as the requirement for multiple documents, which prevents them from accessing land titles. Evidence shows that the entire financial architecture of lending, especially in relation to microcredit, has not taken into account the needs of women, particularly poor women and women uprooted by climate change.

Regarding climate change, Brazil is the fifth biggest emitter of greenhouse gases, an important agro-exporter and also home to the Amazon Basin ecosystems that provide essential services for many people both regionally and globally. Brazil’s Amazon deforestation and the agricultural sector are increasingly main contributors due to the expansion of cattle ranching and soybean production.

Climate change effects can be felt not only in a dynamic environmental and socio-economic system. Important impacts can be expected from both global price changes and local yield changes. Previous studies on the impacts of climate change have focused mainly on the economic impacts, while few impacts, with focus being on average 23 percent of their land values to 2000 in the world[2] the climate scenario[3].

Brazil has an unusually large share of female-headed households compared to other countries in Latin America[4][5], but they are more vulnerable than men. The reasons for this vulnerability include: women’s lower education levels, reasons such as higher levels of per capita income which are also more diversified. This could be because in those households there are several income earners, women are older and so have grown-up children and since they earn less money in general, they are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change.

The Brazilian government should therefore be able to absorb the impacts of climate change regarding decreasing yields and increasing prices of agricultural products. However, according to IDB[6], family farms headed by women have performed worse than men’s farms over recent years and rural women suffer more from the constraints of a social environment marked by discriminatory practices.

### 1.2.2 Colombia

Colombia has a robust normative system and institutions to support the rights of girls and women. The country's 1991 Constitution recognizes women's equality and outlaws gender-based discrimination. Particularly, the High Presidential Commission for Women was created in 1993 with the aim of promoting equal opportunities for women over the years[7]. Vigorous legislation to promote equality like the law requiring that women candidates comprise at least 30% of party electoral lists has been crucial for obtaining higher representation of women in public institutions.

The National Development Plan 2018-22 includes an entire chapter on women’s rights based on three dimensions: the economic, the political and physical integrity, which includes violence against women[1].

Despite a good legislative framework, in practice, girls and women still endure discrimination. The most prominent on women-participation in the labour market and their economic development in general. Women, Business and Life Index scores (48.5) are below the global average of 60.2. Women earn less than men for equal work (the gender pay gap is 19% at the national level), and they have less access to assets and resources (the gender asset gap is 37%). The share of women in decision-making positions remains low, with only 5% of firms having women as top management. Women are also underrepresented in parliament (20%) and in public administration (18%).

Particularly important is the exclusion of rural women in the processes of social and productive order that leads to equality between men and women.

An important factor affecting women in Colombia has been the civil armed conflict of recent decades. An estimated 8.8 million people have been registered as victims of the armed conflict. The majority are victims of forced displacement, of which about 80% are women and children and 87% are from rural areas. Furthermore, 15.8% of the women displaced because of the armed conflict have stated that they were also victims of sexual violence.

Regarding the program-specific areas of intervention, MSMEs and smallholder farmers represent an important part of the Colombian economy. In 2013, according to the National Department of Statistics (DANE), MSMEs and smallholder farmers contributed to 40% of GDP, generated 80% of employment and constituted 90% of the country's productive sector.

According to figures from the Regional Chamber of Commerce, of the total number of entrepreneurs in the capital, 36.7% of legal representatives are women. Figure that can certainly be estimated to be much lower on other areas of the country. Often the need for economic subsistence is the main reason for entrepreneurship, accompanied by the aspiration to balance family and work responsibilities and to achieve professional development.

In Colombia, the historical gap in entrepreneurship by gender have shown a decreasing trend and the pace of male entrepreneurs has been faster than that of female entrepreneurs. However, women still lag behind men when it comes to participating in the formal economy. The perception of gender roles, the sexual division of labour and the lack of access to financial capital are some of the factors that condition women's access to the productive sphere.

Women-entrepreneurs are characterized by participating in fragile business activities, not having a stable credit relation and a higher level of vulnerability (lack of education, training, capital, technology, labour, etc.). Thus, women entrepreneurs operate in highly competitive sectors that are more vulnerable to changes in the economic context. According to the GEM report, with respect to ventures, the extractive sector is the one with the lowest participation of women, while the manufacturing and construction sectors have a higher level of female entrepreneurship (15% for transformation, with 20.5%).

The degree of innovation and internationalization of women's business enterprises is equally low. Their presence in high intensity activities is conditioned by a culture and social structure that create gender differences, which condition their attitude and self-esteem; represent greater difficulties for them. Despite reporting higher educational levels and social skills comparable to those of men, companies created by women tend to have a slower size and expansion than those created by men.

Business financing in Colombia was rated with a score of 3 on 5 on the GEM scale (GEM Colombia, 2022), which indicates that Colombian entrepreneurs do not have sufficient sources of financing such as angel investors, equity capital, bank debt, public subsidies, venture capital, crowdfunding, etc. The gender gap in perceived entrepreneurship skills remains at around 10 points over the last five years and in terms of hours of labour is close to 2 hours in 6 months.

8


---
**Page 150**
---

Out of 48 economies, Colombia rank 2 in TIA, 34 in EBO and 34 in informal investment. The proportion of adults starting or running a new business is highest in Five Latin America & Caribbean countries, being Colombia one of them*. Moreover, it is one of the seven countries in which one in five women were starting or running a new business. Female new enterprise creation has increased by 10% since 2013, while the gender gap has persisted. Regarding the levels of TEA and EBO by gender, the gap is 4 and 2.2 points respectively.

The country has no legislation prohibiting discrimination in access to credit*. Colombia scores 35 regarding access to productive resources for women compared with a global average of 18. The country also scores low on the use of land and use of financial products between women and men. Gap in access is 6.0 and in-use is 1.8. Access to credit product also show a gap at 3.3. In addition, in all forms of credit, the average amount of money disbursed is higher for men than for women. The country scores low on the use of financial products between women and men.

According to a study on female leadership by the Inter-American Development Bank, 54.5% of microfinance institutions have a female general manager and 17.4% of women in senior management positions, the best figures among the target countries. Colombia is one of them*, together with Brazil.

Regarding the rural context, women are increasingly involved in unpaid agricultural jobs and householding due to strong gender roles in rural family structures. Therefore, their entrepreneurship activity tends to move towards sectors other than the traditional ones where they find space for growth and that enables them to have economic independence. Our social model is based on the idea of a nuclear family with a man as head of the family. The number of households has increased in the last 10 years. As a result, women must shoulder the responsibilities of both domestic and productive activities. Their poverty rate is 52.3% compared with 47.9% of households headed by men. Regarding external economic resources, women have less access to credit than men. They are more involved in unpaid work, credit, paid work, land, and participation in decision making. 31.3 % of agricultural workers are considered unpaid family help and these activities are often not reported as work*.

The promotion of rural entrepreneurship and the role played by women in agricultural activities, the increase in income related activities and other sectors in the rural economy constitute an essential aspect of rural development policies to bring progress to the regions hardest hit by the conflict. The Second Biennial Update Report on Climate Change (2017) highlights that Colombia is one of 35 countries with a high vulnerability index to climate change with gender perspective. In Colombia the main source of carbon emissions is linked to the agricultural sector, according to the latest inventory of greenhouse gases (GEAM 2017), this emission of this sector was a 36%, above sectors such as energy and transport. The country has 45% of its territory covered by forests, 89% of the rural population, and 30% of them are agricultural producers. The recognition of the central role played by women in aspects such as agricultural production, food security and resilience in communities to climate change are essential elements for the development of policies that promote gender equality.

From the Directorate of Climate Change, "The Proposal of Technical Guidelines to Promote and Strengthen the Gender Approach in Attention to the Establishment of the PNCG (2017)" was built, which is framed in the provisions of the national and international contents, both in terms of gender approach as in relation to its incorporation in the management of Climate change

# El Salvador

El Salvador has implemented numerous legislative amendments in recent years regarding, among others, improving maternity protection, prevention of violence against women, reinforcing gender equality and non-discrimination in the workplace including equal pay for equal work and a place free from violence.

The Salvadoran National Commission on Women (CONADEM) is the institution responsible for formulating, executing and monitoring compliance with the National Policy for Women.

During the period 2015-2020 a National Equity Plan has been the main policy tool with national scope but with regional and sectoral specifics. Institutional changes have been achieved and today more women are working at levels of employment and in management positions than ever before. In 2020, there were four female ministers (33%) and two female ministers in the period 2009-2014 to seven in the present presidential term (43%). In the municipal council in 2021, 11% of mayors are women.

Women’s rights under Law index scores B8.6 with legal constraints found on the areas of pay (law does not mandate equal remuneration for work of equal value), marriage, parenthood and pension. Access to assets is equal90.

Regarding employment, the EQUALS programme has been rolled out to encourage businesses to establish egalitarian, inclusive and decent workplaces, which can be certified by a “gender equality seal”. Since 2018 employers have a duty to provide childcare facilities for their employees’ children between the ages of 4 months and 3 years for the duration of the work.

El Salvador scores 19.6 in OECD SIGI and a significant 27% of people think that a man should have more rights to a job than a woman and that men makes better business executives. Gender gap in management positions is only 3 points but just 8% firms have women in top management positions.

To support the growing economy of microenterprises and small and medium-sized businesses, in 2019, the Legislative Assembly approved amendments so that 25% of the annual budget for procurement and contracts will be earmarked for goods or services provided by women-owned enterprises. This is a significant step forward to increase access for purchases from businesses whose owner, majority shareholder or legal representative is a woman.

The Women's Entrepreneurship Programme promotes the entrepreneurship and economic empowerment of Salvadoran women through training in business management, financial literacy, and access to credit (even for large enterprises), which are also a more formal type of business. This offers an opportunity to train women so that their business grow. One of the objectives of the National Policy for Enterprise Development is to strengthen competitive and productive capacities of microenterprises.

Companies are located mainly in urban areas (95,13%). In the rural areas, entrepreneurship by women are more than double those led solely by men. Both male and female MSMEs owners used their own resources as a primary source to finance their business. The most common sources were savings, family funds, credit from financial community banks, NGOs, other cooperatives, monyelenders and seed capital granted by COMAPREHE.

Women have lower default rates. However, the lack of assets in their name are the main barriers to accessing formal sources of finance. Access to productive and financial resources scores 22 (14). National data show that less than 30% of enterprises owned by women have access to credit or other financial services. The government has made gender equity as one of its main goals. According to the National Survey of Demand of Financial Services: the gap in favour of men is 5 percentage points.

The use of mobile phones for making payments and transfers is also a significant factor. Women lag behind by 10 percentage points. There is no gender gap in relation to the use of mobile phones to make payments or transfers and the gap in relation to obtaining credit is also small (2%). In 2018 the Salvadoran Institute for the Advancement of Women in coordination with the Central Reserve Bank of El Salvador, conducted an assessment of the invisible barriers to women's access to financial services with a view to prepare guidelines on financial inclusion from a gender perspective.


---
**Page 153**
---

The IDB study on female leadership show that 20% of microfinance institutions have a female general manager and 50% of women in senior management positions.

Measures have been taken in order to ensure women’s right access to land control over land, housing, property, especially rural areas. In this regard, the Ministry of Agriculture has issued guidelines and incorporated a gender perspective into its work plans and provided information on Legislative Decree No. 719, which establishes land as a family asset. The Ministry of Agriculture has also taken steps to include women in its services (agricultural packages, etc.).

Although the rate of rural poverty decreased by 18% between 2000 and 2013, many rural people continue to live in poverty. Traditionally excluded groups such as young people, women, indigenous peoples and seniors are especially vulnerable. Young rural people are trapped between the lack of access to education, which makes it difficult to enter the formal economy, and the need for them to work on family farms or in informal jobs that expose them to violence as victims of crime and, as a result, many choose to migrate. They are reluctant to work in the agriculture sector because of the low income and lack of social security.

The National Policy for Rural, Indigenous and Campesino Women was designed to guarantee human rights and promote the autonomy of rural women. Women and girls are 52% of rural population. 32.1% of households are headed by women (60.7% by men). However, they make up only 9% of the rural population engaged in agricultural activities and almost 30% of those who work as domestic workers. In rural areas, 45% declare themselves to be economically inactive and 50.8% have no income of their own, compared to 17% of men.

The illiteracy rate among women stood at 17.4 per cent in rural areas. In 2019, the rate of participation in the labour force was for women 36%, and 50% in urban areas. The average monthly salary at the national level was $548.40, with women earning $306.11 and men earning $737.40 on average. In rural areas, women earn $213.12 and men earn $279.36 on average.

El Salvador is located in one of the world’s most vulnerable areas as the country has natural hazards in the world; 2.5 billion people live in a poverty-stricken area that is at risk from the earthquakes that make up the so-called “dry corridor”. The effects of climate change and environmental degradation are causing the displacement of thousands of people. The government has developed a series of strategies to address these challenges, including the development of strategies that should be addressed. In the case of adaptation measures, substance farming practices and rural water management are tasks often assigned to women. UN WOMEN highlights strategies such as empowering women through the use of new technologies (such as mobile phones), technology, extension and training services to broaden their capacity and the sustainable use of natural resources.

### 1.2.3 Guatemala

In Guatemala, the Constitution incorporates an article on the right to non-discrimination and the Constitutional Court upheld the exercise of this right in the workplace and the family, informed by a perspective of intersectionality and equality**). The Presidential Secretariat for Women (SEREMI) is the high-level institution which depends directly from the President of the Republic. It is the advisory and coordinating body for public policies to promote the comprehensive development of women.

[UN WOMEN, 2020](#)
[UN WOMEN, 2020](#)
[UN COMICE] Sixth periodic report, December 2021


---
**Page 154**
---

Guatemala, the biggest economy in Central America, is known for its fertile land, abundant natural resources and robust agricultural sector, which employs half of the population and accounts for 10% of the country's GDP. However, the country has one of the highest inequality ratios in Latin America and the lowest rate of women’s participation in the workforce among all countries in Central America. Guatemala is also one of the most violent countries in the world, with some of the worst in the region. Additionally, chronic malnutrition affects almost one half of all children under the age of five, predominantly those living in rural areas (53%). Access to formal financial services and resources, health and education services are still limited for many women and girls. These factors have significant economic constraints to full participation in development.

The National Policy for the Promotion and Comprehensive Development of Women have set three priorities for the period 2017-2022: the eradication of violence against women and girls and women’s economic development. In 2021 the strategy for the implementation of the 2020-2029 National Plan for the Prevention and Eradication of Violence against Women was adopted. In 2017, the Ministry of Labour and Social Security presented a protocol for addressing gender-based violence in the workplace.

Women, Business and Law Index scores 73.8%, with legal constraints found in the areas of workplace (the law does not prohibit discrimination in employment and there is no legislation sexual harassment), pay (law does not mandate equal remuneration between men and women) and entrepreneurship (law does not prohibit discrimination in access to credit). Access to assets are equal.

Regarding entrepreneurship, out of 49 economies, Guatemala ranks 6 in TFA, 5 in EBD and 2 in informal investment. Women in Guatemala own 40.7% of microenterprises and 16.77% of small and medium enterprises*. The proportion of adults starting or running a new business is highest in low LAC countries, being Guatemala one of them and it is one of the countries with the largest gender gap (39 points) in terms of women’s entrepreneurship. Women in Guatemala are more likely to start their own business than men (51% vs 40%) and they are also more likely to be running a new business (62% vs 58%). The proportion of adults starting or running a new business has increased over the pandemic cycle, from 27% in 2019 to 34% in 2021. Women were more than two adults starting a new business for every adult running an established business in 2022, with men slightly more likely than women to be running those New Businesses. Guatemala is an example of a country with low formalization and high female entrepreneurship.

The Women’s Economic Development Act facilitates access to economic and productive resources as well as technological services to implement projects for the economic empowerment of women. As at December 2020, statistical data shows that women received loans in higher amounts than men (157% vs 98%), but with lower interest rates (3.4% vs 6%). Women also had a higher default rate (10%) compared to men (6%). In 2020, financing in lower amounts and with higher interest rates than men, even though women have a lower default rate. 4 out of 15 loans were for women, the interest rate applied was 23.2 (9.4% in men), while the default rate was 1.8 in the case of women compared to 0.6%. Women also had a higher default rate than men (17%). In 2020, 5 out of 15 loans were for women, the interest rate applied was 34% and the default rate was 19%, while only one loan was granted to men. Guatemala scores 28.4 in access to productive and financial resources. In spite of equal ownership rights to immovable property the gender gap in share of ownership reaches 36 points[**].

According to the OECD study on female leadership, 38% of microfinance institutions have a female general manager and 33% of women in senior management positions.

## Honduras

Honduras is a middle-income country of Central America that is facing persistent poverty, insecurity and socio-economic inequality. Poverty rate reached 66% in 2016. The country has one of the lowest Human Development Indexes of the region, quality of life is low and there are high levels of social exclusion.

Since 2015, Honduras has adopted several laws to protect women's rights such as the Wage Equality Act, the Credit for Rural Women Act, regulations for the implementation of the principle of parity on electoral lists, regulations to monitor investigations of violent deaths of women and cases of femicide, among others. Likewise, Honduras has had adopted policies aimed at promoting gender equality in the public sector.

The National Institute for Women is implementing the Second National Gender Equality and Equity Plan 2020-2022 through the Gender Units and the municipal offices for women. The Plan was developed with wide participation of actors from civil society, national and municipal governments and has had the support of international agencies such as AICD, UNFPA and UNDP.

The National Service of Entrepreneurship and Small Businesses gives credits and support to women to strengthen their entrepreneurial skills and access to finance, as well as the development of women-led SMEs (48%).

According to UN WOMEN, the limited access to land and credit severely restrict women's economic prospects and autonomy. Some women wishing to form their own businesses often cannot do so due to their need of capital or financial resources. In 2015, the European Commission launched the Financial Inclusion Plan for Women, aimed at ensuring that data disaggregated by sex is compiled and providing access to aid use of financial services and products by women.

According to ILO data on the agricultural sector, 34% of microinstitutions have a female general manager and 29% of women in senior management positions.

In 2015, 63% of population lived in rural areas and 65% of rural households lived in poverty. Agricultural land accounts for about 28% of the country's land area, and the agricultural sector employs almost 39% of the population. Most of


---
**Page 158**
---

The agricultural land is used for the production of low-yielding crops such as bananas, plantains, rice, maize and beans. In addition, the agricultural sector has lost almost a third of its income over the past 20 years, partly due to the decline in prices for the country's export crops, especially bananas and coffee. **17**

Rural women are particularly vulnerable because of their limited access to education, migration, and poverty. 50% of rural population is composed of women but only 27% of this population is economically active. Women are involved in much lesser extent in agricultural activities with limited access to land or financing. There is an invisibility phenomenon of the contribution of rural women to the economy. The majority of rural women are engaged in informal economic activities, such as agriculture activities (mostly related to subsistence production and taking care of nutrition with their households) with other the economic activities. In spite of having achieved greater educational results, opportunities have not increased for rural women to access productive resources or services that would allow them to participate more effectively in reaching their economic autonomy. Poverty in rural areas has affected the women-headed households in a disproportional manner.

The successive waves of development programmes and social events such as droughts increase women’s vulnerability. Decreases in the quantity and distribution of rainfall lead not only to failed crops, but also to fewer employment and income opportunities, price increases, and changes in the consumption patterns of the most vulnerable population. Therefore, the most affected are rural women who have less access to land and other productive resources than men, as well as labourers and women heads of households**18**. With regard to the active inclusion of women in the design and implementation of climate change policies, the School for Equity and Empowerment of Rural Women offers advice and support to national and international initiatives designed to reduce poverty, and promote equity and equity in the use, conservation and preservation of the environment and natural resources.

### 1.2.6 Mexico

Mexico is the second largest economy in Latin America. Despite being a large, upper-middle-income country, Mexico continues to have high rural poverty levels and wide social and economic disparities.

The country has made significant progress in the achievement of women’s rights and gender equality, especially in key areas at federal level: strengthening of national laws to ensure women and men equality; strong gender institutionalization and increased public resources earmarked for gender equality. The most prominent legislation at the federal level to promote gender equality is the National Constitution**19**, the Federal Law for the Prevention and Sanitizing


---
**Page 159**
---

# Discrimination, the General Law of Equality between Women and Men(59) and the General Law Women Access to a Life Free from Violence(60)

Regarding institutions, the National Institute of Women(71) is the federal office that works for gender equality in Mexico, contributing to the implementation of the National Plan for Gender Equity (2013-2024)(72). The National Plan for Gender Equity (2013-2024)(72) is focused on advancing substantive equality around 6 strategic goals (economic autonomy, domestic work, health rights, violence, decision-making and safe environments). Important milestones recently achieved were the Political-Electoral Reform that ensures equal representation of women in political parties and the Law for the Elimination of Violence against Women.

Despite the progress made, gender equality in Mexico faces a series of structural challenges characterized by an unrelenting gap between formal and substantive equality in all areas of development. Besides, a cause for concern is the extension of generalized violence in several target area of the country, showing patterns of human rights violations and impunity. These include femicide, sexual violence, torture, arbitrary detention, as well as attacks on human rights activists and journalist men and women alike.

Women, Business and Law Index scores 88.8, with legal constraints found in the areas of pay (law does not mandate equal remuneration for work of equal value), parenthood, and pension. Access to assets are equal(73).

Mexico is one of the most populous countries in LAC, with more than six million microfinance borrowers, the largest number in the region with nearly $50 billion but has the smallest loan portfolio which implies a small average loan size provided by MFIs. The majority of these loans are granted to women (62%) and are used for business purposes by entrepreneurs and non-entrepreneurs[8]. Group lending is a good entry point for the underbanked into the formal financial sector but it does not serve well microenterprises as they grow. Since 30% of Mexico microfinance borrowers are women, there is an opportunity to increase their access which will affect women's access to finance. There seems to be a big potential to grow the financial services sector in Mexico.

In 2018, the average interest rates varied according to the amounts of the loans and the size of the borrowing company. For large companies, the average interest rate was approximately 11.78%, SMEs, the average was 13.70%. In recent


---
**Page 161**
---

years, the Mexican government has developed a series of initiatives to support entrepreneurs and strengthen the access of SMEs to finance. These initiatives have included programs for young people and women.

In a recent study by Value for Women on women-led small and growing businesses[1] found that women-led SMEs are private sector leaders in Mexico. The study found that 42% of women-led SMEs had revenues over $50,000 MXN ($3,789 USD) and profits over $4 million MXN ($222,000 USD), which is twice of that of women-led. The study also found that in Mexico 70% of women were looking for finance. Yet, according to this report, many Mexican women-led businesses (42%) have never applied for a loan and only 15% of them have received one. Women-led SMEs are more likely than men-led to sell products. 93% of the sample said they believe that there is a lack of financing opportunities available to and accessible for women-led businesses specifically, while very few (4%) reported knowing about opportunities for financing that exist but are not being used.

Socioeconomic disparities are more marked in rural areas, where 61% of the indigenous population is extremely poor, compared with only 19% of the non-indigenous rural population. Although there is no gender gap in the poverty rate (48%) or unemployment rates (25%), women experience a higher level of deprivation in terms of food, healthcare and education. Most poor women live in rural areas.

Mexico's agriculture sector is facing increasing vulnerabilities and a degraded natural resource base. Rural people are constrained by limited human and social assets, inadequate production-related investments and value-adding infrastructure, lack of access to markets, and the absence of policies that promote gender equality and equal access to markets. These constraints result in low productivity, depressed consumption and incomes, and poverty.

A key factor in the sector's performance is a lack of smallholder access to financing. Although Mexico has various national agricultural credit programs, they are not designed for women farmers or smallholders. Women have limited access to land and inputs and restrictions on decision-making at home. The insertion of women in productive activities requires considering and knowing the role that perform in society, especially taking into account the increase in their remuneration.

Low-income households are increasingly dependent on women's work, increasing their income requires access to microcredits, productive inputs, training and access to better markets. The incursion of women into productive activities has repercussions for family life and social organization, which can have a positive impact on the family that promotes the education and well-being of children, resulting in an increase in the human capital of future generations.

Crops that are key to the smallholder economy, such as coffee and seasonal white corn, are frequently exposed to shocks such as climate-related events or market imperfections. The country also faces vulnerabilities to climate-related disasters and extreme weather conditions.


### 1.2.2.7 Panama

In Panama, the basis for gender equality is the Political Constitution of the Republic, whose article 19 establishes that "There will be no privileges or discrimination based on race, birth, social class, sex, religion or political ideas". According to the World Bank, Panama has a legal framework that promotes, enforces and monitors gender equity, with a focus on violence against women, are in place.

## Women in Panama

Women own 12.4% of microenterprises and 11.04% of small and medium enterprises[1]. Out of 54 economies, Panama ranks 3rd in TIA, 8th in EGD and 69th in informal investment.

The proportion of adults starting or running a new business is highest in five LAC countries: being Panama (together with Guatemala) one of them. Panama is also the only country where more than half of women report that they are planning to start their own business within two years, and 30% expect to employ an additional six or more people in five years time.

There is a significant gender gap in entrepreneurship: women are less likely to be self-employed (15%) compared with men (24%). Women entrepreneurs also face greater barriers than men when it comes to accessing finance. Women-owned micro and small businesses represent 96% of all companies in Panama, generating 45% of total employment according to AMPINE. Also, around 80% of all MSMEs and smallholder farmers are concentrated in Panama capital and more than half of them have less than five employees.

Among the main obstacles that women entrepreneurs face can be mentioned the access to credit. It is necessary to finance the seed capital and make more flexible credit conditions when it comes to loans for women entrepreneurs. In addition to this, there is a limited supply of microfinance services that does not supply the great demand for it[2].

Other problems are related with the lack of information about business registration procedures in an easy and neutral way. Women tend to feel less prepared, and businesses report not having sufficient information on the procedures for business registration and compliance with other rules. Besides, traditional gender roles continue to dispossess women from their entrepreneurial responsibilities to women[3].

According to the IDB study on female leadership: 51% of new enterprises in production industries have a female general manager and 55% of women in senior management positions.

More than 50% of the population lives in urban areas which indicates that rural development has significantly been neglected. In 2017, Panama had an estimated population of 3,600,000 women & 4,800,000 men[4]. Women mostly contribute to agriculture production and the design of strategies to confront climate changes but are not considered key actors in the elaboration of local, national or international strategies. According to (INAM), climate change impacts such as lack of access to water or degradation of the land, unequally affect women’s rights as well the women's health.

1.3 SME and smallholder farmers overview

The Latin America & Caribbean continent represents ca. 8.4% of the world’s total population[20] yet is the third largest region in terms of number of MSMEs. The continent is home to 27.5 million MSMEs, of which 26.2 million are microenterprises and 1.3 million are SMEs[21], in fact, out of 50 countries surveyed by the “Global Entrepreneurship Monitor”, 48% of all MSMEs were located in Latin America & Caribbean region. Of these, 79% of microenterprises were in Latin America, four of which form part of this multi-country proposal(Brazil, Panama, Guatemala and Colombia). The region showed an average TEA of over 24%, compared to a 12% in Africa and Asia & Oceania and ca. 10% in Europe & Central Asia. In terms of the number of women entrepreneurs, Latin America & Caribbean region is second only to EAC countries, which represent 95.9% of all companies, create 60% of the total employment and generate around a 23% of the regional GDP[22].

Women are more likely than men to be engaged in entrepreneurial activity, which can be seen on the high female-led TEA ratio for Latin America: 0.81 in comparison with 0.72 for the countries surveyed in Africa, 0.69 for those in Asia & Oceania and 0.57 in Europe & North America. Despite this, Latin American women entrepreneurs are often driven more by necessity than choice, as they are more likely to be running a family business or working part-time[23]. This finding suggests, out of 50 countries surveyed, in 36 countries more women than men cited that their main motivation for working as an entrepreneur was “to earn a living because jobs are scarce”. Furthermore, in each of the 4 countries surveyed by this multi-country proposal, women were more likely to cite necessity as their main reason for entrepreneurship. For this reason as their main motivation for working as an entrepreneur, it is % of those agreeing with this statement in these four countries that range from higher than and $1.26 million (in the case of Colombia) to less than $0.5 million (in the case of Panama). In this region, support for women entrepreneurs in their activities is often limited by lack of access to finance, poor infrastructure, and the fact that limited other options are available.

In 2019, women represented % of Latin America’s total population, yet only 43% of the region’s total employed population were female. The labour force participation rate of men (77%) in 2015, according to ILO data as of June 20 is significantly higher than that of women (52%), the latter of which has only increased slightly since 2010 (53%). Despite the region’s high rate of women participating in entrepreneurship, they are still subject to a number of geographic and socio-economic constraints. These include access to finance, human and social capital – key constraints for the growth of their businesses[24]. Over% of women in the Latin America & Caribbean region operate in the informal sector, unable to grow out of the informal economy due to a lack of women role models in business, social/cultural constraints, and general demographic responsibilities.

Financial inclusion

Latin America is the region with the second highest level of failure in women led businesses in the world, with women entrepreneurs facing financing constraints as the main reason for business dissolution (as opposed to poor profitability, in the case of men). Whilst access to microfinance for women is easier, women are often not able to afford more “sophisticated” products that provide the appropriate terms and conditions that will enable their microenterprises to grow. This is a key constraint on the growth of women’s businesses[25]. Women entrepreneurs also face key constraints in ensuring the sustainability of MSMEs and smallholder farmers and boosting female livelihoods, whereby for Latin

# America: A $5 billion gap for women-led microbusinesses

Women have been shown to be responsible and loyal financial services clients, while BCG analysis finds that as much as USD 2.5 trillion in annual revenue is lost by financial institutions when they exclude women participants from their services.

Women clients can contribute to reduced client attrition and portfolio risk, and to increased fee income, thanks to women's responsible behaviour towards loan repayments but also to their interest in savings and other complementary products. Women are also more likely than men to save for the future, and women tend to be more likely to save than men, whilst women also show higher savings toward indebtedness than men.

Several barriers limit in general the financial inclusion of women. On the supply side, there are physical barriers (related to time and travel costs) to a financial institution, insecurity and restrictions to mobility and social interactions outside the home, lack of access to information and communication technology, as well as lack of awareness about products (as well as less labour opportunities) and eligibility barriers related to greater difficulty in obtaining an official identification document. On the demand side, women face constraints such as a lack of financial literacy (which is often unconscious, about women's roles) or even the need of permission or signature of husbands. On the demand side, women often have less self-esteem in their skills and capabilities, less levels of financial literacy and can even have less confidence to use financial services.

On top of these constraints, it is also very relevant to take into account social norms and their influence on the different barriers identified. Gender norms are a part of social norms and are defined as "collectively held perceptions and expectations about what men and women should be like or do". These gender norms can limit women's access to education, work, domestic and agricultural activities as well as patriarchal norms about the role of women limit their autonomy in social and economic fields, especially in rural areas and for indigenous women. The Gender Social Norm Index (GSNI) show that in 2018, 54% of countries had a GSNI score above 60%, which means that women are more likely to experience violence and women's reproductive rights worldwide is 38% of women and 10% of men. For data targets countries are: 10.50% in Brazil, 8.00% in Colombia and 12.30% in Mexico.

The project will implement measures to take into account these barriers and will set targets to reach women-owned SMEs and microenterprises that have been excluded or have previously abandoned. Completion of an disaggregated data, training on gender equality and women empowerment (GEWE), gender and SEAP action and policy plans implemented at every level of the financial institution, as well as a focus on business development services such as business management, innovation for business expansion, strategic decision making, financial management, cash flows, savings and investment credit, will help to challenge constraints in the women's participation. As a minimum, 5% of total attendance in training sessions should be women.

The project will also identify opportunities and challenges of targeting women as clients such as the design of financial products that target women and vulnerable populations. Outcomes of this activity will depend on FSP and country context since every FSP will design the financial products that best adapt to women's realities and needs.

[4] P. R. et al., "Gender Equality in Financial Inclusion: A Review of the Literature", Journal of Financial Services Plots/LTS/2015/384, LUNNRTS/2017/212, Ciudad de México, Comisión Económica para América Latina y el Caribe (CEPAL), 2016.

[5] World Bank. "Gender and Financial Inclusion: A Review of the Literature". Washington D.C., USA.

[6] United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). 2020. Gender Social Norms Index (GSNI): Tackling social norms: A game changer for gender inequalities. New York.


## References


---
**Page 166**
---

Participants in the financial system need to be aware of and understand how gender norms limit women’s access and use of financial services even in areas where the law has been changed but endure as a social norm[68]. Barriers and social norms will be taken into account in the trainings implemented as part of the GAP aimed at supporting other activities listed above.

Consultants hired will make emphasis on specific barriers of each country and partnerships with women’s organisations, will also help to ensure a more enhanced knowledge about women's situations and influence other stakeholders. Several activities in the GAP (e.g., 2.3, 2.6, 3.1, 3.2) are aimed at enabling women get the funding they need.

### Agriculture and rural areas

Regarding agriculture, women play a key role in food production, nutrition, and natural resources management. According to FAO, women provide around 20% of all agricultural employment. However, there are some specific issues that make women entrepreneurs more prone to inequality compared with their male counterparts, such as: land rights, productivity, access to credit, and technology.

In the case of rural Central America, inequality is deepened by the structural consequences of urban-centric bias in the distribution of strategic investments for national development. According to a study by ECLAC on women in the agricultural sector in Latin America and the Caribbean, 70% of the female population works in agriculture, while only 35% of the male population in Central America. A high proportion of rural women employed in agriculture are unpaid family workers which puts them at a disadvantage, as they do not receive financial reward for their activities. Increasing the income of these women would have a significant impact on the household economy and the well-being of children who are the household head. This would translate into improvements in education and nutrition of children, and in higher economic growth of the country.

Women are often subject to statutory land laws, and when they do own land, their plots are often relatively small due to many customary land rights systems. The need of collateral and asset-based lending implies that women have to own and control assets to unlock finance. Collateral requirements are generally much stricter for women, and customary land rights can be a barrier in the process of obtaining credit. Women also lack access to key assets and employment directly impacts access to credit, making it more difficult to acquire production inputs and technologies that would enable them to increase their productivity. This is also a missed opportunity since developing good practices in agriculture could help women to become entrepreneurs. In addition, financial institutions could capture an underserved market and boost the entrepreneurial potential of an entire generation.

Moreover, they often are exposed to provide unpaid farm work, and bear a disproportionate burden of care and reproductive responsibilities that can limit their ability to participate fully in agricultural activities.

As said above, the project will implement measures to take into account women’s barriers in providing guarantees and will set targets to reach women farmers. Awareness about social norms will also be part of the activities implemented so those root causes of gender inequality are better known for all participants.


### Gender and climate change

Women are also more vulnerable to the effects of climate change, as they spend more time and energy on activities related to their environment. The United Nations Development Program (UNDP) and the United Nations Environment Program (UN AWARENESS) have identified 5 major areas that link the environment and poverty, including women and


---
**Page 167**
---

girls' direct dependence on natural resources, vulnerability to climatic shocks such as floods and landslides, health effects from the indiscriminate use of agrochemicals, substances that deplete the ozone layer or poor waste disposal.

These effects can increase gender inequality, reduces women's ability to be financially independent, and has an overall negative impact on the social and economic well-being of women, especially in economies that are heavily based on agriculture™. As a result, boys and girls find themselves with less time for education, health care, income generating activities, and participation in community decision making processes, further entrenching unequal gender relations.

Global studies show that 70% of consumer decisions are made by women. However, in decision-making in general, women do not have a say. Women's access to resources is limited due to their lack of education, poor health status, and biodiversity. Women must participate in decision making processes regarding access, use and control of natural resources, as well as the equitable distribution of the resulting benefits.

Public interventions such as microfinance programs, training for women, and other concrete actions, among others, must incorporate gender equity, including access to training, education, access to tools or mechanisms for entrepreneurship.

Currently, Financial Service Providers (FSPs) included in the multi-country project proposal reach 40% women in general through products and services. Typical products provided are MFI loans, village banking, and in some cases, specific agricultural credit. The FSPs have also been involved in other activities such as training for women on business building, specifically during COVID-19 pandemic, and other education related to financial literacy and agricultural best practices.

Women-owned MSMEs and women farmers reach by FSP is much lower, thus the Small Fund (SF) aims to contribute increasing their reach by FSPs and improving products and services offered. By achieving these improvements, it is expected that women end-clients will be empowered.

The project will include an activity aimed at increasing awareness about the potential to increase income, reduce CO2 emissions and improve adaptation through green strategies financed by FSPs.

# Implementation of the project: Replication of successful initiatives implemented in a FSP or innovative company (IC)

A gender gap assessment will be conducted in each institution which together with the existing information and the insights from the pilot phase will be the basis for developing specific gender policy and procedures. The Gender Officer will report to the Investment Committee chairperson that investment is to be approved and provide gender information to decision-making committees. Specific covenants regarding gender equality will be introduced in loan agreements, including a requirement of 50% women on boards of directors. Kuali Fund-GIC and FSPs & IC will provide data on an annual basis as part of an E3 performance report®. Finally, Kuali Fund-GIC will prepare an E3 monitoring report for CDFIES with data and information on the implementation of the GAP.

## Human resources in GVAH for gender activities

A Gender Officer will be appointed and an E&S Officer with expertise in gender will be hired and provide support to the Gender Officer. The Gender Officer will be in charge of integrating the gender approach across the full investment cycle (analysis, legal agreements and monitoring). Gender Officer will also support Kuali Fund-GIC’s staff on gender issues at all times. The Gender Officer will be responsible for ensuring that gender is integrated into all aspects of the work provided to the whole team to enhance competencies and knowledge in the area of gender, SEAF and social inclusion. Moreover, external consultants can be hired to provide support to Kuali Fund Manager and Gender Officer on the elaboration of gender action plans and the implementation of gender policies. The Gender Officer will also be involved in the design of financial products adapted to women’s needs. Priority will be given to local expert consultants.

Within FSPs, there will be the possibility of naming a focal person in gender issues, in order to execute the GAPs more effectively.

## Budget for gender activities

Specific activities included in GAP will be financed with Technical Assistance Facility (TA) budget. CDFIES will monitor the implementation of GAP activities and targets set on an annual basis. The following table show TA disaggregated budget for GAP activities:

| TA Budget Category | Social Performance and Environmental Risks Management Systems (SPM and ERS) | Ecosystemic Projects, Stakeholder engagement and gender actions |
|---|---|---|
| Output 1 | $679,622.00 | - |
| Output 2 | 213,813.71 | - |
| Output 3 | - | 532,714.08 |
| Total | 213,813.71 | 220,686.29 |

# GENDER ACTION PLAN (GAP)

## Activities

### 1.1 Developing a Gender and State ESMO Policy and amend existing ESMOs to incorporate gender aspects and implementation arrangements

#### Indicators and Targets
- Gender and SEAM Policy and new ESMOs adopted and shared with relevant stakeholders.
- The policy management committee will follow the project life cycle and will comprise of representatives from all levels.

#### Baseline
No Gender and SEAM Policy

#### Timeline
Within 3 months of IFP approval.

#### Responsibilities
Kuafi Fund GCF Manager, E&S Officer and Gender Officer

#### Costs
No cost

### 1.2 Appointment of Kuafi Fund GCF’s Gender Officer and an E&S officer with periodic training for Gender and E&S Officers

#### Indicators and Targets
- A Gender Officer appointed among current Kuafi Fund GCF's staff and receiving periodic training.
- An E&O with expertise in gender will be recruited.

#### Baseline
No Gender Officer

#### Timeline
3 months of Kuafi Fund GCF’s approval

#### Responsibilities
Gender Officer will have to report to the Management Committee (MC) and will monitor the members’ SEAM indicators as well as any other SEAM indicators.

#### Costs
Up to EUR 1,000 of GCFs contribution budget for periodic training and specialization courses for Recruitment of E&S officer

### Outcome Statement:
Increased access to gender and socially inclusive financial products for different social groups, in particular women-led SMEs, farms and households, to switch to low-carbon technologies and to implement adaptation strategies to the climate change effects.

Output 1: Kuafi Fund GCF’s Governance. Policies and procedures enhanced to include comprehensive gender approach. Competencies and capacities development enhanced at Kuafi Fund GCF team. Gender Officer position created in gender fund. Country gender assessments extended.

Activities

### Climate awareness activities and training on Climate Smart Agriculture and adoption of Climate-Smart practices (e.g., irrigation generation, buildings, cities, infrastructure development, forestry and land use, etc) being financed by FSP.

#### 4.1
- Follow-up of adaptation products implemented and follow-up of the conditions for women after the training.
- Baseline: Number of attendees to the training - 12,117 of which 30% women.
- New activity: Potential attendees with no adaptation product and not measuring their greenhouse gas emissions.

### Support for women's economic empowerment via FSP through housing loans training and financial literacy.

#### 4.2
